Kuczynski | Two objects have the same length if they are length-congruent. | Measurement and Conventionalism
Kuczynski | Two objects are length-congruent if (but, given that there are many ways to establish length-congruence, not only if) two conditions are met, namely: (i) When juxtaposed, each endpoint of the one is contiguous one of the endpoints of the other, and (ii) The distance between those two objects is constant---it doesn t wax and wane. | Measurement and Conventionalism
Kuczynski | L is longer than L* if L* is congruent with a proper segment of L. | Measurement and Conventionalism
Kuczynski | Absent a definition of congruence, L is longer than L* and L and L* are length-identical are devoid of meaning. | Measurement and Conventionalism
Kuczynski | Absent a definition of length-congruence, no object L can be a length-standard. | Measurement and Conventionalism
Kuczynski | For L to be a length-standard, it must be said what it is for L* to be length-identical with L. | Measurement and Conventionalism
Kuczynski | Given some L-congruent object L*, L* is capable of serving as a length-standard. | Measurement and Conventionalism
Kuczynski | It may turn out that, if L* is assumed to be length-constant, conditions (i)-(iii) are satisfied, but that aleph same conditions are violated if L is assumed to be length-constant. | Measurement and Conventionalism
Kuczynski | Length-congruence holds between one-dimensional objects; triangle-congruence holds between two-dimensional objects; pyramid-congruence holds between three-dimensional objects. | Measurement and Conventionalism
Kuczynski | For any integer n, there are (hypothetical, if not actual) n-dimensional objects and, consequently, n-dimensional congruence. | Measurement and Conventionalism
Kuczynski | A given object is provisionally taken as a length-standard. A congruence-relation is defined. On that basis, other objects are measured. | Measurement and Conventionalism
Kuczynski | Those measurements yield knowledge of physical laws. | Measurement and Conventionalism
Kuczynski | Small distances can be measured using meter rods, and they can also be measured using light rays. | Measurement and Conventionalism
Kuczynski | Large distances can be measured using light rays, but they cannot be measured using meter-rods. | Measurement and Conventionalism
Kuczynski | Within narrow horizons, meter-rod measurements and optical measurements yield the same results. This is a contingent fact. | Measurement and Conventionalism
Kuczynski | Suppose it turned out---as it well might have---that the numbers yielded by meter-rod measurements could not be coordinated with the numbers yielded by optical measurements. | Measurement and Conventionalism
Kuczynski | In that case, there would be no one magnitude that was measured in both ways, and statements to the effect that the Sun is n meters from the Earth would be meaningless. | Measurement and Conventionalism
Kuczynski | One can use a thermometer to measure the temperature of bath water. One cannot use a thermometer to measure the temperature of magma, let alone the temperature of a star. | Measurement and Conventionalism
Kuczynski | On the basis of thermometer-based measurements, one acquires knowledge of low-level physical laws (e.g. that such and such metals have such and such coefficients of expansion). | Measurement and Conventionalism
Kuczynski | On the basis of this knowledge, one acquires knowledge of higher-level physical laws (e.g. that radiation-intensity varies inversely with the square of the distance from the heat-source). | Measurement and Conventionalism
Kuczynski | Day-to-day experience gives us approximate metrical knowledge, on the basis of which we can identify correlations on the basis of which we can obtain hitherto unobtainable metrical data. | Measurement and Conventionalism
Kuczynski | On the basis of this metrical data, we can identify precise laws, on the basis of which we can both add to and refine such metrical knowledge as we already have. | Measurement and Conventionalism
Kuczynski | Two meter-rods can be laid side by side. Non-simultaneous events cannot be laid side by side. | Measurement and Conventionalism
Kuczynski | If it is to be known how two such events compare in respect of duration, one must know of some periodic process that occurred during each event and, further, one must know how many installments of that processes occurred during each. | Measurement and Conventionalism
Kuczynski | If m installments of that process occur during the one event and n installments occur during the other, then, if that process is indeed a perfectly regular one, the length of the one relative to the other is n/m. | Measurement and Conventionalism
Kuczynski | But it cannot be said, it will be objected, that a given periodic process is regular unless there is already a way of determining duration. | Measurement and Conventionalism
Kuczynski | And there is no way of determining duration except in relation to some periodic process. | Measurement and Conventionalism
Kuczynski | Therefore, some periodic process must be stipulated to be perfectly regularity: it must be perfectly regular by convention. | Measurement and Conventionalism
Kuczynski | This position, duly generalized, is known as conventionalism. Conventionalism is false. | Measurement and Conventionalism
Kuczynski | If duration is measured in terms of a process that is intuitively known to be erratic (e.g. somebody s heartbeat), the resulting measurements aren t consistent with one another. | Measurement and Conventionalism
Kuczynski | Duration-measurements are consistent with one another if duration is measured in terms of a process that is intuitively known to be regular (e.g. the series of roundtrips made by a light beam between two mirrors whose distance from each other is fixed). | Measurement and Conventionalism
Kuczynski | Given that, relative to a given framework, an event cannot have distinct durations, it follows that conventionalism is false. | Measurement and Conventionalism
Kuczynski | An extensive magnitude is one such that the degree to which a given object has that magnitude exceeds the degree to any given one of its parts has that magnitude. | Measurement and Conventionalism
Kuczynski | An additive magnitude is one such that the degree to which a given object has that magnitude is the arithmetical sum of the extent to which each of its parts has that magnitude. | Measurement and Conventionalism
Kuczynski | Length and mass are extensive, and within limits they are both additive. | Measurement and Conventionalism
Kuczynski | Speed is extensive and, within limits, additive. Intelligence is intensive. So are temperature and density. | Measurement and Conventionalism
Kuczynski | Given any framework F, the speed of light relative to F is c (?186,000 miles/second). | Measurement and Conventionalism
Kuczynski | A consequence---actually, the very essence of Special Relativity---is that there is no optical test by means of which one determine whether or not one is moving. | Measurement and Conventionalism
Kuczynski | If speed were additive, these statements would be counterlogical. But speed, though extensive, is not additive. | Measurement and Conventionalism
Kuczynski | If you are moving at a rate of 30 mph on a train that is moving in the same direction at a rate of 30 mph, your speed relative to the train station is less than 60 mph. | Measurement and Conventionalism
Kuczynski | A corollary is that length is non-additive. A corollary of this corollary is that volume is not additive. | Measurement and Conventionalism
Kuczynski | If a given object s length is given by an irrational number, that object s length cannot be established by measuring that object. | Measurement and Conventionalism
Kuczynski | Nonetheless, it can be known that a given object s length is given by such a number. | Measurement and Conventionalism
Kuczynski | According to many (e.g. Russell, Poincar , Einstein), this entails that, in taking LS to be length-constant, we are simply adopting a convention, not registering a fact. | Measurement and Conventionalism
Kuczynski | Since it is entirely a matter of convention whether a given object is such a standard, the laws of nature are determined by our conventions. | Measurement and Conventionalism
Kuczynski | Thus, conventionalism is ambiguous. Sometimes it refers to metrical conventionalism, and sometimes it refers to nomic conventionalism. | Measurement and Conventionalism
Kuczynski | Supposing arguendo that conventionalism is correct, it doesn t follow that the laws of nature are entirely a matter of convention. | Measurement and Conventionalism
Kuczynski | Let strict conventionalism be the position that the laws of nature are entirely a matter of convention. Strict conventionalism isn t a form of conventionalism. | Measurement and Conventionalism
Kuczynski | If the laws of nature were convention-determined, as opposed to merely convention-based, there would be no need to take measurements to identify those laws. | Measurement and Conventionalism
Kuczynski | A system of measurement must yield consistent results: if, relative to a given metrical standard, a given object (at a given time, relative to a given framework) both has, and does not have, a given length n, then that standard cannot coherently be used. | Measurement and Conventionalism
Kuczynski | It is an empirical fact that, when objects that we intuitively regard as length-variable (e.g. eels, cylinder-shaped balloons) that are used as length-standards, the resulting measurements are inconsistent with one another. | Measurement and Conventionalism
Kuczynski | It is also an empirical fact that, when objects that we intuitively regard as length-constant are used as length-standards (e.g. platinum rods in temperature controlled rooms), the resulting measurements are consistent with each other to a high degree of approximation. | Measurement and Conventionalism
Kuczynski | Given that n=n, for any n, and given that use of an eel standard constitutes a rejection of this truism, whereas use of an optical standard constitutes acceptance of it, it must be said that, if eel-based measurements yield one set of physical laws and optics-based measurement yields a different set of laws, the latter are the laws that govern nature and the former are not. | Measurement and Conventionalism

KUCZYNSKI | Reference is a property of expression-tokens, not expression-types. No expression-type refers to anything. Only tokens refer. | Foundational Semantic Positions
KUCZYNSKI | Meaning (sense) is a property of expression-types (platonic entities); reference is a property of expression-tokens (spatio-temporal entities). No entity has both meaning and reference in the same sense. | Foundational Semantic Positions
KUCZYNSKI | The meaning of an indexical sentence-type (e.g., 'I am tired') is a function from contexts of utterance to propositions. The meaning of such a token is a proposition. | Foundational Semantic Positions
KUCZYNSKI | All or nearly all sentences of natural language are indexical because they contain tense-markers. Therefore, virtually all sentence-types have functions (not propositions) for their meanings. | Foundational Semantic Positions
KUCZYNSKI | The distinction between 'sentences₁' (expressions with functions from contexts to propositions as meanings) and 'sentences₂' (expressions with propositions as meanings) would introduce a massive, implausible cleavage into a unified class. It is better to treat all sentences uniformly as having two-dimensional structure. | Foundational Semantic Positions
KUCZYNSKI | Sentence-types do not refer to anything. They no more refer than the type 'you' refers. | Foundational Semantic Positions
KUCZYNSKI | Sentence-tokens do not refer to truth-values. One can understand a sentence-token without knowing its truth-value, but one cannot understand a referring term without knowing what it refers to (at the token level). | Foundational Semantic Positions
KUCZYNSKI | Sentence-tokens refer to their meanings. Very roughly, the meaning of a sentence-token is a proposition; more precisely, it is a certain kind of function involving a proposition. | Foundational Semantic Positions
KUCZYNSKI | Forceless sentence-tokens (those embedded in larger sentences without independent assertoric, interrogative, or imperative force) refer to propositions. For example, in 'Necessarily, Mozart wrote music,' the embedded 'Mozart wrote music' denotes the proposition that Mozart wrote music. | Foundational Semantic Positions
KUCZYNSKI | Forced sentence-tokens (those with assertoric, interrogative, or imperative force) refer to functions involving propositions, not to bare propositions. The semantic content of a forced sentence-token is a function that assigns the property of success to that token exactly if the relevant proposition is true. | Foundational Semantic Positions
KUCZYNSKI | The semantic content of an indicative sentence-token must be something that an expression cannot have without having assertoric force. This requires a certain kind of innocuous circularity in the analysis. | Foundational Semantic Positions
KUCZYNSKI | Every indicative sentence-token (e.g., 'Plato snores') is semantically equivalent to 'A(that Plato snores)' where 'A' is an assertoric force-operator that assigns | Foundational Semantic Positions
KUCZYNSKI | Force is never generated by manipulating propositional content. Replacing one proposition-denoting expression with another cannot create or change force. | The Slingshot and Its Refutation
KUCZYNSKI | Force-operators are not merely conventionalized expressions of propositional attitudes. Saying "I believe that Plato snores" expresses belief but is semantically entirely different from asserting "Plato snores." | The Slingshot and Its Refutation
KUCZYNSKI | The assertoric force-operator denotes a function that assigns the property of success to a specific token exactly if a certain proposition is true. This self-referential structure is essential and non-viciously circular. | The Slingshot and Its Refutation
KUCZYNSKI | Let t be an arbitrary sentence-token. t encodes some proposition P, but that is not all t encodes. The semantic content of t as a whole is a function F that assigns the property of success to t exactly if P is true. | The Slingshot and Its Refutation
KUCZYNSKI | The semantic content of the corresponding type T is a function that assigns F to t. | The Slingshot and Its Refutation
KUCZYNSKI | This circularity is not vicious. The sentence-token t is a mere physical entity identifiable without mentioning any function. | The Slingshot and Its Refutation
KUCZYNSKI | For any proposition P, there exists a function F that assigns success to t exactly if P is true. We are simply identifying the semantic content of t with one of those functions. | The Slingshot and Its Refutation
KUCZYNSKI | If the semantic content of t does not mention t, then it would be possible for an expression to have that very content without having assertoric force. Self-reference is required for adequate semantics of force. | The Slingshot and Its Refutation
KUCZYNSKI | The Slingshot argument "proves" absurdities (e.g., that your car is identical with my computer). Therefore, it contains errors that must be exposed. | The Slingshot and Its Refutation
KUCZYNSKI | The Slingshot assumes (SS): Replacing a referring term with a co-referring term can result in a change of meaning. This principle is false. If "the author of Waverly" and "Scott" co-refer, then replacing one with the other should not change meaning. | The Slingshot and Its Refutation
KUCZYNSKI | Intersubstituting co-referring terms preserves reference. But if (SS) is true, then (CR) is false, since replacing co-referring terms can change meaning in non-extensional contexts. | Unified Semantics for Noun-Phrases
KUCZYNSKI | If E is an expression that rigidly designates a proposition or concept, and e is a referring term occurring as a proper part of E, then replacing e with a co-referring term may result in an expression with a different referent. | Unified Semantics for Noun-Phrases
KUCZYNSKI | The Slingshot assumes that sentences do not rigidly denote propositions, but this cannot be assumed in the relevant argumentative context. | Unified Semantics for Noun-Phrases
KUCZYNSKI | Many Slingshots make spurious use of (LL): logically equivalent sentences co-refer. But "1+1=2" and "triangles have three sides" don't say the same thing—one is about triangles, the other is not. | Unified Semantics for Noun-Phrases
KUCZYNSKI | The kind of "logical equivalence" between P and "the x such that x=1 and P" is entirely different from the logical equivalence between "1+1=2" and "triangles have three sides." Without recognizing this, many Slingshots fail. | Unified Semantics for Noun-Phrases
KUCZYNSKI | All noun-phrases—"Plato," "some man," "every bird," "the teacher"—should denote the same basic kind of entity to respect the grammatical unity of the category. | Unified Semantics for Noun-Phrases
KUCZYNSKI | A token of "Plato" denotes a function that assigns truth to a class C exactly if Plato is a member of C. This makes tokens of "Plato" fall into the same semantic category as tokens of quantifiers. | Unified Semantics for Noun-Phrases
KUCZYNSKI | A token of "some" denotes a function F that assigns truth to a class C exactly if C is instantiated (non-empty). | Unified Semantics for Noun-Phrases
KUCZYNSKI | A token of "every" denotes a function F that assigns truth to a class C exactly if C is universally instantiated. | Unified Semantics for Noun-Phrases
KUCZYNSKI | Tokens of proper names are directly referential by way of encoding functions that assign truth to classes based on whether the named individual is a member. | Truth as Instantiatedness and the Unity of the Proposition
KUCZYNSKI | The character of "I am tired" is not a function from contexts to propositions but a function that assigns meaning to its tokens. | Truth as Instantiatedness and the Unity of the Proposition
KUCZYNSKI | Propositions have decompositional structure. A proposition is not specified merely by its truth-conditions; its decomposition must also be given. | Truth as Instantiatedness and the Unity of the Proposition
KUCZYNSKI | The Lewisian analysis (propositions as properties of worlds/sets of worlds) cannot accommodate the internal or decompositional differences between analytically equivalent propositions. | Truth as Instantiatedness and the Unity of the Proposition
KUCZYNSKI | If propositions were merely properties of worlds, analytic propositions would "disappear"—they would tell us nothing since all worlds have them. | Truth as Instantiatedness and the Unity of the Proposition
KUCZYNSKI | The naïve view that "Smith punches Jones" has three constituents (Smith, Jones, the relation of punching) overlooks additional structure. | Truth as Instantiatedness and the Unity of the Proposition
KUCZYNSKI | Frege correctly saw that the proposition "Smith punches Jones" is built out of the relation "x punches y" and the functions "x punches Jones" and "Smith punches x." | Truth as Instantiatedness and the Unity of the Proposition
KUCZYNSKI | A proposition is identical with a set S of properties, and for that proposition to be true is for all members of S to be instantiated. | Truth as Instantiatedness and the Unity of the Proposition
KUCZYNSKI | For the proposition "Smith punches Jones" (Ap), the relevant set S contains exactly these properties: - Ps: the property of being identical with Smith - Pj: the property of being identical with Jones - Psx: the property of being a thing x such that Smith punches x - Pxj: the property of being a thing x such that x punches Jones - Pp: the relation of punching - P: the property of being a world where Smith punches Jones is true | Truth as Instantiatedness and the Unity of the Proposition
KUCZYNSKI | (Ap) is true exactly if all six properties are instantiated. If any one is not instantiated, (Ap) is not true. | Truth as Instantiatedness and the Unity of the Proposition
KUCZYNSKI | The instantiation of these properties is both necessary and sufficient for (Ap)'s truth. There is a one-to-one correspondence between discrete constituents of (Ap) and members of S. | Truth as Instantiatedness and the Unity of the Proposition
KUCZYNSKI | Different propositions can share constituents on this analysis. "Smith punches Jones" and "Plato punches Jones" share Pj, Pxj, and Pp, corresponding to the pre-theoretic intuition that they share constituents (Jones, the property of punching Jones, the relation of punching). | Meaning vs. Cognitive Content
KUCZYNSKI | A contingent proposition is never just true—it is true in virtue of some fact about the basic constituents of the world. Mass-energy is distributed a certain way; the "quantum is rippled" appropriately. | Meaning vs. Cognitive Content
KUCZYNSKI | The truth of "Plato punched Socrates" supervenes on facts about atomic movements, electron jumps, and mass-energy displacements. These facts are more basic than the proposition and can be described without mentioning Plato, Socrates, or punching. | Meaning vs. Cognitive Content
KUCZYNSKI | There is no circularity in analyzing a proposition in terms of the property a world has if that proposition is true, because the property (the "rippling") is more basic than the proposition. | Meaning vs. Cognitive Content
KUCZYNSKI | The storm of mass-energy displacements that realizes an event does not have the structure of the propositions describing it. Jones in the proposition is discrete from his attributes; Jones in reality is inseparable from the states of affairs on which his existence supervenes. | Meaning vs. Cognitive Content
KUCZYNSKI | The proposition "nothing snores" (ns) is identical with a set containing: - The property of being identical with the property "x snores" (the propositional form) - The property UT (universal falsity—every instance of the form is false) - The property P (the way the quantum is rippled in a world where nothing snores) | Meaning vs. Cognitive Content
KUCZYNSKI | The proposition "everything snores" (es) is identical with a set containing: - The property of being identical with the property "x snores" - The property UT (universal truth—every instance of the form is true) - The property P (the appropriate quantum rippling) | Meaning vs. Cognitive Content
KUCZYNSKI | The proposition "something snores" (ss) is identical with a set containing: - The property of being identical with the property of snoring - The property PT (being instantiated) - The property s* (the quantum rippling for a world where something snores) | Meaning vs. Cognitive Content
KUCZYNSKI | Quantified propositions are meta-propositional—they concern classes of propositions. "Something snores" says at least one | Meaning vs. Cognitive Content
KUCZYNSKI | Grammatical inflections do not themselves refer to anything but assign referents to sequences of expressions on the basis of the referents of the components. | Critique of Externalism
KUCZYNSKI | The difference between a sentence-token and an unmeaning heap lies in facts about grammar. | Critique of Externalism
KUCZYNSKI | Word-order in English does exactly what is done in Latin or Russian by case-endings. It is a grammatical device par excellence. | Critique of Externalism
KUCZYNSKI | Grammatical inflections determine what will be the constituents of the propositions meant by sentence-tokens. | Critique of Externalism
KUCZYNSKI | Denotes₁: E denotes₁ O exactly if, in virtue of having the form "…E…," a sentence-token encodes a proposition P such that P's being true involves a state of affairs having O as a constituent. | Critique of Externalism
KUCZYNSKI | Denotes₂: E denotes₂ O exactly if, in virtue of having the form "…E…," a sentence-token encodes a proposition P such that O is a member of the set with which P is identical. | Critique of Externalism
KUCZYNSKI | Particulars like Smith are not separable, isolable constituents of the universe. | Critique of Externalism
KUCZYNSKI | Individuals are properties of states of affairs. | Critique of Externalism
KUCZYNSKI | What we call "properties" (redness, roundness) are really properties of properties of properties—third-order properties. | Critique of Externalism
KUCZYNSKI | Literal meaning (semantic encoding) must be distinguished from communicated meaning (cognitive significance). | Critique of Externalism
KUCZYNSKI | Rationality is measured in terms of communicated meaning, not literal meaning. | Critique of Externalism
KUCZYNSKI | 'Hesperus' names the last celestial body to disappear from the morning sky' is really to say 'Something x is uniquely a last celestial body to disappear from the morning sky and 'Hesperus' names x.' | Necessity A Posteriori
KUCZYNSKI | All perception is existential/predicational. When you see an object, the content of your perception is given by an existence claim: 'there is an object x such that x has shape [...] and luminosity [...] and...' | Necessity A Posteriori
KUCZYNSKI | The cognitive significance of 'Hesperus is lovely' (for someone taught via ostension) is an existence claim: 'There is an object x such that x is alone in the morning sky, has [...] shape, [...] luminosity, and 'Hesperus' names x, and x is lovely.' | Necessity A Posteriori
KUCZYNSKI | 'Hesperus' and 'Phosphorus' are synonymous (both are mere labels for Venus), but sentences containing them can have different cognitive significance because the existence claims through which their meanings are communicated differ. | Necessity A Posteriori
KUCZYNSKI | The proposition literally encoded in a sentence is object-involving, but the cognitive significance may not be. The planet Hesperus is 'existentialized out' of what is cognitively grasped. | Necessity A Posteriori
KUCZYNSKI | Semantic externalism (about literal meaning) is true; content externalism (about mental content) is false. | Necessity A Posteriori
KUCZYNSKI | Semantic externalism: The literal meaning of 'water quenches thirst' differs in world w (where water is H₂O) and world w* (where 'water' refers to XYZ). Literal meaning does not supervene on what is 'in people's heads.' | Necessity A Posteriori
KUCZYNSKI | Content externalism (rejected): The view that two subjects who are intrinsically identical (leaving aside distal causes) can have different mental contents simply because the distal causes of their states differ. | Necessity A Posteriori
KUCZYNSKI | The information Jones-in-w associates with 'water quenches thirst' is identical to what Jones-in-w* associates with it. Both grasp the same existence claim. What differs is the truth-maker, not the truth-condition | Necessity A Posteriori
KUCZYNSKI | Truth-makers can vary while truth-conditions remain constant. | Empty and Fictitious Names
KUCZYNSKI | Externalism creates a dilemma about self-knowledge. | Empty and Fictitious Names
KUCZYNSKI | If Hesperus-thoughts require a causal connection to Hesperus, and if I can know with incorrigible certainty that I am thinking about Hesperus, then I would have incorrigible proof of the external world—which is absurd. | Empty and Fictitious Names
KUCZYNSKI | When Smith says "I know with incorrigible certainty that I am thinking that Hesperus is lovely," the de dicto (wide-scope) reading is what he grasps: he knows incorrigibly that he is thinking an existence claim. | Empty and Fictitious Names
KUCZYNSKI | Whatever is going on in one's Cartesian theatre does not logically necessitate the existence of any external object. | Empty and Fictitious Names
KUCZYNSKI | Conception is never constituted by a causal connection between subject and object. | Empty and Fictitious Names
KUCZYNSKI | A causal connection may be mentioned in the identifying description, but it is never constitutive of the concept itself. | Empty and Fictitious Names
KUCZYNSKI | Causal relations are not fine-grained enough for reference. | Empty and Fictitious Names
KUCZYNSKI | Any causal relation I have to the clay is also a causal relation to the statue, but I can refer to one without referring to the other. | Empty and Fictitious Names
KUCZYNSKI | The causal theory does not apply to expressions with constituent structure. | Empty and Fictitious Names
KUCZYNSKI | My concept of "brown angry cow" is not a causal connection to brown angry cows. | Empty and Fictitious Names
KUCZYNSKI | Fodor's extreme causal theory of concepts (CCC) fails. | Empty and Fictitious Names
KUCZYNSKI | If some concepts are causal connections while others (complex concepts) are not, the concept of "concept" becomes implausibly disjunctive. | Empty and Fictitious Names
KUCZYNSKI | Some thoughts clearly have their representational contents essentially (e.g., the thought that 2+2=4). It is arbitrary to say some thoughts have this property while others (externally individuated thoughts) do not. | Indexicals, Anaphora, and Definite Descriptions
KUCZYNSKI | Externalist mental content appears causally inert. What does causal work is the local, intrinsic brain state shared by Smith and Twin-Smith. The difference in distal cause (Mary vs. Twin-Mary) makes no causal difference to behavior. | Indexicals, Anaphora, and Definite Descriptions
KUCZYNSKI | Jackson and Pettit's attempt to save externalism via "program causes" fails. The distinction between causal efficacy and causal relevance does not rescue the causal potency of externally individuated content. | Indexicals, Anaphora, and Definite Descriptions
KUCZYNSKI | The proposition literally encoded in "Hesperus is Phosphorus" is analytic: it is "Hesperus is Hesperus" (= Venus is Venus), which is trivially necessary. | Indexicals, Anaphora, and Definite Descriptions
KUCZYNSKI | What is a posteriori is not the proposition encoded but the empirical work needed to discover that "Hesperus" and "Phosphorus" co-refer. The cognitive significance involves existence claims that are contingent. | Indexicals, Anaphora, and Definite Descriptions
KUCZYNSKI | "Hesperus might not have been Phosphorus" is, strictly speaking, absurd. A thing cannot be other than itself. What is not absurd is a claim about our epistemic situation: the perceptions associated with "Hesperus" and "Phosphorus" could have been caused by two distinct objects. | Indexicals, Anaphora, and Definite Descriptions
KUCZYNSKI | When we say S expresses a "necessary a posteriori" truth, we really mean: the literal meaning of S is analytic, but empirical work is needed to figure out that fact. | Indexicals, Anaphora, and Definite Descriptions
KUCZYNSKI | The wide-scope/narrow-scope distinction explains the appearance of "necessary a posteriori." Giving the necessity operator narrowest scope yields analytic truths; widest scope yields contingent falsehoods. | Indexicals, Anaphora, and Definite Descriptions
KUCZYNSKI | Empty non-fictitious names (like "Vulcan") were supposed to refer but don't. The cognitive significance of "Vulcan is a planet" is still an existence claim, explaining why such sentences are cognitively meaningful. | Indexicals, Anaphora, and Definite Descriptions
KUCZYNSKI | Fictitious names (like "Sherlock Holmes") are not even supposed to refer—they are supposed to be supposed to refer. Every story operates under an implicit "suppose that" operator. | Indexicals, Anaphora, and Definite Descriptions
KUCZYNSKI | There is no need to posit the actual existence of fictional objects. | Quine on Intensional Verbs (Corrected)
KUCZYNSKI | Mythical objects (Zeus, Hera) are not fictitious—they were posits of erroneous beliefs, like Vulcan and phlogiston. | Quine on Intensional Verbs (Corrected)
KUCZYNSKI | "he" is not ambiguous between its demonstrative and anaphoric uses—the anaphoric "he" is a quotation within the semantic rule. | The Frege-Russell Semantic Rule for Quantifiers (Vindicated)
KUCZYNSKI | Definite descriptions behave exactly like indexicals: tokens of "the phi" refer to the contextually salient instance of the sortal phi. | The Frege-Russell Semantic Rule for Quantifiers (Vindicated)
KUCZYNSKI | Even mathematical descriptions like "the sum of two and three" are context-sensitive in principle. Since only one entity can instantiate that sortal, it is by default always the salient instance. | The Frege-Russell Semantic Rule for Quantifiers (Vindicated)
KUCZYNSKI | "John wants a sloop" is really compressed from "John wants that John has a sloop." 108. | Indirect Discourse
KUCZYNSKI | **Phonetics sometimes obscures the propositional nature of intensional verb complements.** Once we decompress the sentence, (F) yields both wide-scope and narrow-scope readings correctly. | Indirect Discourse
KUCZYNSKI | 109. **In indirect discourse contexts, expressions do not shift in meaning.** "That Plato snores" denotes the same proposition in "Plato snores," "Fred believes that Plato snores," and "That Plato snores is possible." | Indirect Discourse
KUCZYNSKI | 110. **What varies across contexts is what is being said about the proposition, not what the proposition-denoting expression means.** | Indirect Discourse
KUCZYNSKI | 111. **The occurrence of "Plato snores" in "It is possible that Plato snores" is non-truth-functional not because of a meaning shift but because truth has not been ascribed to the proposition—only possibility.** | Indirect Discourse
KUCZYNSKI | **Sentence-tokens refer to functions involving propositions (not truth-values).** | Indirect Discourse
KUCZYNSKI | **Propositions are sets of properties; truth is the instantiation of all members.** | Indirect Discourse
KUCZYNSKI | **The unity of the proposition is explained by the set-theoretic structure containing properties corresponding to all constituents and sub-constituents.** | Indirect Discourse
KUCZYNSKI | **Literal meaning and cognitive significance (communicated meaning) must be distinguished.** | Indirect Discourse
KUCZYNSKI | **Semantic externalism is true; content externalism is false.** | Indirect Discourse
KUCZYNSKI | **The "necessary a posteriori" is really the analytic grasped through empirical investigation.** | Indirect Discourse
KUCZYNSKI | **All noun-phrases denote functions from classes to truth-values, unifying proper names with quantifiers.** | Indirect Discourse
KUCZYNSKI | **Grammar does not refer but assigns referents to expression-sequences.** | Indirect Discourse
KUCZYNSKI | **Individuals are abstractions—properties of states of affairs, not independent substances.** | Indirect Discourse

KUCZYNSKI | There are often pragmatic incentives to act morally. | Introduction to Morality and Self-Interest
KUCZYNSKI | In order to stay out of jail or to move up the corporate ladder, it may be necessary at certain junctures to act morally. | Introduction to Morality and Self-Interest
KUCZYNSKI | But setting aside such pragmatic considerations, is it in one’s interest to act morally? | Introduction to Morality and Self-Interest
KUCZYNSKI | In other words, is it categorically in one’s interest to do so? | Introduction to Morality and Self-Interest
KUCZYNSKI | The purpose of this chapter is to answer that question. | Introduction to Morality and Self-Interest
KUCZYNSKI | That answer is a very qualified “yes.” | Introduction to Morality and Self-Interest
KUCZYNSKI | The very purpose of the laws we create is to incentivize moral conduct and to dis-incentivize immoral conduct. | Introduction to Morality and Self-Interest
KUCZYNSKI | Because in its more extreme forms, non-cooperation with others is therefore punished by law, the pursuits that are legally available to one tend to involve behaviors that are, to some degree at least, conducive to the welfare of others. | Introduction to Morality and Self-Interest
KUCZYNSKI | But, quite obviously, it’s also frequently to one’s advantage to act immorally towards others. | Introduction to Morality and Self-Interest
KUCZYNSKI | It’s usually to one’s advantage to curry favor with those in power. | Introduction to Morality and Self-Interest
KUCZYNSKI | Those in power want you to serve their interests, and their interests are seldom those of morality. | Introduction to Morality and Self-Interest
KUCZYNSKI | No viable legal system could possibly dis-incentivize all immoral behavior. | Introduction to Morality and Self-Interest
KUCZYNSKI | A legal system that eliminated all forms of immorality would, in so doing, penalize all forms of aggressive, anti-social, and self-interested behavior, except in the relatively few cases where it benefited others. | Introduction to Morality and Self-Interest
KUCZYNSKI | This means that, were all human conduct to fall within the scope of such a legal system, there would be no heroes, only zeros. | Introduction to Morality and Self-Interest
KUCZYNSKI | In a world of completely moral people, there would be no innovators, no creators—only milquetoast do-gooders and innocuous squares. | The Advantages and Disadvantages of Immorality
KUCZYNSKI | Given that a certain degree of immorality is inherent in practically any great accomplishment, it follows that a legal system that made it completely impossible to act at all immorally would itself be supremely immoral. | The Advantages and Disadvantages of Immorality
KUCZYNSKI | A world where it isn’t even one’s option to act immorally is a world where one doesn’t have any options. | The Advantages and Disadvantages of Immorality
KUCZYNSKI | Therefore, a legal system that made all immorality impossible would strip people of their autonomy, and, for this reason, would itself be immoral. | The Advantages and Disadvantages of Immorality
KUCZYNSKI | So far as one’s reason for behaving ethically is that it is to one’s advantage to do so, one’s behavior has no moral worth and, therefore, is not itself moral. | The Advantages and Disadvantages of Immorality
KUCZYNSKI | His sadism and wish to hurt others is surpassed only by his wish to save his own skin. | The Advantages and Disadvantages of Immorality
KUCZYNSKI | But Brown’s excellent job performance is fueled by hate and ill-will, not by a love of justice. | The Advantages and Disadvantages of Immorality
KUCZYNSKI | In general, unless the agent’s intention is to act morally, his behavior is not moral, even if, from an external point of view, it is indistinguishable from moral conduct. | The Advantages and Disadvantages of Immorality
KUCZYNSKI | That’s why there are deeply moral people who are unhappy for the very reason that they are so moral. | The Advantages and Disadvantages of Immorality
KUCZYNSKI | But there’s a deeper, less circumstantial connection between hyper-morality and unhappiness. | The Advantages and Disadvantages of Immorality
KUCZYNSKI | The conscience of a deeply moral person, therefore, is one that embodies an attitude of hate, contempt, and aggression towards a facet of oneself. | The Advantages and Disadvantages of Immorality
KUCZYNSKI | Since nobody not weighed down by such a conscience can embody a life of pure morality, perfect morality isn’t even compatible with happiness. | The Advantages and Disadvantages of Immorality
KUCZYNSKI | There are many deeply immoral people who are truly happy. | The Advantages and Disadvantages of Immorality
KUCZYNSKI | They don’t simply feel good; they aren’t experiencing the fake happiness that comes with self-deception and cheap pleasures. | The Advantages and Disadvantages of Immorality
KUCZYNSKI | Being happy consists, at least in part, in judging that one’s life conforms to some ideal that one has and, therefore, that it is in compliance with some value. | The Advantages and Disadvantages of Immorality
KUCZYNSKI | Happiness isn’t so much a feeling as it is a conviction that how one is coincides with how, relative to some value system or other, one should be. | The Advantages and Disadvantages of Immorality
KUCZYNSKI | Thus, it is only to the extent that a creature values things that it makes sense to describe it as happy or unhappy. | The Advantages and Disadvantages of Immorality
KUCZYNSKI | Happiness is partly based on what one does and, therefore, on things that are within one’s control. | The Nature of Happiness and Morality
KUCZYNSKI | Being happy involves behaving in ways that one approves of—it involves doing the things that one believes it important to do. | The Nature of Happiness and Morality
KUCZYNSKI | Smith is a great novelist who has fully capitalized on his talent. But, through no fault of his own, he has a terrible, chronic illness, which causes him constant pain. | The Nature of Happiness and Morality
KUCZYNSKI | According to that analysis, being happy is identical with, or at least involves, having the conviction that one is as one wants oneself to be. | The Nature of Happiness and Morality
KUCZYNSKI | One can be happy in some respects and unhappy in others. Smith is justly proud of his work and he finds his work exquisitely pleasurable. | The Nature of Happiness and Morality
KUCZYNSKI | But does it drain all happiness from it? It does not follow. It’s also possible that Smith is happy vis-à-vis his work but not vis-à-vis his health situation. | The Nature of Happiness and Morality
KUCZYNSKI | Happiness is a structure, not a feeling. In this respect it’s more like one’s knowledge that 1 + 1 = 2 than it is like a passing feeling of joy. | The Nature of Happiness and Morality
KUCZYNSKI | Just as that recognition is an effect of, and is therefore distinct from, that knowledge, so joy and pleasure are effects of happiness, and are therefore distinct from it. | The Nature of Happiness and Morality
KUCZYNSKI | Given that happiness is a structure, it no longer seems unreasonable to say either that one can be happy in some respects while being unhappy in others or that happiness is a judgment. | The Nature of Happiness and Morality
KUCZYNSKI | Judgments, and therefore value-judgments, are not feelings. They are mental structures, not mental events or transient mental conditions, even though they obviously result in such events and conditions. | The Nature of Happiness and Morality
KUCZYNSKI | Therefore, value-judgments can result in joy, sadness, pleasure, and displeasure. And a positive value-judgment about oneself would, for obvious reasons, be likely to result in pleasant feelings, and a negative one would be likely to result in unpleasant feelings. | The Nature of Happiness and Morality
KUCZYNSKI | Moreover, that thesis is consistent with the supposition that one can be happy in some respects (e.g., about one’s literary accomplishments) and unhappy in others (e.g., one’s health or family situation). | The Nature of Happiness and Morality
KUCZYNSKI | For one can obviously judge that one’s life meets or exceeds certain standards, while falling short of others. | The Nature of Happiness and Morality
KUCZYNSKI | We have values, and in many cases our actions are driven by the values that we have. | The Nature of Happiness and Morality
KUCZYNSKI | Only animate beings meeting certain conditions have that privilege. Not all animate beings are subjects. | The Nature of Happiness and Morality
KUCZYNSKI | The difference between animate beings that are subjects and those that are not is that the former, unlike the latter, are able to regulate their own lives according to values that they have. | The Nature of Happiness and Morality
KUCZYNSKI | There are certain kinds of values that they lack that we have. But it is inherent in the very nature of subjecthood that its activity be value-driven. | The Nature of Happiness and Morality
KUCZYNSKI | It’s obvious that some body is associated with X in some way. And, of course, that body could be given a name. | The Nature of Happiness and Morality
KUCZYNSKI | When we have an experience, even one we consider base or trivial, we look it at through the lens of a preexisting structure, formed partly on the basis of other experiences, and that experience may, in its turn, become a part of such a structure. | Conditions for Happiness
KUCZYNSKI | If it’s exceptionally (un)pleasant, we’ll try to remember what caused it so as to (not) recreate it. | Conditions for Happiness
KUCZYNSKI | It is sometimes said that only human beings are self-conscious. If this is true, it is only in a narrow sense of the word “self-conscious.” | Conditions for Happiness
KUCZYNSKI | Because dogs and cats aren’t as good at we are at thinking abstractly, what they know is much more tightly tethered to their perceptual experiences than what we know is tethered to ours. | Conditions for Happiness
KUCZYNSKI | For there to be a subject, it isn’t enough that there be mental events. There must be a structure that not only responds to these events but does so in accordance with views as to how it should respond to them. | Conditions for Happiness
KUCZYNSKI | For a subject to exist, mental events must be responded to in a norm-driven manner. (This claim is crucial and will be clarified and defended in a moment.) | Conditions for Happiness
KUCZYNSKI | It’s one thing for your belief that Max has four cars to be the cause of your belief that Max has more than two cars. | Conditions for Happiness
KUCZYNSKI | M1 and M2 are presumably identical with, or constituted by some brain-structures of some kind. | Conditions for Happiness
KUCZYNSKI | So your belief that Max has four cars brought about your belief that he has more than two cars. | Conditions for Happiness
KUCZYNSKI | The connection between M1 and M2 is similar to the connection between my belief that Max has four cars and your consequent belief that he has more than two cars. | Conditions for Happiness
KUCZYNSKI | There’s nothing conceptually wrong with the idea. In fact, there is some experimental evidence that, at least arguably, suggests that different minds can co-occupy some one brain. | Subjecthood and Norm-Driven Behavior
KUCZYNSKI | The reason that we see brains as constitutive of minds is that we know on independent grounds that the mental events mediated by a given brain tend to constitute some one mind. | Subjecthood and Norm-Driven Behavior
KUCZYNSKI | That means that brains are individuative of minds only in a parasitic sense; which, in its turn, means that, ultimately, brains aren’t individuative of minds at all. | Subjecthood and Norm-Driven Behavior
KUCZYNSKI | My believing that Max has four cars could have led to a jealousy-induced psychotic break during which I had hallucinations of Max showing me the titles to three cars of his. | Subjecthood and Norm-Driven Behavior
KUCZYNSKI | Whenever one mental state is based on another, the first embodies a content-driven response to the second, and it also, for that very reason, embodies a norm-driven response to it. | Subjecthood and Norm-Driven Behavior
KUCZYNSKI | Content-driven reactions are norm-driven reactions. Given a set of mental events, none of which was a norm-driven response to any of the others, those events would not collectively form a single mind or subject. | Subjecthood and Norm-Driven Behavior
KUCZYNSKI | Mental states cohere, and form a single subject, only if some of them are content-driven responses to others. This means that there are subjects only where there are content-driven and therefore norm-driven reactions. | Subjecthood and Norm-Driven Behavior
KUCZYNSKI | A reaction that isn’t driven by any norm isn’t prejudiced, the reason being that prejudice is acceptance of illegitimate norms. | Subjecthood and Norm-Driven Behavior
KUCZYNSKI | An animal’s experience has the relevant sort of cumulativeness and, therefore, embodies the relevant sort of self-consciousness, which is why animals clearly are subjects. | Subjecthood and Norm-Driven Behavior
KUCZYNSKI | For a multiplicity of mental states associated with some one body to constitute a single mind, it is necessary that an awareness of what is appropriate given the contents of existing mental states—of, in other words, what is demanded by the contents of those states—be what drives the sequence of events associated with this body. | Subjecthood and Norm-Driven Behavior
KUCZYNSKI | Value-drivenness is the glue that makes a multiplicity of otherwise disconnected mental states constitute a single subject. | Subjecthood and Norm-Driven Behavior
KUCZYNSKI | The essence of subjecthood is sensitivity to value and, therefore, to a kind of proto-morality, if not a bona fide morality. | Subjecthood and Norm-Driven Behavior
KUCZYNSKI | “But not all values are moral values,” it will be said. “Ted Bundy valued killing people. But that wasn’t a moral value.” | Morality and Flourishing
KUCZYNSKI | “There are so many people who are suffering,” he tells himself, “so many people who don’t have food, water, plumbing, etc. So how can I justify writing novels all day, when others are deprived in such fundamental ways? Wouldn’t my doing so be nothing more than callous self-indulgence?” | Morality and Flourishing
KUCZYNSKI | The answer is clear: Brown’s. It would be nice if Smith’s life were the better one. But it isn’t. Brown has a good life. Smith does not. Smith’s life is morally good, of course. But it isn’t good in the relevant way—in the way that is relevant to his welfare, his flourishing. | Morality and Flourishing
KUCZYNSKI | A certain degree of asceticism and self-discipline is needed to accomplish anything of value. But there is a difference between self-discipline and self-torture. Brown is self-disciplined. | Morality and Flourishing
KUCZYNSKI | An ethical system must be rejected if one must mutilate oneself to comply with it. It should also be kept in mind that, if a given ethical system mutilates those who try to comply with it, those who advocate that system are probably well aware of that fact, and their advocacy of that system is almost assuredly rooted in their wish to hurt and diminish other human beings. | Morality and Flourishing
KUCZYNSKI | Ultimately, the only thing of value is flourishing. A life, such as Smith’s, that is “moral” is, sadly, a worthless one. | Morality and Flourishing
KUCZYNSKI | A life of self-denial—not of appropriate self-discipline, but of self-effacement—is, for that very reason, constructed around the premise that it itself is worthless and, for that reason, aspires to validate that premise through continual self-denigration. | Morality and Flourishing
KUCZYNSKI | No moral system can be viable if it fails to distinguish flourishing, which obviously has moral value, from mere self-indulgence, which does not. | Morality and Flourishing
KUCZYNSKI | The central tenet of any viable moral theory is that, ultimately, the one and only thing that is of value is flourishing. | Morality and Flourishing
KUCZYNSKI | What is generally described as “morality” is a kind of morality—a very important kind. | The Morality of Duty vs. Aspiration
KUCZYNSKI | Some ethicists distinguish between what they call “the morality of duty” and “the morality of aspiration.” | The Morality of Duty vs. Aspiration
KUCZYNSKI | The morality of duty is what would ordinarily be described as morality. It consists of the directives that forbid us, except under special circumstances, from killing, stealing, lying, etc. | The Morality of Duty vs. Aspiration
KUCZYNSKI | The so-called morality of aspiration is a very different sort of thing. It consists of the directives that one must follow if one is to have a good life—a life that is good in the sense in which Brown’s life is good, not in the alienated sense in which Smith’s life is good. | The Morality of Duty vs. Aspiration
KUCZYNSKI | Given how different the so-called “morality” of aspiration is from the acts of self-sacrifice that we would customarily see as paragons of morality—nay, given how antithetical the two are—it is by no means obvious that, in the expression “the morality of aspiration,” the word “morality” shouldn’t be replaced with some other. | The Morality of Duty vs. Aspiration
KUCZYNSKI | Smith’s life is one of constant torment. He is a person of talent and goodness who is rewarded for his troubles with unending distress. | The Morality of Duty vs. Aspiration
KUCZYNSKI | Thus, given what Smith has chosen to do with his life, his life per se is worthless, its only value being that it conduces to the welfare of others. | The Morality of Duty vs. Aspiration
KUCZYNSKI | Smith himself admits this—in his actions, if not his words. In persistently disregarding his own needs, he’s saying: “I live but for others; my own life is but a means to their ends.” | The Morality of Duty vs. Aspiration
KUCZYNSKI | It follows that what is intrinsically valuable for me is different from what is intrinsically valuable for you. | The Morality of Duty vs. Aspiration
KUCZYNSKI | MC is therefore comparable to the statement: (MC*) Effective medicines have value, the reason being that it enables people to have good health. | The Morality of Duty vs. Aspiration
KUCZYNSKI | But “immorality” can’t be defined as “the thwarting of other people’s interests”; there’s more to it than that, and the same thing mutatis mutandis is true of the term “morality.” | The Morality of Duty vs. Aspiration
KUCZYNSKI | In killing Smith, I’m undermining the integrity of social institutions on which the happiness and welfare of people in general depend. | The Morality of Duty vs. Aspiration
KUCZYNSKI | The existence of social structures is constitutive of personal identity. The aspirations that we identify with and that give our lives meaning are always defined in terms of social institutions. | The Morality of Duty vs. Aspiration
KUCZYNSKI | People who write novels are, in so doing, reaching out to others. | The Morality of Duty vs. Aspiration
KUCZYNSKI | The novelists that I know or have known of have all written a few early works that they never expected to publish. | Psychological Integrity and Moral Integrity
KUCZYNSKI | But in composing music, he wasn’t just talking to himself. He was addressing an audience. | Psychological Integrity and Moral Integrity
KUCZYNSKI | But the efforts of such people are in fact addressed to an audience. It may be a future audience. It may even be an imaginary audience. | Psychological Integrity and Moral Integrity
KUCZYNSKI | There are musical dialogues, architectural dialogues, technological dialogues, etc. When people meaningfully respond to other people’s efforts, they are, in so doing, dialoguing with one another. | Psychological Integrity and Moral Integrity
KUCZYNSKI | Even if they were boldly innovative, they were responses to existing style. In fact, what constitutes bold innovation is entirely relative to what is currently out there. | Psychological Integrity and Moral Integrity
KUCZYNSKI | Of course, had Frege lived in such a world and, by some miracle, had arrived at and written the insights for which, in actuality, he is known, his statements would have been meaningful. | Psychological Integrity and Moral Integrity
KUCZYNSKI | What people think of is the morality of duty. But the dependence of flourishing on the morality of duty is subordinate to its dependence on the morality of aspiration. | Psychological Integrity and Moral Integrity
KUCZYNSKI | To be a psychopath is either to have no conscience or to have one that functions only intermittently and, on those occasions, only to a small degree. | Psychological Integrity and Moral Integrity
KUCZYNSKI | A lot of deeply evil people have functioning consciences. Not all evil people are psychopaths (but most, if not all, psychopaths are evil). | Psychological Integrity and Moral Integrity
KUCZYNSKI | People can be evil in some contexts but not others; and, in the contexts where they’re evil, they’re not always evil in every respect. | Psychological Integrity and Moral Integrity
KUCZYNSKI | What Roy DeMeo did for a living was wicked in the extreme. But even in his professional life, Roy DeMeo conducted himself with a certain integrity. | Psychological Integrity and Moral Integrity
KUCZYNSKI | Non-psychopaths can be utterly evil. But there is a very big psychological difference between a psychopath and the non-psychopathic evil person. | Psychological Integrity and Moral Integrity
KUCZYNSKI | To be sure, they can do a lot of harm. Psychopaths often swindle people and they sometimes kill them. | Psychological Integrity and Moral Integrity
KUCZYNSKI | To get as far as he did, Mao had to evaluate his own conduct in a reasonably objective way. | Psychological Integrity and Moral Integrity
KUCZYNSKI | I’m just another person; and, just like others, I have to be judged for my failures. | Cultural and Personal Conceptions of Morality
KUCZYNSKI | Psychopaths don’t hold themselves to objective standards. When they fail, they don’t blame themselves; they blame circumstances. | Cultural and Personal Conceptions of Morality
KUCZYNSKI | Psychopaths are so self-indulgent that they don’t do what they have to do to succeed. | Cultural and Personal Conceptions of Morality
KUCZYNSKI | Evil non-psychopaths are, psychologically if not ethically, in a very different category from psychopaths. | Cultural and Personal Conceptions of Morality
KUCZYNSKI | There are profoundly evil people (e.g., Roy Demeo) who have deep connections with others, who are capable of operating within institutional frameworks (e.g., Molotov), who don’t have parasitic lifestyles (e.g., Ted Kaczynski), and so on. | Cultural and Personal Conceptions of Morality
KUCZYNSKI | Psychopaths, on the other hand, always have most or all of them. | Cultural and Personal Conceptions of Morality
KUCZYNSKI | Psychopaths cannot be honest with anybody, including themselves. | Cultural and Personal Conceptions of Morality
KUCZYNSKI | For non-psychopaths, the deceitfulness and manipulativeness are not ends unto themselves. | Cultural and Personal Conceptions of Morality
KUCZYNSKI | Thus, the self-recriminations of an evil non-psychopath have a basis in a wish to comply with a morality of duty, albeit one that others might (claim to) hold in contempt. | Cultural and Personal Conceptions of Morality
KUCZYNSKI | Given that conscience, and nothing besides, underlies all (non-coerced) compliance with the morality of duty, this shows that the self-censures of a Mao or a Genghis Khan are rooted in the very relationship to value and normativity that inhibits non-psychopathic, non-evil people from acting in antagonistic ways towards others. | Cultural and Personal Conceptions of Morality
KUCZYNSKI | Am I not simply dismissing these various different conceptions of morality, all of them reasonable, in saying that moral goodness is (the promotion of) flourishing? | Cultural and Personal Conceptions of Morality
KUCZYNSKI | The one thing that Smith truly values, in both himself and others, is a keen intellect. | Conclusion and Exercises
KUCZYNSKI | The one thing that Jones truly values is wealth. He holds people who don’t have it in contempt, and his one goal in life is to make more and more of it. | Conclusion and Exercises
KUCZYNSKI | The one thing that Lewis truly values is power—the ability to dominate others, to subjugate them to one’s will. | Conclusion and Exercises
KUCZYNSKI | The one thing that Ramsey truly values is loyalty to country and to national tradition. | Conclusion and Exercises
KUCZYNSKI | The one thing that Clancy truly values is doing God’s will. | Conclusion and Exercises
KUCZYNSKI | Finally, the one thing that Brown truly values is kindness. Money, power, and intelligence are fine; but whatever value they have is derivative of their ability to create a world in which people treat one another with decency and respect. | Conclusion and Exercises
KUCZYNSKI | Smith realizes that a world in which nobody valued anything other than a keen intellect would not be a good one. | Conclusion and Exercises
KUCZYNSKI | But Smith thinks that such traits, though necessary, aren’t in and of themselves, meritorious—they are good in an instrumental, not an intrinsic, sense. | Conclusion and Exercises
KUCZYNSKI | But for the fact that they may conduce to the sharpening of one’s intellect, they are worthless. | Conclusion and Exercises
KUCZYNSKI | These people disagree about what constitutes flourishing, but they all believe that flourishing is the one thing of value. | Conclusion and Exercises

KUCZYNSKI | There are often pragmatic incentives to act morally. In order to stay out of jail or to move up the corporate ladder, it may be necessary at certain junctures to act morally. | Introduction to Morality and Self-Interest
KUCZYNSKI | But setting aside such pragmatic considerations, is it in one’s interest to act morally? In other words, is it categorically in one’s interest to do so? | Introduction to Morality and Self-Interest
KUCZYNSKI | The very purpose of the laws we create is to incentivize moral conduct and to dis-incentivize immoral conduct. | Introduction to Morality and Self-Interest
KUCZYNSKI | Because in its more extreme forms, non-cooperation with others is therefore punished by law, the pursuits that are legally available to one tend to involve behaviors that are, to some degree at least, conducive to the welfare of others. | Introduction to Morality and Self-Interest
KUCZYNSKI | But, quite obviously, it’s also frequently to one’s advantage to act immorally towards others. It’s usually to one’s advantage to curry favor with those in power. | Introduction to Morality and Self-Interest
KUCZYNSKI | No viable legal system could possibly dis-incentivize all immoral behavior. Laws are blunt instruments. | Introduction to Morality and Self-Interest
KUCZYNSKI | And they should be blunt instruments, at least in some ways. | Introduction to Morality and Self-Interest
KUCZYNSKI | This means that, were all human conduct to fall within the scope of such a legal system, there would be no heroes, only zeros. | Introduction to Morality and Self-Interest
KUCZYNSKI | There would be no Beethovens, no Einsteins, no Freuds (Sigmund or Anna), no Mendelssohns (Felix or Fanny), no Emily Dickinsons, no Madame Curies, and no Emily Brontes. | Introduction to Morality and Self-Interest

KUCZYNSKI | Nothing is more evil than preventing people from flourishing. | The Advantages and Disadvantages of Immorality
KUCZYNSKI | Given that a certain degree of immorality is inherent in practically any great accomplishment, it follows that a legal system that made it completely impossible to act at all immorally would itself be supremely immoral. | The Advantages and Disadvantages of Immorality
KUCZYNSKI | A world where it isn’t even one’s option to act immorally is a world where one doesn’t have any options. | The Advantages and Disadvantages of Immorality
KUCZYNSKI | Therefore, a legal system that made all immorality impossible would strip people of their autonomy, and, for this reason, would itself be immoral. | The Advantages and Disadvantages of Immorality
KUCZYNSKI | So far as one’s reason for behaving ethically is that it is to one’s advantage to do so, one’s behavior has no moral worth and, therefore, is not itself moral. | The Advantages and Disadvantages of Immorality
KUCZYNSKI | In general, unless the agent’s intention is to act morally, his behavior is not moral, even if, from an external point of view, it is indistinguishable from moral conduct. | The Advantages and Disadvantages of Immorality
KUCZYNSKI | All publicly accepted systems of morality are to the effect that, with a few narrowly defined exceptions, no act that it is to one’s advantage to perform is permissible if it involves hurting someone else. | The Advantages and Disadvantages of Immorality
KUCZYNSKI | The “moral” person, almost by definition, grossly undervalues his own needs and aspirations, and the moral act is often the one that is the most self-undermining and inimical to one’s own welfare. | The Advantages and Disadvantages of Immorality
KUCZYNSKI | Thus, many people would be much happier if they were less moral. | The Advantages and Disadvantages of Immorality
KUCZYNSKI | A precondition for being profoundly moral is having a punitive and restrictive conscience: a conscience that allows one to do, feel, or think as one wishes only under narrowly defined circumstances—a conscience that holds one’s own psychological needs, and therefore one’s very self, in the deepest contempt. | The Advantages and Disadvantages of Immorality
KUCZYNSKI | The conscience of a deeply moral person, therefore, is one that embodies an attitude of hate, contempt, and aggression towards a facet of oneself. | The Advantages and Disadvantages of Immorality
KUCZYNSKI | There is happiness only where there is an enduring psychological subject—a mere body is not enough. | Happiness and Moral Worth
KUCZYNSKI | You’ve failed to live up to your own values, and you are thus unhappy. | Happiness and Moral Worth

KUCZYNSKI | Value-drivenness is the glue that makes a multiplicity of otherwise disconnected mental states constitute a single subject. | Morality and Flourishing
KUCZYNSKI | Morality thus has much deeper roots than we are inclined to think. | Morality and Flourishing
KUCZYNSKI | Morality is about respect for subjects. Moral values are those that must be complied with if subjects are to be allowed to exist—if, in other words, multiplicities of mental states are to have the integrity needed for them to constitute single minds. | Morality and Flourishing
KUCZYNSKI | Thus, a sensitivity to value, and thus a kind of morality, lies at the root of all subjecthood. | Morality and Flourishing
KUCZYNSKI | Flourishing as the one thing that has non-derivative moral value. | Morality and Flourishing
KUCZYNSKI | Smith would very much like to write novels. He’s extremely talented. He’d be happy if he spent his days writing. But Smith feels that, if he were to do so, he’d be acting immorally. | Morality and Flourishing
KUCZYNSKI | Driven by his admirable concern for the dictates of morality, Smith spends his days installing sewage systems in poor countries, giving food to the hungry, etc. | Morality and Flourishing
KUCZYNSKI | Let’s contrast Smith’s situation with Brown’s. Like Smith, Brown is a talented novelist. But unlike Smith, Brown permits himself to write novels. | Morality and Flourishing
KUCZYNSKI | Although Brown is a selfish swine, he does have artistic integrity. | Morality and Flourishing
KUCZYNSKI | Whose life is better, Smith’s or Brown’s? It’s obvious that Smith’s life is the morally better one. But what I’m asking is: whose life is actually a richer, better, less empty one? The answer is clear: Brown’s. | Morality and Flourishing
KUCZYNSKI | Smith’s life is conforming to a standard that is totally external to itself—that has no basis in its own logic. | The Morality of Duty vs. Aspiration
KUCZYNSKI | Brown is self-disciplined. He works hard to hone his skills as a novelist. | The Morality of Duty vs. Aspiration
KUCZYNSKI | Smith’s self-discipline is about not doing what he has to do to flourish; it’s about denying who he is, not enhancing it. | The Morality of Duty vs. Aspiration
KUCZYNSKI | Some ethicists have tried to show that, ultimately, lives such as Brown’s are hollow and that lives such as Smith’s are meaningful and good. | The Morality of Duty vs. Aspiration
KUCZYNSKI | An ethical system must be rejected if one must mutilate oneself to comply with it. | The Morality of Duty vs. Aspiration
KUCZYNSKI | Ultimately, the only thing of value is flourishing. | The Morality of Duty vs. Aspiration
KUCZYNSKI | No moral system can be viable if it fails to distinguish flourishing, which obviously has moral value, from mere self-indulgence, which does not. | The Morality of Duty vs. Aspiration
KUCZYNSKI | The central tenet of any viable moral theory is that, ultimately, the one and only thing that is of value is flourishing. | The Morality of Duty vs. Aspiration
KUCZYNSKI | It consists of the directives that forbid us, except under special circumstances, from killing, stealing, lying, etc. | The Morality of Duty vs. Aspiration
KUCZYNSKI | The so-called morality of aspiration is a very different sort of thing. | The Morality of Duty vs. Aspiration
KUCZYNSKI | We’ve already answered the first question—the answer to it is “yes.” We saw this when discussing Smith and Brown. | The Morality of Duty vs. Aspiration
KUCZYNSKI | Smith himself admits this—in his actions, if not his words. | The Morality of Duty vs. Aspiration
KUCZYNSKI | Acting morally at least sometimes consists of acting in a way that’s conducive to the flourishing of others. | The Morality of Duty vs. Aspiration
KUCZYNSKI | It would therefore make no sense to say that flourishing itself has no value. | The Morality of Duty vs. Aspiration
KUCZYNSKI | MC is therefore comparable to the statement: (MC*) Effective medicines have value, the reason being that it enables people to have good health. | The Morality of Duty vs. Aspiration
KUCZYNSKI | Acts that we typically describe as “immoral” are those that are incompatible with the interests of others. | The Morality of Duty vs. Aspiration
KUCZYNSKI | A judge who sentences a serial killer to a prison sentence violates the interest of another human. | Psychological Integrity and Moral Integrity
KUCZYNSKI | In killing Smith, I’m undermining the integrity of social institutions on which the happiness and welfare of people in general depend. | Psychological Integrity and Moral Integrity
KUCZYNSKI | Wanting to be a judge or a civil engineer in a state of anarchy would be like wanting to be a father but to have no children. | Psychological Integrity and Moral Integrity
KUCZYNSKI | People do not, ultimately, write novels only for their own amusement. | Psychological Integrity and Moral Integrity
KUCZYNSKI | The novelists that I know or have known of have all written a few early works that they never expected to publish. | Psychological Integrity and Moral Integrity
KUCZYNSKI | But it doesn’t follow that one must oneself comply with the morality of duty to have a flourishing life. | Psychological Integrity and Moral Integrity
KUCZYNSKI | The other point is that being evil isn’t the same thing as being a psychopath. | Psychological Integrity and Moral Integrity
KUCZYNSKI | Evil people can, and usually do, have consciences. | Psychological Integrity and Moral Integrity
KUCZYNSKI | Roy DeMeo conducted himself with a certain integrity. He didn’t break his word to those with whom he was professionally transacting; he repaid his debts, honored his contracts, etc. | Psychological Integrity and Moral Integrity
KUCZYNSKI | There is a very big psychological difference between a psychopath and the non-psychopathic evil person. | Psychological Integrity and Moral Integrity
KUCZYNSKI | If one can’t admit that one has fallen short, one won’t ever make the needed changes to oneself, and won’t succeed in one’s endeavors, be they morally wholesome ones or profoundly wicked ones. | Cultural Differences in Moral Values
KUCZYNSKI | To get as far as he did, Mao had to evaluate his own conduct in a reasonably objective way. | Cultural Differences in Moral Values
KUCZYNSKI | Given that what I want is such and such, am I doing what I have to do, or am I falling short? And if I am falling short, how must I change myself to get back on track? | Cultural Differences in Moral Values
KUCZYNSKI | Mao had to censure himself and henceforth act in a way that wouldn’t warrant additional self-censure. To be sure, Mao was not censuring himself for what others would regard as moral lapses. | Cultural Differences in Moral Values
KUCZYNSKI | There are standards. I can fall short of them, just like anyone else. And I have fallen short of them. Like anyone else, I must change myself to make things right. | Cultural Differences in Moral Values
KUCZYNSKI | Other people can’t get themselves off the hook by blaming circumstances or by blaming the standards they’ve failed to meet. Neither can I. I must blame myself. | Cultural Differences in Moral Values
KUCZYNSKI | In general, a certain morality of duty is inherent in any case of self-censure. | Cultural Differences in Moral Values
KUCZYNSKI | Thus, embodied in any case of self-censure is compliance with a certain morality of duty. | Cultural Differences in Moral Values
KUCZYNSKI | Psychopaths are so self-indulgent that they don’t do what they have to do to succeed. | Cultural Differences in Moral Values
KUCZYNSKI | There are usually people to whom they’re honest. By contrast, psychopaths cannot be honest with anybody, including themselves. | Cultural Differences in Moral Values
KUCZYNSKI | What’s going on is that they do feel it, but not in the right contexts. | Cultural Differences in Moral Values
KUCZYNSKI | In conclusion, the self-discipline of a Mao is, from a psychological standpoint, no less conscience-driven than your reluctance to steal so and so’s money. | Cultural Differences in Moral Values
KUCZYNSKI | And given that the ability to self-censure, and change oneself in light of one’s self-censures, underlies all cases of non-trivial flourishing, in both the evil and the good alike, it follows that a wish to comply with a morality of duty underlies, and is essential to, any case of flourishing. | Cultural Differences in Moral Values
KUCZYNSKI | In identifying goodness with flourishing and the promotion thereof, I am not mistaking my personal idiosyncrasies for moral universals; nor am I saying anything inconsistent with anyone’s moral practices or beliefs. | Conclusion and Exercises
KUCZYNSKI | The differences in moral outlook between people and cultures have to do with differences in their views as to what constitutes flourishing. | Conclusion and Exercises
KUCZYNSKI | Nobody thinks that flourishing is bad or that its frustration is good, but people disagree as to what it is to flourish. | Conclusion and Exercises
KUCZYNSKI | Some people think it involves connectedness to the ways of one’s ancestors and, thus, in fidelity to tradition; others that it consists in breadth and intensity of awareness; others that it consists in having harmonious relations with others; others that it consists in submission to God’s will; others that it consists in loyalty to one’s comrades; and so on. | Conclusion and Exercises
KUCZYNSKI | The one thing that Smith truly values, in both himself and others, is a keen intellect. | Conclusion and Exercises
KUCZYNSKI | The one thing that Jones truly values is wealth. He holds people who don’t have it in contempt, and his one goal in life is to make more and more of it. | Conclusion and Exercises
KUCZYNSKI | The one thing that Lewis truly values is power—the ability to dominate others, to subjugate them to one’s will. | Conclusion and Exercises
KUCZYNSKI | The one thing that Ramsey truly values is loyalty to country and to national tradition. | Conclusion and Exercises
KUCZYNSKI | The one thing that Clancy truly values is doing God’s will. | Conclusion and Exercises
KUCZYNSKI | Finally, the one thing that Brown truly values is kindness. Money, power, and intelligence are fine; but whatever value they have is derivative of their ability to create a world in which people treat one another with decency and respect. | Conclusion and Exercises
KUCZYNSKI | Each of these people realizes that, if the world is to function, other people must have values other than the one she happens to cherish. | Conclusion and Exercises
KUCZYNSKI | People would be forced by economic necessity to become plumbers, hospital orderlies, etc. But their hearts wouldn’t be in their work, and their performance would often be shoddy. | Conclusion and Exercises
KUCZYNSKI | Smith knows all this and thus concedes that things besides keenness of intellect have a kind of value—but the value in question is of a strictly instrumental nature. | Conclusion and Exercises
KUCZYNSKI | People must have other character traits—for example, they must have at least some regard for others, they must not neglect their children, they must in general (though obviously not always) keep their word, etc. | Conclusion and Exercises
KUCZYNSKI | These people disagree about what constitutes flourishing, but they all believe that flourishing is the one thing of value. | Conclusion and Exercises
KUCZYNSKI | No two people disagree that flourishing is good. At most, they disagree about what flourishing involves. | Conclusion and Exercises
