kuczynski | What he is saying makes perfect sense when decoded | Perfect Sense Decoded
kuczynski | The good—i.e. the real, as Plato puts it—is what conduces to social stability | Good as Stability
kuczynski | And the bad—i.e. the mere shadows—are what threaten social stability | Bad Threatens Stability
kuczynski | A just society is one in which the galley slaves labor away in their dungeon, where they belong | Galley Slaves in Dungeon
kuczynski | And the aristocrats sit on the deck of the ship and enjoy the view | Aristocrats on Deck
kuczynski | Plato's epistemological views make little sense if taken as epistemological views | Little Epistemological Sense
kuczynski | But they make plenty of sense if taken as attempts to rationalize his social position | Rationalize Social Position
kuczynski | Descartes' epistemological views, unlike Plato's, are defensible on their own terms | Descartes Defensible
kuczynski | Like Plato's views, they are attempts to cope with social unrest | Cope with Unrest
kuczynski | When Descartes was writing, advances in knowledge were quickly stripping the Catholic Church of all credibility | Church Losing Credibility
kuczynski | Science as we know it was just coming into existence at that time | Science Emerging
kuczynski | With every step that it took, some cornerstone of Church doctrine was turning out to be totally wrong | Church Doctrine Wrong
kuczynski | This had to have been an enormous source of anxiety for Europeans | Enormous Anxiety
kuczynski | Given how thoroughly the Catholic Church had dominated every aspect of European life for the previous thousand years | Church Dominated
kuczynski | The Church had provided certainties | Church Provided Certainties
kuczynski | Those certainties proved false | Certainties Proved False
kuczynski | So Descartes took it upon himself to find new certainties | Find New Certainties
kuczynski | Just like Plato, he did epistemology to cope with threatening changes | Epistemology to Cope
kuczynski | But there is a difference | There Is a Difference
kuczynski | Plato was a reactionary | Plato Reactionary
kuczynski | He dealt with threatening changes by trying to thwart them | Thwart Changes
kuczynski | His epistemology is correspondingly conservative | Conservative Epistemology
kuczynski | For Plato, it isn't enough to see it or feel it or touch it | Not Enough to Sense
kuczynski | In order to be real, the high priest of the intellect has to give it his personal seal of approval | Intellect's Seal
kuczynski | Translation: before a social change can be allowed to occur, the aristocrats have to decide amongst themselves whether to let it occur | Aristocrats Decide
kuczynski | Descartes, by contrast, is anti-reactionary | Descartes Anti-Reactionary
kuczynski | Descartes accepts the changes that are occurring | Accepts Changes
kuczynski | He is looking for certainties within that new set of verities | Certainties in New Verities
kuczynski | Both Philosophers advocate particularly extreme forms of rationalism | Both Extreme Rationalism
kuczynski | Meaning, in effect, that they overplay the role of thought in the acquisition of knowledge | Overplay Thought
kuczynski | And underplay the role of observation | Underplay Observation
kuczynski | Both philosophers were uneasy about the changes in question | Both Uneasy
kuczynski | Descartes ambivalently so | Descartes Ambivalent
kuczynski | And Plato unambivalently so | Plato Unambivalent
kuczynski | And responded with rationalism-driven skepticism about the reality of the observed | Rationalism-Driven Skepticism
kuczynski | Plato celebrated the supposed unreality of the observable | Celebrated Unreality
kuczynski | In keeping with his complete antipathy towards the social changes in question | Complete Antipathy
kuczynski | Whereas Descartes attempted to overcome it | Descartes Overcame
kuczynski | Corresponding to his mixed feelings about those changes | Mixed Feelings
kuczynski | Both OCD and philosophy were about retreating from the exigencies of external actual reality | Retreating from Reality
kuczynski | Into the safety of internal pseudo-reality | Safety of Pseudo-Reality
kuczynski | Both OCD and philosophy are intellectualized impotence | Intellectualized Impotence
kuczynski | I am obviously not the first to say this about philosophy | Not the First
kuczynski | And I'm probably not even the first to say that it holds of both OCD and philosophy | Both OCD Philosophy
kuczynski | But I am probably one of the first to say that what we are dealing with here is not a mere analogy but a veritable homology | Veritable Homology
kuczynski | From a psychopathologist's viewpoint, OCD and philosophy are cut from the very same cloth | Same Cloth
kuczynski | What about political philosophers | Political Philosophers Question
kuczynski | Don't they engage the world | Engage World Question
kuczynski | Aren't they a counterexample to your contention that philosophy is about autistic non-engagement | Counterexample Question
kuczynski | Yes and no | Yes and No
kuczynski | Mostly no | Mostly No
kuczynski | Consider Plato, as he is a paradigm case of a political philosopher | Plato Paradigm Case
kuczynski | Plato's system was completely a priori | Completely A Priori
kuczynski | Yes—he was talking about how to reform the world | Talking Reform
kuczynski | But really, that isn't what he was doing | Not Really Reforming
kuczynski | He was really talking about how to replace the world with a world of his own | Replace with Own World
kuczynski | A world that had little reality, and little justification, outside of his own thought-space | Little Reality Outside Thought
kuczynski | Plato was really talking about replacing the actual world with his thought-world | Actual with Thought-World
kuczynski | He wasn't in any real way engaging the actual world | Not Engaging Actual
kuczynski | The absurdity of what Plato was doing comes out in a lot of ways | Absurdity Shows
kuczynski | In the rigidity of the society that he was proposing | Rigid Society
kuczynski | In the stereotyped—and in that respect decidedly OCD—protocols governing both education and interactions between different people | OCD Protocols
kuczynski | In the absurd idea, put forth in his dialogue The Laws, that even the most minor violations of law should be punished by death | Death for Minor Violations
kuczynski | In the incoherent idea that society should be ruled by a philosopher king | Philosopher King Incoherent
kuczynski | Incoherent, because societies are to be run by sapient doers, not sapient thinkers | Doers Not Thinkers
kuczynski | By people who do not flee from the world into intellectual constructions | Not Flee into Constructions
kuczynski | And are therefore not to be run by philosophers | Not by Philosophers
kuczynski | Granting, of course, that they are to be run by people who are philosopher-like to the extent, but only to the extent, that they are intelligent | Philosopher-Like in Intelligence
kuczynski | Or consider Marx, another textbook case of a political philosopher | Marx Textbook Case
kuczynski | Yes—he was talking about how to change the world | Marx Talking Change
kuczynski | But really, he was talking, quite explicitly, about destroying the existing social structure | Destroying Structure
kuczynski | And replacing it with one that had no real basis—other than a totally fabricated one—in reality | No Real Basis
kuczynski | And which, when it was implemented, led to loss of life and opportunity on an unprecedented scale | Unprecedented Loss
kuczynski | Not all political philosophers are as guilty as Marx and Plato of failing to engage actual political realities | Not All Guilty
kuczynski | Some political philosophers do not simply propose that existing political realities be replaced with their childish a priori fantasies | Not Childish Fantasies
kuczynski | John Locke made a number of extremely reasonable points about law and economics | Locke Reasonable
kuczynski | That, when implemented, were enormously successful | Enormously Successful
kuczynski | Much the same is true, though in some respects to an even greater degree, of Adam Smith and David Hume | Smith and Hume
kuczynski | Who, along with Locke, created the discipline of modern free-market economics | Created Free-Market Economics
kuczynski | Adam Smith was really an ex-philosopher who left the discipline of philosophy in order to start a new discipline | Smith Ex-Philosopher
kuczynski | Locke and Hume were hard-core empiricists | Hard-Core Empiricists
kuczynski | Their whole agenda as philosophers being to empiricize philosophy | Empiricize Philosophy
kuczynski | Their criticism of existing philosophy was that it failed to engage the world | Philosophy Failed to Engage
kuczynski | Their objective was to reinvent the discipline in such a way that it did engage the world | Reinvent to Engage
kuczynski | Their political philosophies—which were very good—did engage the world | Good and Engaged
kuczynski | But to the extent that they did, they were almost anti-philosophical political philosophies | Anti-Philosophical
kuczynski | Political theory can engage the world | Political Theory Engages
kuczynski | And that does represent a limitation on the scope of my point about philosophy categorically being in the nature of an autistic withdrawal from reality | Limitation on Point
kuczynski | But there is one last point to make | One Last Point
kuczynski | Which does to some extent buttress my conception of philosophy as autistic retreat | Buttress Autistic Retreat
kuczynski | Hume and Locke were arch-empiricists | Arch-Empiricists
kuczynski | Meaning that, in their view, you don't know it if you don't have direct sensory evidence of it | Direct Sensory Evidence
kuczynski | Empiricism collapses into the view that we can have knowledge of nothing except our own conscious states | Collapses to Conscious States
kuczynski | For the empiricist rejects all rules of inference that would enable us to posit the existence of anything | Rejects Inference Rules
kuczynski | And we are therefore left unable to know anything that is not simply a datum of consciousness | Only Consciousness Data
kuczynski | In their attempt to make philosophy engage the world | Attempt to Engage
kuczynski | Locke and Hume empiricized philosophy | Empiricized Philosophy
kuczynski | But in empiricizing philosophy, Locke and Hume made philosophy collapse into pure skepticism | Collapse into Skepticism
kuczynski | Pure skepticism being the most complete conceivable autistic withdrawal from reality into one's own mind | Most Complete Withdrawal

kuczynski | This paper is divided into three parts. | Introduction
kuczynski | In the first, we discuss the cogency of Kant’s arguments concerning causation and induction. | Introduction
kuczynski | Those arguments are spurious, we will find, because they all rest on the erroneous assumption that all non-empirical truths are trivial. | Introduction
kuczynski | At the same time, so we will also find, each of those arguments corresponds to a cogent argument that does not involve that assumption. | Introduction
kuczynski | In the second part, we discuss the viability of Hume’s analyses of causation and induction. | Introduction
kuczynski | It is argued that those analyses are fallacious, and an attempt is made to identify the relevant fallacies. | Introduction
kuczynski | In the third part, we draw some of the epistemological consequences of our response to Hume. | Introduction
kuczynski | By a 'proposition' I mean the non-linguistic entity meant by a complete sentence. | Introduction
kuczynski | I do not mean the sentence itself. | Introduction
kuczynski | 'La neige est blanche' and 'snow is white' mean the same thing. | Introduction
kuczynski | This common meaning is what I will refer to as a 'proposition'. | Introduction
kuczynski | Kant held that some propositions are both synthetic and also a posteriori. | Introduction
kuczynski | This contention is the heart of the system of thought set forth in the Critique of Pure Reason. | Introduction
kuczynski | I will now argue that this contention of Kant’s is false and that his argument for it is spurious. | Introduction
kuczynski | But I will also argue that Kant’s false contention can readily be turned into a correct one and that his spurious argument can readily be turned into a cogent one. | Introduction
kuczynski | Nothing more than a few terminological changes are needed to accomplish this. | Introduction
kuczynski | First, I will argue that the premises of Kant’s argument turn out to entail the opposite of the thesis that there are synthetic a priori truths. | Introduction
kuczynski | Those premises entail that there are virtually no a priori truths, let alone synthetic a priori truths. | Introduction
kuczynski | Kant turns out to be even more of an opponent of a priori truth than John Stuart Mill. | Introduction
kuczynski | Second, I will argue that Kant’s reasoning involves a confusion concerning the two different epistemological roles that mental imagery can play. | Introduction
kuczynski | On the one hand, mental imagery can serve as a vehicle for conceptual analysis. | Introduction
kuczynski | On the other hand, mental images are spatiotemporal phenomena in their own right, and can thus be the object of empirical study. | Introduction
kuczynski | Kant fails to distinguish the two quite distinct roles that mental images can have; and his views concerning the synthetic a priori are a reflection of that. | Introduction
kuczynski | Kant’s astonishing claims about causality, personal identity, and rationality turn out to be correct and founded on cogent arguments. | The Cogency of Kant’s Transcendental Arguments
kuczynski | Kant’s reasoning must be 'de-psychologized', i.e. his statements about psychology must be reinterpreted as statements about logic. | The Cogency of Kant’s Transcendental Arguments
kuczynski | The word 'concept' has two meanings – one logical, one psychological. | The Cogency of Kant’s Transcendental Arguments
kuczynski | In this paper, I will typically use the word 'concept' in its non-psychological sense. | The Cogency of Kant’s Transcendental Arguments
kuczynski | When I describe a proposition as 'conceptually necessary', I mean that it holds entirely in virtue of facts about concepts in the non-psychological sense of the word. | The Cogency of Kant’s Transcendental Arguments
kuczynski | The proposition triangles have three sides is therefore 'conceptually necessary', as I will be using this expression. | The Cogency of Kant’s Transcendental Arguments
kuczynski | Sometimes I will use the expression 'conceptually true' as a synonym for 'conceptually necessary.' | The Cogency of Kant’s Transcendental Arguments
kuczynski | In his view, some propositions are necessarily true in virtue of facts about our minds. | The Cogency of Kant’s Transcendental Arguments
kuczynski | When I wish to discuss that kind of necessity, I will use the term 'representational necessity.' | The Cogency of Kant’s Transcendental Arguments
kuczynski | If a proposition is known to be true in some way other than through empirical work, Kant describes it as 'a priori.' | The Cogency of Kant’s Transcendental Arguments
kuczynski | 1+1=2 is thus a priori. For Kant, a priority is a property of our knowledge of propositions. | The Cogency of Kant’s Transcendental Arguments
kuczynski | A consequence of this conception of a priority – one that Kant himself draws -- is that a given proposition could, in principle, be a priori for one creature and a posteriori for another. | The Cogency of Kant’s Transcendental Arguments
kuczynski | For Kant, a proposition is 'a posteriori' if it is not a priori. | The Cogency of Kant’s Transcendental Arguments
kuczynski | Kant uses the term 'analytic' to describe propositions – not sentences, and not our knowledge of anything. | The Cogency of Kant’s Transcendental Arguments
kuczynski | Kant’s official definition of 'analytic' is this: a proposition is analytic if 'nothing is contained in the predicate which is not contained in the subject.' | The Cogency of Kant’s Transcendental Arguments
kuczynski | A proposition is 'synthetic' if it is not analytic. | The Cogency of Kant’s Transcendental Arguments
kuczynski | For Kant, a+b is larger than a is a paradigm case of an 'analytic' proposition, and John and Mary like to play tennis is a paradigm case of a proposition that Kant would describe as 'synthetic.' | The Cogency of Kant’s Transcendental Arguments
kuczynski | We may conclude that, for Kant, a proposition is 'analytic' exactly if it is conceptually true – exactly if it holds entirely in virtue of the structures of the concepts composing it. | The Cogency of Kant’s Transcendental Arguments
kuczynski | A proposition is synthetic exactly if it is not analytic. | The Cogency of Kant’s Transcendental Arguments
kuczynski | A proposition is synthetic a priori if it has both these properties. | The Cogency of Kant’s Transcendental Arguments
kuczynski | According to Kant, the propositions 7+5=12 and a straight line is the shortest distance between two points are synthetic a priori truths. | The Cogency of Kant’s Transcendental Arguments
kuczynski | 7+5=12 is true a priori, in Kant’s view, because no sense-experience is needed to know that it is true. | The Cogency of Kant’s Transcendental Arguments
kuczynski | It is synthetic because it is not true wholly in virtue of facts about the structures of the concepts composing it. | The Cogency of Kant’s Transcendental Arguments
kuczynski | But it very much seems that, contrary to what Kant says, both of these propositions are analytic. | The Cogency of Kant’s Transcendental Arguments
kuczynski | The truth of 7+5=12 seems to be guaranteed by the constitutions of the concepts 7, 5, +, and so on. | The Cogency of Kant’s Transcendental Arguments
kuczynski | 1+1=2 is clearly analytic, as Kant himself acknowledges. | The Cogency of Kant’s Transcendental Arguments
kuczynski | Kant is thus guilty not only of error, but of inconsistency with his own premises, in believing that 7+5=12 is synthetic. | The Cogency of Kant’s Transcendental Arguments
kuczynski | Kant begins by saying that, when you think 7+5, you don’t necessarily think =12. | The Cogency of Kant’s Transcendental Arguments
kuczynski | The fuzziness is due to the fact that Kant is not clear, either in his thinking or in his exposition, about whether he is using the term 'concept' in the logical, or the psychological sense; he goes back and forth. | The Cogency of Kant’s Transcendental Arguments
kuczynski | Given that you don’t think =12 when you think 7+5, it seems to follow that your concept (i.e. your grasp) of 7+5 doesn’t contain or include your concept =12. | The Cogency of Kant’s Transcendental Arguments
kuczynski | When you think straight line, you don’t necessarily think shortest distance between two points. | The Cogency of Kant’s Transcendental Arguments
kuczynski | What you think, says Kant, is purely qualitative, not quantitative; you think of a line with a certain shape, not a line of a certain distance. | The Cogency of Kant’s Transcendental Arguments
kuczynski | In Kant’s view, as we’ve just seen, the truth of 7+5=12 does not follow wholly from the concepts involved in that proposition: it is therefore synthetic. | The Cogency of Kant’s Transcendental Arguments
kuczynski | At the same time, Kant denies that it is an empirical truth: he denies that it is made true by what happens when apples are laid next to apples, when quarts of liquid are added to other quarts of liquid, and so on. | The Cogency of Kant’s Transcendental Arguments
kuczynski | Arithmetical truths are not inductive generalizations that we could, in principle, find to be false. | The Cogency of Kant’s Transcendental Arguments
kuczynski | The very existence of synthetic a priori propositions raises difficult questions. | The Cogency of Kant’s Transcendental Arguments
kuczynski | How do you know that 7+5=12? Not through conceptual analysis. Not through the senses. You learn it through an act of the imagination. | The Cogency of Kant’s Transcendental Arguments
kuczynski | You imagine seven objects laid next to five other objects, and then see that the resulting aggregate comprises exactly 12 objects. | The Cogency of Kant’s Transcendental Arguments
kuczynski | Kant refers to images of this sort as 'intuitions.' So it is through 'intuition' that you learn the truth of 7+5=12. | The Cogency of Kant’s Transcendental Arguments
kuczynski | By 'intuition' Kant means an act of imaging: a kind of para-perception. | The Cogency of Kant’s Transcendental Arguments
kuczynski | Kant’s whole point is that the concepts involved in 7+5=12 do not, by themselves, guarantee its truth. | The Cogency of Kant’s Transcendental Arguments
kuczynski | So the para-perceptions that teach us the truth of 7+5=12 do not do so by apprising us of necessary relations holding among the components of that proposition. | The Cogency of Kant’s Transcendental Arguments
kuczynski | At the same time, those para-sensory experiences don’t tell us anything about the external world: you can’t learn anything new about the external world through an act of imagination. | The Cogency of Kant’s Transcendental Arguments
kuczynski | They show us facts, not about the world or about concepts, but about how we must sense-perceive the world. | The Cogency of Kant’s Transcendental Arguments
kuczynski | We cannot form an image of a straight line between two points that is longer than some other line between those two points. | The Cogency of Kant’s Transcendental Arguments
kuczynski | For Kant, recourse to 'intuition' apprises us of what we can, and cannot, imagistically represent. | The Cogency of Kant’s Transcendental Arguments
kuczynski | In Kant’s view, these necessities are really of a psychological nature. | The Cogency of Kant’s Transcendental Arguments
kuczynski | Our 'forms of thought' are such that we must see the world (literally see it) as being such that, in it, parallel lines don’t meet, a straight line is the shortest distance between two points, two objects coupled with two other objects make four objects, and so on. | The Cogency of Kant’s Transcendental Arguments
kuczynski | So 'intuition', i.e. para-perceptual experience, shows us how we must sense-perceive the world. | The Cogency of Kant’s Transcendental Arguments
kuczynski | Given only that we are psychologically compelled to see the world as being such that, in it, thus and such is the case, it doesn’t follow that thus and such really is the case. | The Cogency of Kant’s Transcendental Arguments
kuczynski | These intuitions, he says, tell us about the 'phenomenal' world, not the 'noumenal' world. | The Cogency of Kant’s Transcendental Arguments
kuczynski | By Kant’s own definition, the 'phenomenal world' is just the world as it appears to us: it is how we see the world. | The Cogency of Kant’s Transcendental Arguments
kuczynski | The 'noumenal' world is the actual world. So by Kant’s own admission, these intuitions inform us only about how we see the world, not about how it is. | The Cogency of Kant’s Transcendental Arguments
kuczynski | Intuition begins precisely where conceptual analysis ends. So, on Kant’s view, intuitions don’t tell us anything about the spatiotemporal world and they don’t tell us anything about concepts. | The Cogency of Kant’s Transcendental Arguments
kuczynski | At most, intuition gives us only the knowledge that we are psychologically compelled to see the world as being such that, in it, 7+5=12 and parallels don’t meet. | The Cogency of Kant’s Transcendental Arguments
kuczynski | According to Kant, it does not register a fact about the structures of concepts (in the non-psychological sense). | The Cogency of Kant’s Transcendental Arguments
kuczynski | Further, it does not register a fact about our own psychology. Consequently, it must register some fact about the spatiotemporal world: all the other options have been exhausted. | The Cogency of Kant’s Transcendental Arguments
kuczynski | In any case, it becomes a truth that cannot be known in any way other than through examination of the external world. | The Cogency of Kant’s Transcendental Arguments
kuczynski | According to Kant, we can’t know anything about the noumenal world. Therefore we don’t have any knowledge of such truths, let alone a priori knowledge. | The Cogency of Kant’s Transcendental Arguments
kuczynski | When you learn through mental imagery that a straight line intersects a circle at no more than two points, you are learning something that holds entirely in virtue of facts about the structures of the concepts CIRCLE and LINE, and so forth. | The Cogency of Kant’s Transcendental Arguments
kuczynski | You are not learning something that holds in virtue of facts about our cognitive machinery. | The Cogency of Kant’s Transcendental Arguments
kuczynski | There is no reason why images should not mediate conceptual analysis. | The Cogency of Kant’s Transcendental Arguments
kuczynski | What led Kant astray was a failure to distinguish the different roles that mental imagery can play in the acquisition of knowledge. | The Cogency of Kant’s Transcendental Arguments
kuczynski | Kant noted that mental imagery is a kind of para-perception. He concluded from this that, when mental imagery is involved in the discovery of some truth, that truth is synthetic – like those truths that we use perception to discover. | The Cogency of Kant’s Transcendental Arguments
kuczynski | But surely the fact that a solid has to have a minimum of five sides is not synthetic. | The Cogency of Kant’s Transcendental Arguments
kuczynski | That proposition holds entirely in virtue of mathematics – in virtue of the structure of the concepts five and solid and so forth. | The Cogency of Kant’s Transcendental Arguments
kuczynski | It is true that I rely to some extent on imagery to learn this truth. But the imagery is a vehicle for conceptual analysis. | The Cogency of Kant’s Transcendental Arguments
kuczynski | We know a priori that the world, as we perceive it, is such that parallel lines don’t meet in it, triangles have interior angles adding up to 180°, and so on. | The Cogency of Kant’s Transcendental Arguments
kuczynski | The truth is that, on Kant’s account, even this knowledge turns to be a posteriori. | The Cogency of Kant’s Transcendental Arguments
kuczynski | As before, what led Kant astray was a failure to distinguish the two different epistemic roles that attention to our own mental processes can have. | The Cogency of Kant’s Transcendental Arguments
kuczynski | for example, that I want to find out whether I can imagine a 17-sided figure. Here I will, of course, generate mental imagery, and attend to the content of that imagery. | Kant’s Views on Synthetic and Analytic Propositions
kuczynski | In attending to my mental imagery, I am doing empirical work, and the truth that I discover (that I cannot visualize a 17-sided figure) is as a posteriori as Socrates was bald. | Kant’s Views on Synthetic and Analytic Propositions
kuczynski | For Kant, you learn that you can’t represent parallel lines as intersecting by noting that you cannot imagine it; you observe your own mental imagery. | Kant’s Views on Synthetic and Analytic Propositions
kuczynski | So you learn this putative fact about your representational powers through a kind of self-observation, and not through any kind of conceptual analysis. | Kant’s Views on Synthetic and Analytic Propositions
kuczynski | When Kant says that 'in the phenomenal world' parallels don’t meet, he is just saying that we cannot visually represent a world where parallels don’t meet. | Kant’s Views on Synthetic and Analytic Propositions
kuczynski | So our knowledge of this fact about the phenomenal world turns out to be a posteriori. | Kant’s Views on Synthetic and Analytic Propositions
kuczynski | You must understand that geometers do not derive their proofs from diagrams, although the expository approach makes it seem so. | Kant’s Views on Synthetic and Analytic Propositions
kuczynski | The cogency of the demonstration is independent of the diagram, whose only role is to make it easier to understand what is meant and to fix one’s attention. | Kant’s Views on Synthetic and Analytic Propositions
kuczynski | It is universal propositions, i.e. definitions and axioms and theorems which have already been demonstrated, that make up the reasoning, and they would sustain it even if there were no diagram. | Kant’s Views on Synthetic and Analytic Propositions
kuczynski | The fact that I use a diagram to prove the intermediate value theorem or the Pythagorean theorem doesn’t prove that either of those theorems is synthetic. | Kant’s Views on Synthetic and Analytic Propositions
kuczynski | Kant says we must see the world as being such that, in it, parallel don’t meet, and 7+5=12, and the area of a triangle ½ base×height, and so on; and he says that this due to our forms of thought. | Kant’s Views on Synthetic and Analytic Propositions
kuczynski | The necessity that attaches to the proposition that parallels don’t meet is epistemological, not psychological; transcendental, not empirical. | Kant’s Views on Synthetic and Analytic Propositions
kuczynski | That proposition describes a necessary precondition for our being able to have any experience at all of the world. | Kant’s Views on Synthetic and Analytic Propositions
kuczynski | In fact, he goes out of his way to make it clear that the necessity in question is non-psychological. | Kant’s Views on Synthetic and Analytic Propositions
kuczynski | We cannot coherently think of a world where triangles have two sides or where 2+2=3. | Kant’s Views on Synthetic and Analytic Propositions
kuczynski | That is because propositions like 2+2=3 and triangles have two sides are conceptually false and consequently draw a limit to what we can conceive of. | Kant’s Views on Synthetic and Analytic Propositions
kuczynski | Because we have certain cognitive machinery -- certain 'forms of thought' -- we are compelled to see the world in a certain way. | Kant’s Views on Synthetic and Analytic Propositions
kuczynski | He rightly wants to hold that propositions like 7+2=9 and the area of a (Euclidean) triangle is ½ the base×height are a priori. | Kant’s Views on Synthetic and Analytic Propositions
kuczynski | But he also says that we sense-perceive the world as being such that these propositions are true in it. | Kant’s Views on Synthetic and Analytic Propositions
kuczynski | So, on Kant’s view, these propositions say something about the spatiotemporal world. | Kant’s Views on Synthetic and Analytic Propositions
kuczynski | But, as Frege clearly stated, 7+2=9 doesn’t say anything about the spatio-temporal world; it doesn’t say that if you hold up two fingers and then hold up seven fingers, nine fingers are on display. | Kant’s Views on Synthetic and Analytic Propositions
kuczynski | The proposition a straight line is the shortest distance between two points says nothing at all about the spatio-temporal world; it defines a certain kind of space. | Kant’s Views on Synthetic and Analytic Propositions
kuczynski | Ultimately, there is one thing wrong with Kant’s view: his conception of analyticity is much too narrow. | Kant’s Views on Synthetic and Analytic Propositions
kuczynski | Everything else that is wrong about Kant’s position can be traced back to this one mistake. | Kant’s Views on Synthetic and Analytic Propositions
kuczynski | Kant grants analytic status only to propositions like (a) circles are round. | Kant’s Views on Synthetic and Analytic Propositions
kuczynski | He denies analytic status to propositions like (b) a circle is a class of points equidistant from a given point in a plane. | Kant’s Views on Synthetic and Analytic Propositions
kuczynski | A proposition is analytic exactly if one is guilty of incoherence in virtue of countenancing its negation. | Kant’s Views on Synthetic and Analytic Propositions
kuczynski | An analytic proposition is one that is made true by concepts. | Kant’s Views on Synthetic and Analytic Propositions
kuczynski | An analytically false proposition is one that is made false by concepts: a counter-conceptual proposition. | Kant’s Views on Synthetic and Analytic Propositions
kuczynski | What Kant refers to as 'synthetic a priori' truths are nothing other than what we are describing as hard analytic truths. | Kant’s Views on Synthetic and Analytic Propositions
kuczynski | What Kant refers to as analytic truths are what we are describing as easy analytic truths. | Kant’s Views on Synthetic and Analytic Propositions
kuczynski | Kant’s main concern is not a taxonomy of propositions. That is incidental to what he is doing. | Kant’s Views on Synthetic and Analytic Propositions
kuczynski | In Kant’s view, given the very fact that these things are true, we can deduce certain things about the structure of the world. | Kant’s Views on Synthetic and Analytic Propositions
kuczynski | Kant refers to those conditions as 'transcendental': they are the conditions that are necessarily satisfied by any world in which there are entities that have coherent, if not always accurate, thoughts and representations. | Kant’s Views on Synthetic and Analytic Propositions
kuczynski | A 'transcendental argument' is one having the form: given only that we have experiences of such and such a kind, it follows that the world must have thus and such features. | Kant’s Views on Synthetic and Analytic Propositions
kuczynski | Where Kant talks about 'synthetic a priori', we should instead talk of 'hard analytic.' | Kant’s Views on Synthetic and Analytic Propositions
kuczynski | This minor adjustment de-psychologizes Kant’s arguments, and enables them to go through. | Kant’s Views on Synthetic and Analytic Propositions
kuczynski | I will argue that there is an analytic connection – a hard analytic connection, but an analytic connection no less – between the proposition there exist beings who have the cognitive wherewithal to raise metaphysical questions and propositions concerning the structure of the world itself. | Kant’s Views on Synthetic and Analytic Propositions
kuczynski | One of Kant’s main objectives is to show that there are necessary connections among the events that we experience. | Kant’s Views on Synthetic and Analytic Propositions
kuczynski | Hume said that there are sequences and regularities of events, but that there is never any kind of internal connection holding among them. | Kant’s Views on Synthetic and Analytic Propositions
kuczynski | For event x to cause event y is just for the sequence consisting of x followed by y to instantiate a general regularity. | Kant’s Views on Synthetic and Analytic Propositions
kuczynski | It very much seems that pushing the button makes the computer turn on, that flipping the switch makes the light turn on. | Kant’s Views on Synthetic and Analytic Propositions
kuczynski | Imagine a world where there is no causation, where there are merely sequences of events. Such a world would comprise nothing enduring. | Kant’s Views on Synthetic and Analytic Propositions
kuczynski | This is because persistence is itself a causal notion. If something persists, there is ipso facto a causal sequence of some kind. | Kant’s Views on Synthetic and Analytic Propositions
kuczynski | So given only the fact that there are beings who have the ability to raise questions about anything, let alone about the necessary preconditions for experience, it follows that causal relations do not consist in mere sequences of events, but in internal relations of some kind. | Kant’s Views on Synthetic and Analytic Propositions
kuczynski | But notice that this transcendental argument turns on the existence of entirely analytic connections among propositions; no 'synthetic a priori' is involved. | Kant’s Views on Synthetic and Analytic Propositions
kuczynski | Given the very concept of a rational thought process, it follows that analytically there are causal relations among events. | Kant’s Views on Synthetic and Analytic Propositions
kuczynski | The justification for this inference would have to be empirical, not strictly logical. | Kant’s Use of Intuition
kuczynski | To have an empirical justification for that inference, we’d have to know that, in general, the future resembles the past. | Kant’s Use of Intuition
kuczynski | Knowledge of the past gives us a rational basis for beliefs about the future. | Kant’s Use of Intuition
kuczynski | But that is itself an empirical thesis, whose only possible justification lies in our past experience. | Kant’s Use of Intuition
kuczynski | We know that past futures resembled past pasts. | Kant’s Use of Intuition
kuczynski | How could that knowledge provide any rational basis for believing that future futures would resemble future pasts? | Kant’s Use of Intuition
kuczynski | For such a belief to have a rational basis, we’d need some assurance of the rationality of induction. | Kant’s Use of Intuition
kuczynski | There can be no such assurance unless there is an assurance that the future resembles the past. | Kant’s Use of Intuition
kuczynski | We are evidently caught in an unbreakable, vicious circle. | Kant’s Use of Intuition
kuczynski | Given two distinct states of affairs, one is not guilty of self-contradiction if one affirms the existence of the one but denies the existence of the other. | Kant’s Use of Intuition
kuczynski | Since causality holds between distinct states of affairs, there is no strictly logical connection between cause and effect. | Kant’s Use of Intuition
kuczynski | A causal connection consists in the operation of some force that demands the existence of the one event, given the existence of the other. | Kant’s Use of Intuition
kuczynski | If by 'force' is meant 'causal necessity', then that view is an innocuous triviality. | Kant’s Use of Intuition
kuczynski | If by 'force' is meant 'logical necessity', then that view is simply false, as we’ve just seen. | Kant’s Use of Intuition
kuczynski | If by 'force' is meant some constituent of the spatio-temporal world, then there can be no legitimate reason to believe in its existence. | Kant’s Use of Intuition
kuczynski | We perceive events and regularities among events. | Kant’s Use of Intuition
kuczynski | What we perceive gives us a basis for believing that certain kinds of events are likely to accompany certain other kinds of events. | Kant’s Use of Intuition
kuczynski | What we perceive does not give us any basis for perceiving that things altogether different from events are likely to accompany events. | Kant’s Use of Intuition
kuczynski | Therefore there can be no empirical basis for a belief in forces. | Kant’s Use of Intuition
kuczynski | If there is to be any rational justification for such a belief, it must be strictly logical or a priori. | Kant’s Use of Intuition
kuczynski | Being spatiotemporal entities, forces can only be known on empirical grounds. | Kant’s Use of Intuition
kuczynski | There are no necessary, and therefore no causal, relations between distinct events, or at least none we could have any legitimate reason to believe in. | Kant’s Use of Intuition
kuczynski | Hume says that given any two distinct events, there is no strictly logical connection between them. | Kant’s Use of Intuition
kuczynski | Logical relations hold, not between states of affairs, but between pieces of information. | Kant’s Use of Intuition
kuczynski | If A and B are distinct events, then there is no strictly logical connection between the proposition A occurs and the proposition B occurs. | Kant’s Use of Intuition
kuczynski | Depending on how we describe A and B, a proposition affirming the existence of the one might entail one affirming the existence of the other. | Kant’s Use of Intuition
kuczynski | For Hume’s argument to go through, we must find some non-tendentious way of describing A and B. | Kant’s Use of Intuition
kuczynski | I believe that (*) and (**) leave out the relevant data, and that the relation between A and B is in no way contingent. | Kant’s Use of Intuition
kuczynski | Are there instantaneous states of affairs? Is there anything that exists in three, but not in four, dimensions? | Kant’s Use of Intuition
kuczynski | It is now generally held, such talk is elliptical for talk of arbitrarily short, non-instantaneous states of affairs. | Kant’s Use of Intuition
kuczynski | There can be no movement, and hence no velocity, at an instant. | Kant’s Use of Intuition
kuczynski | There are no strictly non-dynamic spatiotemporal properties – no property such that possession of it doesn’t involve movement. | Kant’s Use of Intuition
kuczynski | A corollary is that there is no complete description of any state of affairs that does not say what happens over a stretch of time. | Kant’s Use of Intuition
kuczynski | An argument exactly similar to the one just given establishes that no state of affairs occupies an unextended point. | Kant’s Use of Intuition
kuczynski | The pushing of the button is not one event, but billions upon billions, as it supervenes on innumerable displacements mass-energy. | Kant’s Use of Intuition
kuczynski | To give a complete description of the pushing of the button, we’d have to describe each of these displacements. | Kant’s Use of Intuition
kuczynski | For there are obviously no instantaneous displacements of anything. | Kant’s Use of Intuition
kuczynski | A complete description of the occurrences during int would include a description of occurrences happening after int. | Kant’s Use of Intuition
kuczynski | Any description of any given event would include a description of subsequent events. | Kant’s Use of Intuition
kuczynski | That by itself would shatter Hume’s view that there is no necessary connection between any event and any subsequent event. | Kant’s Use of Intuition
kuczynski | There is a strictly logical connection between (complete descriptions of) any two events that are connected by a continuous series of events. | Kant’s Use of Intuition
kuczynski | Our belief that there is no such connection would have to be seen as a projection into the external world of cavities in our belief-system. | Kant’s Use of Intuition
kuczynski | Hume’s position, then, involves two blunders. First, it fails to recognize that logical relations hold among pieces of information, not among states of affairs. | Kant’s Use of Intuition
kuczynski | Second, it assumes that there are infinitely short, and infinitely small, states of affairs. | Kant’s Use of Intuition
kuczynski | Given any property F, it is nonsense – false on conceptual, as opposed to empirical grounds -- to speak of F’s being instantiated in spatial or temporal point. | Kant’s Use of Intuition
kuczynski | It would be meaningless to say that x was a square, but then to deny x any of the associated causal or kinematic properties. | Kant’s Use of Intuition
kuczynski | For x to have the shape of a square is for x to be a dynamic system of a certain kind. | Kant’s Use of Intuition
kuczynski | To say that x has endured for a certain period of time, or has moved from A to B, is to make a causal claim. | Kant’s Use of Intuition
kuczynski | It follows that no state of affairs can be described by a non-causal proposition, making it impossible even to articulate Hume’s puzzle regarding causality in a way that doesn’t presuppose its existence. | Kant’s Use of Intuition
kuczynski | Talk of instants and points is itself talk of arbitrarily small, or arbitrarily short, occurrences. | Kant’s Use of Intuition
kuczynski | It isn’t logically or epistemologically possible to start with empty points and instants, then fill them (so to speak) with states of affairs, and then build spatio-temporally extended events out of those. | Kant’s Use of Intuition
kuczynski | Instead, we must start with events and, on that basis, work our way backwards towards points and instants. | Kant’s Use of Intuition
kuczynski | To say that space-time consists of points and instants, as opposed to granules of non-null size, is to say that events have an 'analogue', and not a 'digital', structure. | Kant’s Use of Intuition
kuczynski | The belief that space and time are composed of points strikes me as a projection into the physical world of this fact about language. | Kant’s Use of Intuition
kuczynski | The belief that temporally extended events must be built out of non-extended events is sheer anthropomorphism. | Kant’s Use of Intuition
kuczynski | Once it is granted that discourse about points and instants is elliptical for discourse about extended (but arbitrarily small or short) events, the problem ceases to be how to establish a necessary connection between distinct events. | Kant’s Use of Intuition
kuczynski | Hume assumes that there are entirely non-causal descriptions of states of affairs. | Kant’s Use of Intuition
kuczynski | What Hume doesn’t realize is that each of the statements involved in describing that scenario is replete with causal content. | Kant’s Use of Intuition
kuczynski | The persistence of each of those objects is itself a causal fact. | Kant’s Use of Intuition
kuczynski | The problem with this viewpoint is that the concept of a disruption has only a psychological or pragmatic significance. | Kant’s Use of Intuition
kuczynski | Hume’s puzzle thus presupposes a highly anthropomorphic conception of causality: there are causal relations only where there are events that are psychologically disruptive. | Critique of Kant’s System
kuczynski | But such a conception is no basis for any analysis of causality. | Critique of Kant’s System
kuczynski | Hume’s analysis of the concept of “force” is beyond reproach. | Critique of Kant’s System
kuczynski | But that analysis is moot since, contrary to what Hume held, we don’t need forces to connect cause and effect. | Critique of Kant’s System
kuczynski | Hume is also right that there is no necessity other than logical necessity. | Critique of Kant’s System
kuczynski | There is obviously some principled connection between the pushing of the button and the ascent of the elevator. | Critique of Kant’s System
kuczynski | Attempts to reduce such connections to constant conjunctions have not faired very well, though they persist to this day. | Critique of Kant’s System
kuczynski | The apparent absence of a logical connection between the two is an artifact of the impoverished descriptions of those events employed by philosophers attempting to demonstrate the irrationality of induction. | Critique of Kant’s System
kuczynski | Finally, those descriptions never mention the fact that the data that motivates (and, so I will argue, justifies) our inductive beliefs is never, in the final analysis, propositional. | Critique of Kant’s System
kuczynski | That data is perceptual, and thus doesn’t decompose into permutation-friendly entities like propositions and sentences, and is thus less likely to reinforce the false view that space-time is comparably permutation-friendly and thus incapable of hosting events that have internal connections to one another. | Critique of Kant’s System
kuczynski | E precedes E* in time exactly if there is some (possible) causal process, e.g. a light ray, beginning with E and ending with E*. | Critique of Kant’s System
kuczynski | This suggests that temporal order is itself a causal notion. | Critique of Kant’s System
kuczynski | Obviously there is no atemporal description of anything spatiotemporal. | Critique of Kant’s System
kuczynski | It follows that any description of anything spatiotemporal is itself a causal description. | Critique of Kant’s System
kuczynski | If that is right, then it is not possible even to articulate Hume’s objections to causality. | Critique of Kant’s System
kuczynski | Recognition of (O) was forced on us by empirical discoveries, in particular by Relativity Theory. | Critique of Kant’s System
kuczynski | If there is no possible causal connection between E and E*, then it makes little sense to say that they have any spatial or temporal position with respect to each other. | Critique of Kant’s System
kuczynski | Hume famously argued that there are no strictly logical grounds for the view that every event must have a cause – that there are no ex nihilo events. | Critique of Kant’s System
kuczynski | His argument is simple: propositions alleging uncaused events, e.g. the newspaper spontaneously combusted, are not self-contradictory. | Critique of Kant’s System
kuczynski | Since what is not self-contradictory is possible, ex nihilo creation and change is therefore possible. | Critique of Kant’s System
kuczynski | I believe that Hume’s analysis of induction is bound up with his belief in the logical possibility of ex nihilo creation. | Critique of Kant’s System
kuczynski | As we’ve just seen, the latter belief is itself an application of his digital conception of spatio-temporal reality. | Critique of Kant’s System
kuczynski | In a moment, I will argue that, when we appropriately de-digitize this conception, Hume’s analysis of induction crumbles. | Critique of Kant’s System
kuczynski | Hume’s conception of causality involves a falsely “digital” conception of spatiotemporal reality. | Critique of Kant’s System
kuczynski | In his view, each region of space and of time is a distinct box that can be filled with a state of affairs. | Critique of Kant’s System
kuczynski | If the contents of one of those boxes are replaced or annihilated, that has no ramifications as far as the other boxes are concerned. | Critique of Kant’s System
kuczynski | The spatio-temporal world is thus, almost by definition, completely permutation-friendly. | Critique of Kant’s System
kuczynski | Given this viewpoint, it becomes trivially impossible to establish any causal relations between distinct events. | Critique of Kant’s System
kuczynski | Persistence is a causal phenomenon. Spatio-temporal continuity is a form of persistence. Consequently, continuity is a causal phenomenon. | Critique of Kant’s System
kuczynski | By definition, continuity has no place in a digital system. | Critique of Kant’s System
kuczynski | Hume’s “demonstration” that there are no causal relations (or that such relations reduce to regularities) is thus an artifact of his assumption that space and time have a structure comparable to that of an array of digits. | Critique of Kant’s System
kuczynski | But that assumption question-beggingly presupposes the very conception of causality that Hume is trying to defend. | Critique of Kant’s System
kuczynski | Every case of persistence is a case of continuous change. What we describe as changes are actually changes in the manner of change. | Critique of Kant’s System
kuczynski | Hume’s tendency to digitize blinds him to the logical basis of induction, since induction, as I will now argue, consists in positing exactly those continuities that would “de-digitize” our description of reality. | Critique of Kant’s System
kuczynski | Hume is, of course, entirely right to say that (a) every swan I’ve ever seen is white does not entail (b) the next swan I will see will be white. | Critique of Kant’s System
kuczynski | But this does not have the consequences that Hume believes it to. | Critique of Kant’s System
kuczynski | Strictly speaking, it is not our knowledge of (a)-(a4), or of any other propositions, that motivates and justifies our inductions: what does so is the sensory data that those propositions so schematically model. | Critique of Kant’s System
kuczynski | Hume’s argument is supposed to concern the data that warrants (or is supposed to legitimate) our inductions. In actuality, his argument concerns certain models of that data. | Critique of Kant’s System
kuczynski | Ultimately, our perceptions are, as Hume himself insists, our only source of information about the spatiotemporal world. | Critique of Kant’s System
kuczynski | Hume is therefore right to say that, like all other empirical beliefs, our inductions can be justified only by sensory experience. | Critique of Kant’s System
kuczynski | Where Hume goes astray is in his failure to register a crucial fact about sensory information. | Critique of Kant’s System
kuczynski | The content of a perception doesn’t decompose in the same way as the content of the propositions that we would typically use to report those perceptions. | Critique of Kant’s System
kuczynski | What is reported by such a proposition is not the content of the perception itself, but rather a judgment about the content of that perception. | Critique of Kant’s System
kuczynski | Describing such propositions as “data” is like identifying a court’s finding of guilty with the actuality of guilt. | Critique of Kant’s System
kuczynski | There is thus a sense in which (a)-(a4) are not the data that motivate (or legitimate) belief in (b). (a)-(a4) are not data, but meta-data. | Critique of Kant’s System
kuczynski | The logical basis for induction is precisely what is lost in the process of digitization involved in embodying one’s knowledge in (a)-(a4). | Critique of Kant’s System
kuczynski | When you see a white swan, what your perception gives you is a continuous state of affairs. | Critique of Kant’s System
kuczynski | The data that actually motivates your belief in (b) is embodied in analogue representations. | Critique of Kant’s System
kuczynski | What is given to you in such representations is never a discrete state but, at most, a relatively discrete state of affairs. | Critique of Kant’s System
kuczynski | Any given perception presents a continuous reality. It is a datum that what is given to you in a given perception is spatio-temporally co-located with what is given to you in other perceptions. | Critique of Kant’s System
kuczynski | This, in turn, means that they are connected by a continuous series of events: no ex nihilo changes are involved. | Critique of Kant’s System
kuczynski | We’ve seen that, for an arbitrary event E, if D is a complete description of E, then D does entail the occurrence of events that, by ordinary standards, are distinct from E. | Critique of Kant’s System
kuczynski | To make such an inference is, implicitly, to hold that the scenario described by (A) is a logical consequence of a complete description of any of the events in its neighborhood. | Critique of Kant’s System
kuczynski | Hume holds that counter-inductive beliefs like (A) involve unusual, but otherwise acceptable, permutations of objects and properties. | Critique of Kant’s System
kuczynski | But states of affairs cannot be understood in combinatorial terms; they don’t consist of discrete, replaceable parts. | Critique of Kant’s System
kuczynski | Hume rightly said that (A) and (C) are compatible propositions. But this says very little about either the states of affairs corresponding to those propositions or about the non-digital (analogue) data schematically represented by those propositions. | Critique of Kant’s System
kuczynski | We must distinguish between digital- and analogue-compatibility. We must distinguish what is permissible, given propositions like (A) and (C), from what is permissible given the analogue data of which (A) and (C) are schematic representations. | Critique of Kant’s System
kuczynski | When we reconsider the data leading to belief in propositions like (C), i.e. when we consider (C*), we find that all kinds of discontinuities and perforations of the space-time manifold would be involved in a belief in (A). | Critique of Kant’s System
kuczynski | When this constraint is taken into account, the logical permissibility of inferences like (A) becomes more questionable than Hume makes it out to be. | Critique of Kant’s System
kuczynski | Hume’s conception of induction involves a similar fallacy. When we say that (A) is merely counter-inductive, we are excluding two types of information. | Critique of Kant’s System
kuczynski | Just as Bob has instinctive beliefs about why he loves Mary, so we have instinctive beliefs about why we think we believe that the pan is hot. | Critique of Kant’s System
kuczynski | Those meta-beliefs can no more be regarded as authoritative than can Bob’s stated answer about why loves Mary. | Critique of Kant’s System
kuczynski | Part of the reason one believes that future fires will be hot is that past fires were hot. | Critique of Kant’s System
kuczynski | But the reason one gives weight to that fact is to be found in other pieces of knowledge in one’s cognitive background. | Critique of Kant’s System
kuczynski | Mediating between the present and the past are continuous processes. Temporal and spatial relations involve continuity, persistence, and causation. | Critique of Kant’s System
kuczynski | Where there is ex nihilo creation, there is an absence of causality and, therefore, of spatio-temporal relatedness. | Critique of Kant’s System
kuczynski | Counter-inductive hypotheses implicitly involve such breaches. Therefore such hypotheses are not to be countenanced. | Critique of Kant’s System
kuczynski | Hume wrongly saw our theories as created by associative mechanisms. But it is our theories about our theories, not the theories themselves, that are created by such mechanisms. | Critique of Kant’s System
kuczynski | What Hume falsely said about our thinking -- that it consists entirely of a kind of Freudian free-association -- is true of Hume’s thinking about our thinking. | Critique of Kant’s System
kuczynski | When you try to articulate the grounds for an inductive belief of yours, you tend to access the relevant data by way of associative mechanisms. | Critique of Kant’s System
kuczynski | From Hume to the present, our theories about induction have only been elaborations of those association-based answers. | Critique of Kant’s System
kuczynski | But this doesn’t mean that there is no logical basis for induction; it corresponds only to the trivial fact that we can’t recover the logical basis of our inductions from degenerate representations of that basis -- representations that highlight what is emotionally (or affectively) significant, and leave everything else out. | Hume’s Puzzles on Induction and Causality
kuczynski | Our experience gives us smooth curves. Our sentential representations of our experience (“past fires have been hot”) give us isolated points that are compatible with infinitely many different curves. | Hume’s Puzzles on Induction and Causality
kuczynski | On this basis, Hume and others say that there is no logical basis for induction. But that doesn’t follow, since the justification for our inductions lies in the smooth curves of experience, and not in the jagged edges of our descriptions thereof. | Hume’s Puzzles on Induction and Causality
kuczynski | Hume’s views on induction are thus artifacts of his own methods. Given an association-driven review of our inductive-practices, it is indeed going to be impossible to recover the logic that underlies them. | Hume’s Puzzles on Induction and Causality
kuczynski | By their very nature, such associations leave out emotionally sterile, but logically crucial, connecting information. Hume’s empiricism as a whole is a study of artifacts. | Hume’s Puzzles on Induction and Causality
kuczynski | Since, in his system, knowledge is identified with sequences of images, it follows trivially that reality must be understood in terms of the associative-mechanisms governing such sequences. | Hume’s Puzzles on Induction and Causality
kuczynski | Being affect-driven, such sequences track disruptions, skipping over the emotionally neutral periods that intervene. A reality seen through the lens of such processes will inevitably consist of singularities – as breakdowns in the conditions that permit causal and inductive explanation. | Hume’s Puzzles on Induction and Causality
kuczynski | A given proposition (e.g. John is eating chicken) has a structure that is not remotely comparable to that of the corresponding reality or to that of the perceptual data through which we learn about that reality. | Hume’s Puzzles on Induction and Causality
kuczynski | Causation is bound up with continuity – with the very features of reality that resist the sort of digitization associated with sentences. | Hume’s Puzzles on Induction and Causality
kuczynski | Finally, the viability of induction is bound up with the existence of causal mechanisms linking states of affairs. All of this is obvious. | Hume’s Puzzles on Induction and Causality
kuczynski | It therefore follows from what is obvious that the logical relations holding between two propositions will not typically do justice to the relations of necessitation (causal and explanatory) holding between the corresponding states of affairs. | Hume’s Puzzles on Induction and Causality
kuczynski | There must, I would therefore suggest, be a purely emotional basis our failure to see this, and for our subsequent willingness to accept Hume’s otherwise unpalatable nihilism regarding causation and induction. | Hume’s Puzzles on Induction and Causality
kuczynski | Throughout its history, logic has been identified with the logic of sentences or of para-sentential entities like propositions. But, as we’ve seen, not all data is embodied in sentences. | Hume’s Puzzles on Induction and Causality
kuczynski | The existence of digital representations, like sentences, is parasitic on that of non-digital representations. So ultimately data is always embodied in media that are not only not sentences, but lack the defining feature of sentences: namely, decomposability into minimal units of significance. | Hume’s Puzzles on Induction and Causality
kuczynski | The concept of information is inseparable from concepts like entailment and compatibility. So obviously logical relations hold among analogue-information, and are not confined to sentences. | Hume’s Puzzles on Induction and Causality
kuczynski | Sentence-logic is therefore but one kind of logic and, in light of the derivative nature of digital information, is a fairly superficial kind of logic. | Hume’s Puzzles on Induction and Causality
kuczynski | The identification of sentence-logic with logic as a whole is so obviously false that we must wonder what induced us to acquiesce to it. | Hume’s Puzzles on Induction and Causality
kuczynski | I believe that acquiescence to be motivated, at least in part, by a wish to rationalize the tendentious and emotion-driven conception of reality embodied in Hume’s empiricism. | Hume’s Puzzles on Induction and Causality
kuczynski | Our acceptance of Hume’s analyses of causation and induction, despite their obvious discordance with everything we believe, is a special case of that acquiescence and is thus an expression of that same wish. | Hume’s Puzzles on Induction and Causality
kuczynski | The compartmentalizations embodied in a digital conception of reality give legitimacy to associative mechanisms that have an emotional basis and in which we therefore have an emotional investment. | Hume’s Puzzles on Induction and Causality
kuczynski | Given the non-digital structure of the spatiotemporal world, there are purely logical limits as to what hypotheses can be coherently entertained. | Hume’s Puzzles on Induction and Causality
kuczynski | So far as we think otherwise, it is because we are wrongly identifying that data that motivate our hypotheses with the propositions that we use to model that data. | Hume’s Puzzles on Induction and Causality
kuczynski | Logical relations are generally seen as holding between digital representations – between sentences and para-sentential entities like propositions. We have seen that logical relations hold among non-digital, and therefore non-propositional, data. | Hume’s Puzzles on Induction and Causality
kuczynski | The principles governing these relations have yet to be articulated, and a priori resist articulation within a digital medium like language. | Hume’s Puzzles on Induction and Causality
kuczynski | Correct inductions are analogue-logically valid inferences from the data embodied in our sense-perceptions. Counter-inductive inferences are analogue-logically invalid inferences from that same data. | Hume’s Puzzles on Induction and Causality
kuczynski | If you know anything about the external world, such knowledge is derived from your sense-perceptions. But the proposition that there is no external world is logically consistent with the existence of those perceptions. | Hume’s Puzzles on Induction and Causality
kuczynski | We beg the question if we respond by saying that our perceptions probabilify our empirical beliefs, while not giving them 100% certainty. | Hume’s Puzzles on Induction and Causality
kuczynski | To know that x is probable given y, we must know that events relevantly like x accompany events relevantly like y. Unless we already have some assurance that our perceptions are veridical, we cannot know that events of the appropriate types ever accompany our perceptions. | Hume’s Puzzles on Induction and Causality
kuczynski | The skeptic’s position involves the view – actually, it is the view – that our perceptions are ex nihilo creations, having little or no continuity with events in their vicinity. | Hume’s Puzzles on Induction and Causality
kuczynski | Because such discontinuities are incompatible with the datum that our perceptions are temporally co-located, it is illogical, and not merely counter-intuitive, to believe in their existence. | Hume’s Puzzles on Induction and Causality
kuczynski | So far as philosophers believe otherwise, it is because they are conflating the data given to us in experience – the data constitutive of experience, in fact – with our propositional schematizations of it. | Hume’s Puzzles on Induction and Causality
kuczynski | It is a datum that our mental events are temporally co-located. It is a datum that the itch I felt a moment ago is on the same time-line as the twinge I am feeling right now. | Hume’s Puzzles on Induction and Causality
kuczynski | We saw earlier temporal succession is a causal notion. Where there is a fissure or discontinuity, there is no temporal succession, unless some other continuous sequence of events steps into the breach. | Hume’s Puzzles on Induction and Causality
kuczynski | Given two completely disconnected events, it cannot significantly be said that one precedes the other. Of course, events that are relatively disconnected can be related in time. | Hume’s Puzzles on Induction and Causality
kuczynski | The world of the skeptic is discontinuous on a number of levels. Sleep obliterates consciousness. So if the skeptic is right, then every awakening is a case of ex nihilo creation. | Hume’s Puzzles on Induction and Causality
kuczynski | But sleep and unconsciousness would not be the only sources of ex nihilo creation in the skeptic’s world, since many discontinuities are internal to the state of wakefulness. | Hume’s Puzzles on Induction and Causality
kuczynski | A skeptic must say that each resumption of visual experience is a case of creation ex nihilo and that each cessation of it is a case of annihilation. | Hume’s Puzzles on Induction and Causality
kuczynski | It is a datum that our experiences are temporally co-located. For reasons we’ve discussed, it follows that any two experiences are connected by a continuous series of states. | Hume’s Puzzles on Induction and Causality
kuczynski | Therefore the discontinuities described a moment ago are merely apparent. If those discontinuities reflected the ultimate structure of reality, then there would be no temporal connection between the experiences of the person who wakes and the one falls asleep. | Hume’s Puzzles on Induction and Causality
kuczynski | Obviously the mental realm cannot furnish the requisite continuities, and must therefore be supplemented by a non-mental realm. If the occurrences in that non-mental realm are to furnish the needed continuities, they must have a character at least approximating to that suggested by our perceptions. | Hume’s Puzzles on Induction and Causality
kuczynski | We saw earlier that counter-inductive hypotheses are logically incompatible with the data embodied in our experiences, even though they are compatible with the more obvious propositionalizations of that data. | Hume’s Puzzles on Induction and Causality
kuczynski | There are thus strictly logical reasons why, given that data, it is not an option to countenance counter-inductive hypotheses as to the nature of the non-mental events that establish the continuities presupposed by our experience. | Hume’s Puzzles on Induction and Causality
kuczynski | So there are strictly logical reasons why we must say that it is the activity of rocks and trees, muons and quarks, and not of a capricious demon, that accounts for the existence and specific character of our mental lives. | Hume’s Puzzles on Induction and Causality
kuczynski | In conclusion, supposing that our cognitive processing of the information embodied in our sense-experience is logically valid, the datum that our experiences follow one another in time is logically inconsistent with the skeptical proposal that reality is exhausted by one’s mental states. | Hume’s Puzzles on Induction and Causality
kuczynski | It must be kept in mind that the logical validity just mentioned is analogue-logical validity. It must also be kept in mind that any propositionalization of our experience is a model of our experience, and not the experience itself. | Hume’s Puzzles on Induction and Causality
kuczynski | Consequently, if we attempt to justify (or dismiss) inductive beliefs on the basis of such propositionalizations, we are committing the same fallacy as a juror who gives more weight to a defense-lawyer’s theory than to a video-recording of the defendant committing the crime. | Hume’s Puzzles on Induction and Causality
kuczynski | It is often said that a “brain in a vat” could have exactly the experiences we do. I agree. It is inferred from this that our perceptions needn’t be veridical to any degree. I do not think that this inference is warranted. | Hume’s Puzzles on Induction and Causality
kuczynski | Suppose that a scientist, or even a series of random occurrences, is stimulating brain B in such a way that B has a flow of experience qualitatively just like yours. B would have many false beliefs. | Hume’s Puzzles on Induction and Causality
kuczynski | But we must remember that the behavior of the scientist (or of the random series of events) would have the formal properties supposed by B to be had by the external world. | Hume’s Puzzles on Induction and Causality
kuczynski | Suppose we fool someone into believing that they are in Hawaii by placing outside their window a giant computer monitor showing scenes from that tropical paradise. That person does indeed have the wrong beliefs. | Hume’s Puzzles on Induction and Causality
kuczynski | But he has those wrong beliefs only because the images on the monitor have much the same structure as the sort of beach-side revelry that he wrongly believes to be occurring outside his window. | Hume’s Puzzles on Induction and Causality
kuczynski | That person therefore has a great deal of formal knowledge about what is going on outside his window, even though his interpretation of this formal data is in error. | Hume’s Puzzles on Induction and Causality
kuczynski | It is true that, from a social or pragmatic viewpoint, that person’s beliefs are quite wrong. But that person -- call him Smith – knows much more about the occurrences outside his window than our best physicist know about the sub-atomic world. | Hume’s Puzzles on Induction and Causality
kuczynski | Our belief that Smith has no knowledge embodies our tendency to think in emotional, rather than strictly epistemological, terms. | Hume’s Puzzles on Induction and Causality
kuczynski | Smith has a gift for abstract thought. He is thus able to give a formal description of what he sees, and can leave out the emotional or pragmatic pseudo-information that would constitute almost the entirety of an ordinary person’s discourse. | Hume’s Puzzles on Induction and Causality
kuczynski | So Smith’s description doesn’t contain emotional extrapolations like: “my prayers are answered! I no longer have to suffer the torment of those long Chicago winters!” | Hume’s Puzzles on Induction and Causality
kuczynski | Smith’s description is a conservative and accurate description of what he actually sees – certain patterns of mass-energy displacements. | Hume’s Puzzles on Induction and Causality
kuczynski | In terms of richness and accuracy, Smith’s description of what is going on outside his window would dwarf our best descriptions of the sub-atomic (or even the sub-molecular) world. | Hume’s Puzzles on Induction and Causality
kuczynski | Systematic deception always involves making certain allowances for the formal structure of the realities being misrepresented. | Critique of Hume’s Digital Conception of Reality
kuczynski | Smith is, in absolute terms, quite well-informed about the occurrences outside his window. | Critique of Hume’s Digital Conception of Reality
kuczynski | envatted brain B would, in absolute terms, be quite well-informed as to the nature of the reality lying beyond its experiences. | Critique of Hume’s Digital Conception of Reality
kuczynski | B would be quite wrong about everything that had any emotional significance. | Critique of Hume’s Digital Conception of Reality
kuczynski | B knows incalculably more about the external world than we do about anything beneath the molecular level. | Critique of Hume’s Digital Conception of Reality
kuczynski | The presumption that B knows nothing about the external world incorporates an irrelevant pragmatic or emotional perspective. | Critique of Hume’s Digital Conception of Reality
kuczynski | Causation involves continuity. Given how rich and articulated our sensory experience is, and given that every aspect of it must arise by way of continual changes from external realities, it is logically out of the question that the correspondence between our perceptions and external reality should be confined to our bodily surfaces. | Critique of Hume’s Digital Conception of Reality
kuczynski | once it is settled how the world is in one small area of space-time, the rest of the world is locked in. | Critique of Hume’s Digital Conception of Reality
kuczynski | No knowledge of the external world, or even of the internal (psychological) world, is analytic. | Critique of Hume’s Digital Conception of Reality
kuczynski | As we saw in our discussion of Kant, all spatio-temporal knowledge is empirical and therefore synthetic a posteriori. | Critique of Hume’s Digital Conception of Reality
kuczynski | Moritz Schlick’s slogan 'no synthetic a priori' is a simple truth. | Critique of Hume’s Digital Conception of Reality
kuczynski | Given our experiences, it analytically follows that there is an external world having a character not entirely at odds with that suggested by our perceptions. | Critique of Hume’s Digital Conception of Reality
kuczynski | Hume’s counter-intuitive analysis of causation presupposes that spatio-temporal relations are non-causal, and thus that space and time are empty vessels whose properties are wholly invariant with respect to those of the events populating them. | Critique of Hume’s Digital Conception of Reality
kuczynski | Once it is acknowledged that temporal co-locatedness must be understood in causal terms, we have no trouble finding a purely logical foundation for our inductive practices. | Critique of Hume’s Digital Conception of Reality
kuczynski | I believe that the system we have just described is a de-psychologized, de-idealized version of that described by Kant. | Critique of Hume’s Digital Conception of Reality
kuczynski | Where Kant talks of psychological necessity, I speak of logical necessity. | Critique of Hume’s Digital Conception of Reality
kuczynski | Kant’s austere conception of logical truth required him to regard as psychological what were in fact strictly logical necessities. | Critique of Hume’s Digital Conception of Reality
kuczynski | Under Kant’s system, all those beliefs could be correct only if relativized to 'phenomena' (perceptions, appearances) and denied any connection with 'noumena' (non-psychological realities). | Critique of Hume’s Digital Conception of Reality
kuczynski | once it is granted that not all analytic truth is trivial, and that there are deep analytic connections, there ceases to be any need for this psychologocization of epistemology. | Critique of Hume’s Digital Conception of Reality
kuczynski | Our knowledge of the external world is not merely probable. Our beliefs concerning the external world analogue-logically valid inferences from the data of experience. | Critique of Hume’s Digital Conception of Reality
kuczynski | We saw that space and time are themselves causal notions. | Critique of Hume’s Digital Conception of Reality
kuczynski | This implies, I believe, that if there is a possible causal connection between E and E*, then such a connection actually exists. | Critique of Hume’s Digital Conception of Reality
kuczynski | My life post-dates Caesar’s because there are causal processes beginning with the last events of his life and ending with the first events of mine. | Critique of Hume’s Digital Conception of Reality
kuczynski | If we choose to say that (#) could be true in the absence of any connection between E and E*, then we are saying E’s preceding E* is a bare fact – a primitive. | Critique of Hume’s Digital Conception of Reality
kuczynski | This is, of course, what Hume would have said. But if we say that the truth of (#) is a primitive, then the truth of (O) and (P) would acquire an accidental status. | Critique of Hume’s Digital Conception of Reality
kuczynski | To avoid the theoretical arbitrariness associated with such a position, we must say that (#) is true exactly if there is some actual causal process beginning with E and ending with E*. | Critique of Hume’s Digital Conception of Reality
kuczynski | For E to come before E* is for E is to be a cause of E*. For those events to be simultaneous is for neither to be a cause of the other. | Critique of Hume’s Digital Conception of Reality
kuczynski | Obviously the concept of causality is embedded within the concept of an event. | Critique of Hume’s Digital Conception of Reality
kuczynski | there are no instantaneous events: if E1 has no causal relation to E2, then (unless they are simultaneous) they are ipso facto not parts of the same event. | Critique of Hume’s Digital Conception of Reality
kuczynski | Since E and E* are simultaneous exactly if there is no causal connection between them, we may say that an instant in time is the class of all events such that there is no causal connection between any two of them. | Critique of Hume’s Digital Conception of Reality
kuczynski | Given this, we must follow Whitehead and Russell in identifying instants with classes of non-interacting states of affairs. | Critique of Hume’s Digital Conception of Reality
kuczynski | For two distinct events to be in different places, it is sufficient that neither be a cause of the other. | Critique of Hume’s Digital Conception of Reality
kuczynski | Whenever we talk about 'the same place', we must identify the relevant framework. | Critique of Hume’s Digital Conception of Reality
kuczynski | Since it is conceptually (as opposed to merely empirically) impossible for simultaneous events to have any effect on each other, there is no barrier to saying that absence of a causal relation constitutes difference of place. | Critique of Hume’s Digital Conception of Reality
