kuczynski | If we take the notions of event and cause as primitive – as we are inclined to do anyway – we can successfully construct the framework that Hume, taking the concepts of space and time as primitive, failed to construct. | Critique of Hume’s Digital Conception of Reality
kuczynski | I believe that Hume’s positivism has cast long and dark philosophical shadows. | Critique of Hume’s Digital Conception of Reality
kuczynski | It is commonplace in epistemology that the cause of a belief must be distinguished from the justification for it. | Critique of Hume’s Digital Conception of Reality
kuczynski | But thanks to the influence of Hume’s thought, this distinction has been over-extended, resulting in some implausible and sterile views concerning the acquisition of scientific knowledge. | Critique of Hume’s Digital Conception of Reality
kuczynski | A number of thinkers – Kant, Chomsky, and Jerry Fodor among them – respond to this by saying, in effect, that Newton innately knew the physical principles that he appeared to derive from experience. | Critique of Hume’s Digital Conception of Reality
kuczynski | The Kant-Chomsky view is given by the statement: 'our innate cognitive structures almost encode a knowledge of all the theories we will ever produce (experience is needed only to activate those structures).' | Critique of Hume’s Digital Conception of Reality
kuczynski | But the truth is that Kant and Chomsky are really operating within a Humean framework, and are presupposing the truth of Hume’s epistemological nihilism. | Critique of Hume’s Digital Conception of Reality
kuczynski | Kant and Chomsky are really saying that, leaving aside the knowledge acquired directly from sense-perception, you can’t acquire any knowledge of the external world. | Critique of Hume’s Digital Conception of Reality
kuczynski | When it seems as though we are learning about the external world, we aren’t: we are merely becoming aware of what we already know. | Critique of Hume’s Digital Conception of Reality
kuczynski | Chomsky’s answer is: 'evolution.' It is thanks to 500 million years of evolution that our innate cognitive structure is so closely hewed to the objective structure of the world. | Critique of Hume’s Digital Conception of Reality
kuczynski | Kant’s answer is that we don’t have any knowledge, or even any experience, of any genuinely objective (non-psychological) reality. | Critique of Hume’s Digital Conception of Reality
kuczynski | The world that we experience is a world of our own making. The innate so-called knowledge we’ve been speaking of has no objective counterpart, and thus isn’t knowledge at all. | Critique of Hume’s Digital Conception of Reality
kuczynski | It seems a datum that we have knowledge of the external world. | Critique of Hume’s Digital Conception of Reality
kuczynski | Chomsky’s nativism is really a way of passing the buck from the individual onto evolution. But it is left unexplained how evolution can discharge that function. | Critique of Hume’s Digital Conception of Reality
kuczynski | As we noted earlier, Chomsky’s rationalism is just Humean irrationalism displaced from the individual onto the species. | Critique of Hume’s Digital Conception of Reality
kuczynski | there is always a difference between justification and causation. | Analogue-Digital Distinction and Induction
kuczynski | Obviously facts about evolution do explain why we are able to know so much. | Analogue-Digital Distinction and Induction
kuczynski | Surely we have evolution to thank for the fact that we know more than jellyfish. | Analogue-Digital Distinction and Induction
kuczynski | But the explanation for this is that evolution gave us the right cognitive tools. | Analogue-Digital Distinction and Induction
kuczynski | Empiricism – at least Hume’s version of it -- eliminates induction as a source of knowledge. | Analogue-Digital Distinction and Induction
kuczynski | This, in turn, makes all non-analytic knowledge impossible. | Analogue-Digital Distinction and Induction
kuczynski | Consequently, evolution has to take over the epistemological role previously filled by God. | Analogue-Digital Distinction and Induction
kuczynski | Evolution becomes an all purpose explanatory stop-gap, much as God used to be. | Analogue-Digital Distinction and Induction
kuczynski | I do not deny that there is innate knowledge. | Analogue-Digital Distinction and Induction
kuczynski | It seems an empirical fact that many animals (including human beings) have innate knowledge relating to, among other things, food and safety. | Analogue-Digital Distinction and Induction
kuczynski | It is quite another to say, with Kant and Chomsky, that we are, in effect, born knowing Newtonian mechanics. | Analogue-Digital Distinction and Induction
kuczynski | If the evolutionary (pre-established-harmony) story were the right one, we would have no epistemic plasticity at all. | Analogue-Digital Distinction and Induction
kuczynski | We are not subject to such instantaneous paralyses. | Analogue-Digital Distinction and Induction
kuczynski | Obviously our biological structure imposes strict limits on our intellectual development. | Analogue-Digital Distinction and Induction
kuczynski | No matter how unusual the circumstances, and no matter how little evolutionary precedent those circumstances have, our ability to make at least minimally adequate inductive inferences never vanishes. | Analogue-Digital Distinction and Induction
kuczynski | In the final analysis, Chomsky and Kant say that, give or take a few nuances, we are born knowing everything we will ever know. | Analogue-Digital Distinction and Induction
kuczynski | Experience merely activates latent knowledge; its role is purely causal, never rational. | Analogue-Digital Distinction and Induction
kuczynski | We can avoid this less than satisfying position if we see induction as a logical process. | Analogue-Digital Distinction and Induction
kuczynski | Our theoretical knowledge is obviously not limited to the stock of ideas that we are born with. | Analogue-Digital Distinction and Induction
kuczynski | Nobody was born knowing the inverse square law. | Analogue-Digital Distinction and Induction
kuczynski | Induction involves intelligence. | Analogue-Digital Distinction and Induction
kuczynski | Humean empiricism is inconsistent with this; and so, despite first appearances, is Chomskyan-Kantian nativism. | Analogue-Digital Distinction and Induction
kuczynski | In rendering induction defunct, Hume makes it impossible to acquire any knowledge that transcends the deliverances of one’s senses at a given moment. | Analogue-Digital Distinction and Induction
kuczynski | The result is that all knowledge must be innate. | Analogue-Digital Distinction and Induction
kuczynski | Since we can’t learn anything, we must be born knowing everything that we ever come to know. | Analogue-Digital Distinction and Induction
kuczynski | On our view, it isn’t necessary to posit vast stock-piles of innate scientific knowledge. | Analogue-Digital Distinction and Induction
kuczynski | To be sure, there is innate knowledge. | Analogue-Digital Distinction and Induction
kuczynski | The cognitive machinery through which we acquire empirical knowledge would seem to embody knowledge of conceptual truths. | Analogue-Digital Distinction and Induction
kuczynski | But the kind of knowledge that enables one to delineate the logical consequences of the information at one’s disposal is entirely analytic and non-empirical. | Analogue-Digital Distinction and Induction
kuczynski | It therefore isn’t necessary to agree with the implausible view of Kant and Chomsky that we innately know Newtonian mechanics. | Analogue-Digital Distinction and Induction
kuczynski | Fortunately, it isn’t necessary to posit so much innate empirical knowledge to explain our success at forming theories. | Analogue-Digital Distinction and Induction
kuczynski | If we accept Hume’s views on induction, then this is exactly what we are forced to say. | Analogue-Digital Distinction and Induction
kuczynski | On Hume’s view, no inductive belief has any justification. | Analogue-Digital Distinction and Induction
kuczynski | Theories are never justified and the thought-processes that produce them are never rational. | Analogue-Digital Distinction and Induction
kuczynski | On Hume’s view, nothing causes anything. | Analogue-Digital Distinction and Induction
kuczynski | Instead of causation, there are only regularities. | Analogue-Digital Distinction and Induction
kuczynski | To explain an event is thus to show only that it is an instance of some regularity. | Analogue-Digital Distinction and Induction
kuczynski | One problem with this conception of explanation is that it very much seems not to be true of psychological explanation. | Analogue-Digital Distinction and Induction
kuczynski | To explain a person’s conduct is to make that conduct intelligible; it is to show that it makes sense. | Analogue-Digital Distinction and Induction
kuczynski | To explain why Jim went to the bank is not to show that his doing so is an instance of some regularity. | Analogue-Digital Distinction and Induction
kuczynski | It is to show why, given the operative conditions, it makes sense that Jim went to the bank. | Analogue-Digital Distinction and Induction
kuczynski | People can understand their own conduct, at least within limits. | Analogue-Digital Distinction and Induction
kuczynski | Your having this knowledge doesn’t involve your subsuming your behavior under general regularities. | Analogue-Digital Distinction and Induction
kuczynski | It is fairly obvious that Hume’s conception of explanation is inapplicable to psychological phenomena. | Analogue-Digital Distinction and Induction
kuczynski | In fact, practically every investigator has, to some degrees or other, accepted Hume’s conception of physical explanation. | Analogue-Digital Distinction and Induction
kuczynski | According to the latter group of thinkers – I am thinking of Dilthey in particular -- there is a fundamental difference between psychological and non-psychological explanation. | Analogue-Digital Distinction and Induction
kuczynski | Psychological explanation, it is said, consists in entering into the subjectivity of the person or persons studied. | Analogue-Digital Distinction and Induction
kuczynski | By contrast, non-psychological explanation consists in discerning comprehensive regularities. | Analogue-Digital Distinction and Induction
kuczynski | But the word 'explanation' is not ambiguous – it is not like the word 'bank'. | Analogue-Digital Distinction and Induction
kuczynski | Differences in subject-matter tend to be reflected in differences in methodology. | Analogue-Digital Distinction and Induction
kuczynski | In both cases, you are showing why it makes sense that some event occurred. | Analogue-Digital Distinction and Induction
kuczynski | There is thus some one concept associated with the word 'explanation.' | Analogue-Digital Distinction and Induction
kuczynski | Typically, 'explanation' connotes a gain in objectivity – an assimilation of the singular to the universal. | Analogue-Digital Distinction and Induction
kuczynski | According to the view proposed in the present paper, explanation always consists in showing a logical and principled relation between initial and terminal conditions. | Analogue-Digital Distinction and Induction
kuczynski | There is no need to reduce psychology to statistics or to create some artificial division between the human and the natural sciences. | Analogue-Digital Distinction and Induction
kuczynski | The debate between Dilthey and Hempel is a tempest in a teapot, being entirely internal to Hume’s erroneous purely statistical conception of explanation. | Analogue-Digital Distinction and Induction
kuczynski | The conception of explanation demanded by Hume’s analysis has another unwholesome feature. | Analogue-Digital Distinction and Induction
kuczynski | Exceptionless though they are, these regularities are obviously explanatorily irrelevant. | Analogue-Digital Distinction and Induction
kuczynski | Hume’s answer is that the right properties can be selected on purely statistical grounds. | Analogue-Digital Distinction and Induction
kuczynski | But the very concept of a property is explanatorily pregnant, and cannot be used in this context without begging the question. | Analogue-Digital Distinction and Induction
kuczynski | This exposes a certain circularity in Hume’s reasoning. | Analogue-Digital Distinction and Induction
kuczynski | Properties like copper, wet, soap, cotton – the properties in terms of which we understand the world – are not inductively or causally innocent. | Analogue-Digital Distinction and Induction
kuczynski | The moment you describe something as copper, you are making various inductive and causal claims. | Analogue-Digital Distinction and Induction
kuczynski | So Hume’s arguments regarding causation and induction become tautologically false if they are stated in terms of any of the properties that we ordinarily use. | Analogue-Digital Distinction and Induction
kuczynski | If Hume’s position regarding induction is stated in terms of such expressions, it becomes trivially false; if not, then it becomes a triviality, from which nothing can be inferred as to the efficacy of induction. | Consequences of the Proposed System
kuczynski | One lesson of this discussion is that, contrary to what Hume alleges, explanation is not a purely statistical concept, since the terms in which the relevant statistics are articulated will inevitably be explanatorily committed. | Consequences of the Proposed System
kuczynski | Carl Hempel famously proposed the so-called deductive-nomological conception of explanation. Event E is explained exactly if L conjoined with C1…Cn entails E, where L is a proposition describing a natural law and C1…Cn are propositions describing the conditions operative in the relevant area of space-time. | Consequences of the Proposed System
kuczynski | If he means a principled, i.e. an explanatorily significant, relationship, then his analysis is an empty tautology, as it amounts to this: E is explained exactly if there is an explanatorily significant regularity of which E is an instance. | Consequences of the Proposed System
kuczynski | If his analysis is to be anything more than a tautology, Hempel must use the term “law” to denote a mere regularity. | Consequences of the Proposed System
kuczynski | But even if we side-step this last problem, the Hempel-Hume analysis still fails. The physical world is characterized by many regularities (leaving aside the explanatorily sterile ones just discussed). | Consequences of the Proposed System
kuczynski | But presumably these regularities express, and do not constitute, the existence of principled relationships. | Consequences of the Proposed System
kuczynski | Our intuitive feeling is that this regularity is a consequence, and not the substance, of the some kind of principled relationship holding among the properties involved. | Consequences of the Proposed System
kuczynski | If Hume’s analysis is right, this presumption is wrong. But if this presumption is wrong, then the existence of this regularity becomes a coincidence of unspeakable proportions. | Consequences of the Proposed System
kuczynski | For reasons already discussed, this problem vanishes if, instead of vainly and counter-intuitively trying to understand the concept of explanation in purely statistical terms, we give weight to our pre-Humean belief that explanations expose principled relationships. | Consequences of the Proposed System
kuczynski | Our inhibitions to doing so vanish once we acknowledge the two main points of this paper, namely: propositions are merely models, and bad ones at that, of the analogue data given in sense-perception; and, second, space and time are not causally innocuous concepts. | Consequences of the Proposed System
kuczynski | Until the early 1960’s, the philosophy of science was dominated by the following viewpoint. Science organizes empirical data. Hypotheses are pictures or models of facts. | Consequences of the Proposed System
kuczynski | Their correspondence, or lack thereof, with the facts can be decided rather straightforwardly – in essentially the same way that the veracity of a drawing can be decided. | Consequences of the Proposed System
kuczynski | In a word, the results of scientific endeavor are embodied in general statements that either do or do not correspond with the facts, and what the facts are is to no degree a function of how we think or of how we represent those thoughts to one another. | Consequences of the Proposed System
kuczynski | In his book the Structure of Scientific Revolutions, Thomas Kuhn proposed a different picture. First of all, we must distinguish “normal” from “revolutionary” science. | Consequences of the Proposed System
kuczynski | Revolutionary science consists in producing a new “paradigm”: a new framework within which research is to be conducted. | Consequences of the Proposed System
kuczynski | A paradigm is not exactly a theory; it would be better described as a kind of meta-theory – a general outlook within which the construction of theories takes place. | Consequences of the Proposed System
kuczynski | Normal science consists in fleshing out a paradigm. “Facts” and “data” are internal to paradigms. | Consequences of the Proposed System
kuczynski | Given that facts must be understood in terms of the categories picked out by expressions like “cotton”, “wood”, “square” – i.e. given that facts must be understood in terms of the categories corresponding to our expressions – the paradigm-internal nature of facts reflects the truth that empirical expressions are theoretically committed, and thus presuppose paradigms. | Consequences of the Proposed System
kuczynski | In Kuhn’s view, since facts are all internal to paradigms, empirical facts cannot warrant the acceptance or rejection of a given paradigm. | Consequences of the Proposed System
kuczynski | Paradigms are to be accepted or rejected entirely on non-epistemic grounds. Einstein’s paradigm is not better than Newton’s, or even than Aristotle’s. | Consequences of the Proposed System
kuczynski | It is just preferable on non-epistemic grounds – grounds comparable to those on which Bach’s music is preferable to Telemann’s. | Consequences of the Proposed System
kuczynski | Given a paradigm, says Kuhn, data can be marshaled for or against a hypothesis. So the picture proposed by Kuhn’s predecessors – scientific activity is regulated by a knowledge of what the relevant data are – may be true of activity within a given paradigm. | Consequences of the Proposed System
kuczynski | But it is not true of the activity on the basis of which paradigms themselves are accepted or rejected. | Consequences of the Proposed System
kuczynski | If Kuhn is right, then once a paradigm is accepted, all the important issues have been decided in advance. | Consequences of the Proposed System
kuczynski | Thus, if Kuhn is right, we must reject the presumption that theories are true or false. Kuhn’s view is therefore a kind of recrudescence of Kant’s a priorism. | Consequences of the Proposed System
kuczynski | Kuhn is generally seen as a great iconoclast – as somebody who dared to challenge the supremacy of a positivist, Humean conception of scientific explanation. | Consequences of the Proposed System
kuczynski | But Kuhn’s work actually supports the very positivism that he aspires to overthrow. Kuhn is saying: given that the positivist picture is wrong, it follows that scientific endeavor isn’t a rational process. | Consequences of the Proposed System
kuczynski | In other words, Kuhn is saying that, if you want to hold onto the idea that science is rational, you must be a positivist. | Consequences of the Proposed System
kuczynski | Kuhn says that there are no “neutral givens”, i.e. no theory-neutral data; that exponents of different paradigm “live in different worlds” (in an evidential, not a literal, sense). | Consequences of the Proposed System
kuczynski | It is only within paradigms that one theory can be empirically preferable to another; and that, with that qualification, no theory is better than any other. | Consequences of the Proposed System
kuczynski | Einstein’s physical theories are closer to the truth than Aristotle’s. Some theories are better than others; and theory T is better than theory T* exactly if T is more accurate than T*. | Consequences of the Proposed System
kuczynski | Freud was either right to say that there is unconscious mental activity, or he was wrong to say this. The question is one of fact, not of our choice of paradigms, or forms of representation, or anything of the sort. | Consequences of the Proposed System
kuczynski | Scientific growth is regulated by canons of rationality. There are principled ways of settling disputes between advocates of different paradigms. | Consequences of the Proposed System
kuczynski | Although some data is theory-internal, there is still a great deal of theory-neutral data. Einsteinians and Newtonians use the same thermometers. | Consequences of the Proposed System
kuczynski | Freudians and anti-Freudians can both agree that patient X has just attempted suicide. There is no denying that Kuhn’s work is deeply important and that at some level what he is saying is true. | Consequences of the Proposed System
kuczynski | But so far as his contentions are accurate, Kuhn has obviously over-stated them. The debate between Kuhn and the positivists parallels the debate regarding psychological explanation between Dilthey and Hempel. | Consequences of the Proposed System
kuczynski | On the one hand, we have a sterile and implausible descriptivist conception of scientific explanation: theories are statistical generalizations; to explain an event is to subsume it under such a generalization. | Consequences of the Proposed System
kuczynski | On the other hand, we have a seemingly bold, but actually timid, irrationalism: scientific theories aren’t true or false; rationality has only a marginal role in scientific endeavor. | Consequences of the Proposed System
kuczynski | Kuhn’s and Dilthey’s theories are really ways of arguing for the theories with which they are supposed to be opposed. The villain here is Hume’s conception of induction. | Consequences of the Proposed System
kuczynski | Induction doesn’t consist in irrational leaps into the unknown. It consists in analogue-logical inferences from perceptual data. | Consequences of the Proposed System
kuczynski | Inductive explanation consists in finding principled relationships among data. The relevant logical principles are embodied in analogue-content, and thus resist articulation within the more familiar digital-propositional logic on which we focus in philosophical discourse. | Consequences of the Proposed System
kuczynski | The reaction had by Hume – and, following him, Mill, Carnap, Hempel, and many others –was to say that induction is unprincipled, and that theories are can be justified only in relative sense. | Consequences of the Proposed System
kuczynski | There are obviously causal relations between facts about bodies and facts about minds. But it is a mystery how psychological states could arise in a world of atoms and molecules. | Consequences of the Proposed System
kuczynski | It is a mystery why a kick in the shins should lead to feelings of any sort, or why a desire for chocolate ice-cream should lead to movements in one’s arms and legs. | Consequences of the Proposed System
kuczynski | Hume said that there is no mystery. All explanation, and thus all de-mystification, consists simply in discovering invariable concomitances. | Consequences of the Proposed System
kuczynski | Mind-body relations are obviously characterized by such concomitances. Given the very fact that such regularities exist, there is nothing to explain: there is no mind-body problem. | Consequences of the Proposed System
kuczynski | This viewpoint, it can safely be said, is quite artificial. Hume’s statistical conception of explanation is false; and so, therefore, is his solution to the mind-body problem. | Consequences of the Proposed System
kuczynski | The sphere of conscious mental activity is characterized by discontinuities – by a pattern of ex nihilo creations and subsequent annihilations. | Consequences of the Proposed System
kuczynski | Freud proposed that conscious psychological episodes are installments in sequences of events large portions of which are constituted by unconscious psychological activity. | Consequences of the Proposed System
kuczynski | If this is right, then psychological reality is continuous and law-governed, notwithstanding the erratic and discontinuous nature of activity within the sphere of consciousness. | Consequences of the Proposed System
kuczynski | It has been said on a number of occasions that theories positing the existence of unconscious mental activity are categorically false. | Consequences of the Proposed System
kuczynski | But given what we have seen in regards to causation and explanation, Freud’s analysis would seem to be an entirely logical, if not positively mandatory, application of sound scientific methodology. | Consequences of the Proposed System
kuczynski | Whether Freud’s theories are to be accepted is a strictly empirical question that is to be resolved wholly on the basis of the accuracy of the specific contents of those theories. | Consequences of the Proposed System

kuczynski | Psychosis is a mental impairment that is precipitated and sustained by a break with external reality | Neurosis vs Psychosis
kuczynski | Neurosis is a mental impairment that is precipitated and sustained by a break with one's emotional condition | Neurosis vs Psychosis
kuczynski | The neurotic flees from his emotions; the psychotic flees from reality | Neurosis vs Psychosis
kuczynski | Neurosis may lead to a blunting of one's awareness of certain aspects of external reality, but any such loss of awareness is subordinate to loss of awareness of one's emotional condition | Neurosis vs Psychosis
kuczynski | Similarly, psychosis may lead to a disruption of one's understanding of one's emotional condition, but any such disturbance is subordinate to the psychotic's loss of awareness of external reality | Neurosis vs Psychosis
kuczynski | Though symmetrical in the just-described respects, neurosis and psychosis are ultimately not mirror images of each other | Neurosis vs Psychosis
kuczynski | This is a consequence of the fact that psychosis involves a partial regression to a condition of psychological infancy, the operative term being 'partial' | Neurosis vs Psychosis
kuczynski | An infant is not psychotic | Neurosis vs Psychosis
kuczynski | The infant's psychological condition is integrated and healthy, even though the infant's understanding of reality is even more subjective, and therefore more projective, than that of the adult psychotic | Neurosis vs Psychosis
kuczynski | The adult psychotic, on the other hand, has the psychological architecture of an adult as well as that of an infant | Neurosis vs Psychosis
kuczynski | Therefore, the psychotic has two entirely distinct and incompatible ways of processing information | Neurosis vs Psychosis
kuczynski | He does not have a unified value system; he does not have a unified understanding of how to implement such values as he has; and his psychological condition is therefore therefore one of utter disarray | Neurosis vs Psychosis
kuczynski | And therein lies the peculiarly damaging nature of psychosis. Nothing comparable holds of the neurotic | Neurosis vs Psychosis
kuczynski | Neurosis is about containing threats to one's integration by repressing them | Neurosis vs Psychosis
kuczynski | To repress a conceit is not to bury one's knowledge of it in the unconscious; it is rather to bury one's knowledge of its emotional valence in the unconscious | Neurosis vs Psychosis
kuczynski | As a result, the neurotic's condition is one of relatively unimpaired intellectual awareness | Neurosis vs Psychosis
kuczynski | In fact, in some circumscribed ways, it may be even be a condition heightened intellectual awareness, owing to the fact that the neurotic is likely to compensate for his impoverished emotional state by hypercathecting his intellect | Neurosis vs Psychosis
kuczynski | Moreover, by stripping the contents of consciousness of their emotional valences, the neurotic guarantees that he has a hyper-stable, though also an ossified psyche | Neurosis vs Psychosis
kuczynski | The essence of psychosis is an inability to organize consciously experienced emotion; the essence of neurosis is an inability to have consciously experienced emotion | Neurosis vs Psychosis
kuczynski | The former is paralyzed by a superabundance of emotion, the latter by lack of it | Neurosis vs Psychosis

kuczynski | To know that an argument is formally correct, it is necessary to know only its syntactic structure, not its semantics | Formal Correctness
kuczynski | Syntactic form is completely different from geometrical form—morphology is a property of tokens, not types | Syntax vs Morphology
kuczynski | Two morphologically identical expression-tokens can have completely different syntactic forms | Morphology-Syntax Gap
kuczynski | What syntactic structure an expression has is a function of what it means—of its semantics | Syntax and Semantics
kuczynski | Given any sentence-type, there is no limit to how different in respect of morphology two tokens of it can be | Morphological Variation

kuczynski | Syntactic form has little or nothing to do with morphological form | Form Distinction
kuczynski | A being responding to form in the morphological sense is not thereby responding to form in the syntactic sense | Morphological vs Syntactic Response

kuczynski | Given any language rich enough to mediate human thoughts, syntactic structures will fail to track logical forms | Syntax-Logic Gap
kuczynski | The hypothesis that Smith is following rule R is confirmationally different from the hypothesis that he merely accords with R | Rule-Following Confirmation
kuczynski | If somebody grasps arithmetic, that knowledge will be expressed in ways other than symbolic manipulation | Arithmetic Knowledge

kuczynski | If calculators have semantic knowledge, it is completely ex nihilo with none of the moorings we would expect | Calculator Semantic Knowledge

kuczynski | Wittgenstein's point that any course of action can be made to accord with a rule does not have the ramifications often thought | Wittgenstein on Rules
kuczynski | The hypothesis that I follow add one is confirmationally different from the hypothesis that I follow add a million then subtract 999,999 | Rule Confirmation

kuczynski | The information through which one computes meanings is replete with conceptual structure | Pre-Semantic Structure

kuczynski | Such judgments are at least sometimes principled, but the principles often fall outside introspective awareness | Sub-Personal Principles
kuczynski | Given that piece X has such musical properties, it follows conceptually, not inductively, that it is rock 'n' roll | Conceptual Classification
kuczynski | Jones may never articulate the grounds for judgments as efficiently as he operates with the term, but the concept still has analytic structure | Implicit Analytic Structure
kuczynski | What is sub-personally known vastly exceeds what is personally known along certain dimensions | Sub-Personal Knowledge
kuczynski | Information in the sub-personal realm is transmitted and processed exponentially faster than at the personal level | Sub-Personal Processing
kuczynski | If we act like things that follow rules involving concepts like c-command, there is good evidence we really do follow those rules | Rule-Following Evidence
kuczynski | The hypothesis that I follow add one is confirmationally different from the hypothesis that I merely accord with it | Following vs According

kuczynski | If calculators have semantic knowledge, it is completely ex nihilo with none of the moorings we would expect | Ex Nihilo Knowledge

kuczynski | The rule-following to which calculator behavior corresponds can be off-loaded onto the person behind the calculator | Off-Loading to Users

kuczynski | What people think is part of what fixes what expressions mean | Thought Fixes Meaning

kuczynski | If Mentalese symbols' meanings aren't fixed by thought, it becomes questionable whether they deserve to be called symbols | Questionable Symbols
kuczynski | Scientific progress consists in making principled extensions of concepts, but SCT may have over-extended the concept of a symbol | Over-Extension
kuczynski | The word language is ambiguous, but not in the way Lewis thinks | Language Ambiguity
kuczynski | Linguistic meaning never refers to function-theoretic pairings, and language never refers to a set of such pairings | Against Function-Theoretic Meaning
kuczynski | Lewis is right that language is ambiguous but wrong about the nature of the ambiguity | Lewis Partly Right

kuczynski | Saussure's distinction between langue and parole is legitimate | Saussure Vindicated

kuczynski | To grasp a proposition, I must grasp its constituents—this cannot be done through other propositions without vicious regress | Grasp and Regress
kuczynski | It must be assumed that ultimately the contents of our mental representations of propositional constituents are non-propositional | Non-Propositional Content
kuczynski | The statement propositions are classes containing their logical consequences is viciously circular | Logical Consequence View Circular

kuczynski | If we identify propositions with classes of consequences, equivalent propositions would be indistinguishable | Equivalence Problem

kuczynski | PWS cannot distinguish between 1+1=2 and triangles have three sides since they're true in the same worlds | PWS Conflation

kuczynski | Smith and this instance of pain don't have isolable spatiotemporal significances, but they do have isolable significances | Abstract Significances
kuczynski | Our view is that those significances are not spatiotemporally discrete entities—an independently plausible view | Non-Spatiotemporal Meaning

Kuczynski | Let us suppose, if only for argument’s sake, that such phenomena exist. Kuczynski | Once an object moves that first inch, all of the remaining inches are a foregone conclusion. | Philosophical and Ethical Questions
Kuczynski | All position is relative position. Therefore, changes in position are changes in relative position. | Philosophical and Ethical Questions
Kuczynski | For there is nothing in relation to which x s position can change. Nor can it be said that x exists at a given time or for a certain period of time. | Philosophical and Ethical Questions
Kuczynski | So when it is said that xi must move   inch in order to move an inch, and   inch in order to move a   inch, and so on, the point being made is this: (1) Given any displacement d, no matter how small, if the universe were better populated and otherwise more heterogeneous than it currently is but all other factors, in including the laws of nature, were held constant, it would be possible for there to be an even more smaller displacement d*. | Philosophical and Ethical Questions
Kuczynski | The more objects there are in a given universe, the greater the number of distances with which a given distance can be compared. The more such distances, the higher degree of precision with which a given distance can be meaningfully described. | Philosophical and Ethical Questions

Kuczynski | Proof can't self-certifyThis Theorem: Formality needs something beyond formality (meta-formal judgment). | Natural Language and Metalinguistic Hierarchies
Kuczynski | The pattern: * Gödel showed that formal systems need something outside themselves (semantic truth) * This theorem shows that formalism itself needs something outside itself (meta-formal judgment) * At each level, closure is impossible | Natural Language and Metalinguistic Hierarchies
Kuczynski | If rationality cannot be exhaustively formalized, what is rationality? If formalism requires non-formal resources, what are those resources? | Natural Language and Metalinguistic Hierarchies
Kuczynski | Natural languages are not a single recursive expression-class. They are hierarchies of such classes, stratified by self-reference levels. | Natural Language and Metalinguistic Hierarchies
Kuczynski | English = E₀ ∪ E₁ ∪ E₂ ∪ ... ∪ Eₙ ∪ ... Where: E₀ = Base recursive expression-class (doesn't mention 'English' or reference the language itself) | Natural Language and Metalinguistic Hierarchies
Kuczynski | This is Tarski's hierarchy for truth, but applied to language-as-object rather than just truth predicates. | Natural Language and Metalinguistic Hierarchies
Kuczynski | An expression truly belongs to a language L only if: 1. It's syntactically well-formed in L (built from L-vocabulary using L-rules) AND 2. It has semantic content in L (refers, has truth-conditions, means something) | Natural Language and Metalinguistic Hierarchies
Kuczynski | The hierarchy isn't just adding new vocabulary. It's semantically activating syntax that was already present but inert. | Natural Language and Metalinguistic Hierarchies
Kuczynski | Generative Eₙ₊₁ from Eₙ requires: * Semantic judgment (which strings get semantically activated?) * Metalinguistic creativity (introducing new referring terms) * Recognition of what constitutes 'talking about Eₙ as a totality' | Natural Language and Metalinguistic Hierarchies
Kuczynski | The series (E₀, E₁, E₂, ...) is not recursive. This is a direct application of the main theorem: recursivity cannot recursively enumerate itself. | Natural Language and Metalinguistic Hierarchies
Kuczynski | The Fundamental Point Even if we grant that (E₀, E₁, E₂, ...) is recursive, the language in which we describe that recursivity cannot itself be part of the series. | Natural Language and Metalinguistic Hierarchies
Kuczynski | Why Logicism Failed The logicist project (Frege, Russell) aimed to reduce all mathematics to logic. But they never had a clear conception of what 'logic' meant. | Natural Language and Metalinguistic Hierarchies
Kuczynski | Logic = Whatever System I'm Using (second-order logic with comprehension) * Problem: This is set theory in disguise, not logic | Natural Language and Metalinguistic Hierarchies
Kuczynski | This theorem shows: They couldn't even recursively enumerate the candidate formal systems, so even the restricted project can't be carried out algorithmically. | Natural Language and Metalinguistic Hierarchies
Kuczynski | No language can fully characterize itself * Parallel to: No formal system can fully characterize formal systems * Parallel to: Recursivity cannot recursively define itself | Natural Language and Metalinguistic Hierarchies
Kuczynski | Semantic grounding requires stepping outside * To give meaning to self-referential expressions, you need a metalevel * The metalevel itself requires a meta-metalevel * No final level of complete semantic closure | Natural Language and Metalinguistic Hierarchies
Kuczynski | The limits of language cannot be stated in language * Wittgenstein's ladder, but mathematically proven * Any statement of language's limits requires transcending those limits | Natural Language and Metalinguistic Hierarchies
Kuczynski | Conclusion We've shown that natural languages cannot be single recursive expression-classes. They must be hierarchies where: 1. Each level can refer to lower levels but not to itself 2. Syntax can be recursive at each level, but semantics requires non-recursive operations 3. The generating series itself is not recursive 4. Any attempt to characterize the hierarchy requires stepping outside it | Breaking the Stranglehold of Recursion
Kuczynski | The pattern is clear: Formalism cannot formalize its own boundaries. Recursion cannot enumerate itself. Language cannot linguistically characterize itself. | Breaking the Stranglehold of Recursion
Kuczynski | And as we'll see in Part IV, this pattern has infected multiple domains through the false equation Rational = Recursive, creating pseudo-problems that dissolve once we recognize non-recursive rationality. | Breaking the Stranglehold of Recursion
Kuczynski | The central claim: a false equation has infected philosophy, linguistics, cognitive science, and AI for over a century: RATIONAL = RECURSIVE | Breaking the Stranglehold of Recursion
Kuczynski | This equation says: to make sense of something is to recursively formalize it. To understand X is to give recursive rules for X. Reasoning is following algorithms. Knowledge is what can be recursively justified. | Breaking the Stranglehold of Recursion
Kuczynski | Semantics is not recursive. This is the fundamental architecture of natural language. | Breaking the Stranglehold of Recursion
Kuczynski | But meaning is not always compositional. You can use recursive syntax to build expressions whose semantics is non-recursive. | Breaking the Stranglehold of Recursion
Kuczynski | Semantically: * ✗ Has no referent in E₀ (E₀ doesn't exist as a completed object yet) * Only acquires meaning in E₁ (when E₀ becomes an object) * ✗ Meaning is not a compositional function of part-meanings * ✗ Non-recursive | Breaking the Stranglehold of Recursion
Kuczynski | Standard semantic compositionality principle: ⟦α β⟧ = f(⟦α⟧, ⟦β⟧) The meaning of a complex expression is a function of: * The meanings of its parts * How they're syntactically combined | Breaking the Stranglehold of Recursion
Kuczynski | But "the totality of expressions of English" violates this: * Each word has meaning in E₀ * Syntactic combination is well-formed * ✗ But the whole phrase has no referent in E₀ | Breaking the Stranglehold of Recursion
Kuczynski | This is not a bug. It's a feature of how natural language handles self-reference and metalinguistic expressions. | Breaking the Stranglehold of Recursion
Kuczynski | Why Compositionality Fails The General Principle For any natural language L with syntax S: You can use S to construct expressions whose: * Syntax is recursive (built by S's rules) * Semantics is non-recursive (meaning/reference not compositionally determined) | Breaking the Stranglehold of Recursion
Kuczynski | Formal semantics (Montague, Davidson, etc.) tries to give recursive semantic rules: * ⟦John runs⟧ = runs(john) * ⟦every man runs⟧ = ∀x[man(x) → runs(x)] This works for fragments of language where: * All primitives have fixed referents * Compositionality holds * No metalinguistic expressions * No naming/ostension * No self-reference | Breaking the Stranglehold of Recursion
Kuczynski | These are semantically essential but non-recursive. Linguistic Competence: UG + X Vindicating and Extending Chomsky Chomsky discovered that language has a recursive core: Universal Grammar (UG). But UG is not the whole story. | Breaking the Stranglehold of Recursion
Kuczynski | The naming/ostension capacity is irreducibly non-recursive because: Depends on demonstrative reference: * "That child there" requires perceptual grounding * Perception is not a linguistic/recursive operation | Breaking the Stranglehold of Recursion
Kuczynski | The Empirical Observation The meta-semantic capacity is empirically obvious: Every time someone: * Names their newborn child * Coins a new term for a discovered phenomenon * Creates a neologism for a new concept * Points and says "that thing there" | Breaking the Stranglehold of Recursion
Kuczynski | So: Neural networks not being recursive is a feature, not a bug. They might actually be better suited to approximate the non-recursive aspects of language than rule-based systems ever were. | Breaking the Stranglehold of Recursion
Kuczynski | Neural/Statistical Approaches Are Vindicated: * Not inferior to symbolic/recursive approaches * Actually better suited for non-recursive aspects of language * The future of CL, not a temporary expedient | Breaking the Stranglehold of Recursion
