Kuczynski | The Upshot Pattern recognition doesn't just describe discovery—it enables and explains it. Discoveries happen when someone recognizes a pattern others missed. | Domains Liberated from Recursive Tyranny
Kuczynski | Detect when statements contradict * Ensure coherence via formal rulesWhat actually happens: * Recognizing contradiction = seeing that statements can't both be true * Pattern recognition of incompatibilityEvidence: * Contradictions can be hidden (people miss them) * Expert logicians better at spotting (trained pattern recognition) * Sometimes requires insight to see inconsistency | The Positive Program
Kuczynski | Mathematical case: * Proving system consistency: requires seeing that no derivation could yield P and ¬P * Often requires insight (recognizing structural properties) * Example: Euclidean geometry consistent (give model—you recognize axioms all satisfied)Upshot: Consistency-checking is pattern recognition of compatibility. | The Positive Program
Kuczynski | Function 3: Making Predictions / Drawing Consequences What deduction was supposed to do: * Derive conclusions from premises * Follow logical rules mechanicallyWhat actually happens: * Drawing consequences = recognizing what follows from whatScientific case: * Newton's laws + initial conditions → predict positions * Looks deductive. | The Positive Program
Kuczynski | All pattern recognitionUpshot: Drawing consequences is recognizing what-follows-from-what patterns. | The Positive Program
Kuczynski | Function 4: Providing Justification / Giving Reasons What deduction was supposed to do: * Justify beliefs by deriving from other beliefs * Build chains of justificationWhat actually happens: * Justification = showing why conclusion is warranted * This is showing the evidential/explanatory patternEvidence: * Good justifications make you SEE why conclusion follows * Bad justifications feel mechanical, don't provide insight * Understanding justification ≠ checking steps mechanicallyUpshot: Justification is making patterns visible, not mechanical derivation. | The Positive Program
Kuczynski | Function 5: Ensuring Rigor / Avoiding Error What deduction was supposed to do: * Follow strict rules to avoid mistakes * Mechanical checking ensures correctnessWhat actually happens: * Rigor = careful attention to what follows * Pattern recognition of inferential structure, gaps, assumptionsProof-checking: * Automated: computers verify mechanically * Human: we see whether proof correct (pattern recognition of validity) * But even automated checking was set up by humans who recognized what patterns to check | The Positive Program
Kuczynski | Upshot: Rigor is disciplined pattern recognition, not mere rule-following. | The Positive Program
Kuczynski | Function 6: Transmitting Certainty / Preserving Knowledge What deduction was supposed to do: * Transfer certainty from premises to conclusion * Knowledge transmitted via valid inferenceWhat actually happens: * Knowledge transmission = recognizing that evidence supports conclusion * Seeing why conclusion is warranted given what you knowEvidence: * Can follow valid argument without understanding (just checking steps) * Real understanding = seeing WHY conclusion follows * This is pattern recognitionUpshot: Knowledge transmission requires recognizing evidential patterns. | The Positive Program
Kuczynski | Function 7: Clarifying Concepts / Making Definitions Explicit What deduction was supposed to do: * Analyze: Break complex concepts into simpler components * Define: Give necessary and sufficient conditions * Clarify: Remove ambiguity through formal precisionWhat actually happens: * Conceptual clarification is pattern recognition of meaning-relationships * Defining "bachelor" as "unmarried adult male": Recognize semantic equivalence, see these capture same pattern, perceive no counterexamples | The Positive Program
Kuczynski | This is pattern recognition, not deduction: * Definitions aren't derived; they're recognized as apt * Good definitions capture patterns of use * Testing requires seeing whether they fit casesDeeper point: * Concept formation (creating NEW concepts) clearly non-deductive: * "Natural selection" (Darwin) * "Unconscious" (Freud) * "Information" (Shannon) * Not defined from prior concepts—created by recognizing new patterns | The Positive Program
Kuczynski | Upshot: Pattern recognition recovers conceptual work: recognizing semantic relationships, recognizing meaning-patterns, creating new concepts. | The Positive Program
Kuczynski | What Deduction Actually Is Given all the above: DEDUCTION IS NOT A SEPARATE FACULTY OR METHOD. Deduction is a SPECIAL CASE of pattern recognition: * Patterns that are maximally rigid * Context-independent * Exceptionless * Formally codifiableCharacteristics of "deductive" patterns: * Necessity: Pattern admits no exceptions (modus ponens always valid) * Formality: Pattern depends only on logical form, not content * Explicitness: Pattern can be stated as explicit rule * Mechanizability: Pattern can be checked algorithmically (in principle) But even recognizing and applying these patterns requires pattern recognition. | The Positive Program
Kuczynski | The Recovery Thesis Pattern recognition recovers ALL functions attributed to deduction: 1. Truth-preservation (recognizing valid inference) 2. Consistency-checking (recognizing compatibility) 3. Consequence-drawing (recognizing what follows) 4. Justification (showing evidential patterns) 5. Rigor (disciplined pattern recognition) 6. Knowledge-transmission (recognizing support) 7. Conceptual clarity (recognizing meaning-patterns) In every case: * Pattern recognition does the actual cognitive work * Deduction describes a special class of patterns (necessary, formal, explicit) * But recognizing, applying, and verifying deduction requires pattern recognition | The Positive Program
Kuczynski | The Radical Claim Deduction doesn't do ANY work that pattern recognition doesn't do more fundamentally. Even in paradigm deductive domains: Mathematics: * Mathematical intuition is rational (not mystical) * Recognizing unprovable truths (Gödel sentence) * Understanding proofs requires seeing why steps follow * Pattern recognition, not just formal manipulationLogic: * Valid inference forms = codified patterns * But recognizing instances requires pattern recognition * Creating new logics requires seeing new patternsComputer science: * Algorithms = explicit recursive patterns * But designing algorithms requires recognizing problem-patterns * Debugging requires anomaly-detection | The Positive Program
Kuczynski | Why This Matters Deduction gets demoted: * Not essence of rationality * Not foundation of knowledge * Just one tool among many (useful in formal contexts)Pattern recognition gets promoted: * Actual mechanism of rational thought * Operates in deduction, induction, abduction, perception * Subsumes what deduction was supposed to doThe theoretical landscape shifts: OLD: * Deduction (primary, foundational) * Induction (secondary, problematic) * Everything else (non-rational)NEW: * Pattern recognition (primary, foundational, rational) * Deduction (special case: rigid formal patterns) * Induction (special case: regularity patterns) * Abduction (special case: explanatory patterns) * All are modes of pattern recognition | The Positive Program
Kuczynski | Do You Need Euclid? NO. What Euclid did: * Made geometric reasoning explicit (axioms, definitions, proofs) * Made it teachable (via formal steps) * Made it verifiable (check each step) * Made it transmissible (write down exactly)But the actual geometric insight: * Seeing that triangles with equal sides have equal angles * Recognizing that parallel lines never meet * Perceiving that angles in triangle sum to 180°This is pattern recognition. | The Positive Program
Kuczynski | So: Do you NEED Euclid? For what? * For geometric insight? No. Pattern recognition suffices. * For teaching efficiently? Yes. Formalization helps. * For rigorous proof? Yes. Explicit axiomatization enables verification. * For doing geometry at all? No. People did geometry before and without Euclid. | The Positive Program
Kuczynski | Position 1: UG Is Real But Pattern Recognition Is the Mechanism The view: * Chomsky RIGHT that there's innate linguistic structure * Chomsky RIGHT that syntax is recursive/generative * But mechanism isn't "applying rules"—it's recognizing and producing syntactic patterns * UG = innate pattern-recognition capacity specifically for syntactic structuresNot: "Mind contains rules like 'S → NP + VP'" But: "Mind structured to recognize NP-VP patterns, embedding patterns, agreement patterns" | The Positive Program
Kuczynski | The mechanism: * UG isn't rule-system that gets applied * UG is structured pattern-recognition capacity * Like: Visual system structured to recognize edges, shapes, faces * Not "rules for edge-detection" * But neural architecture that naturally responds to edge-patterns | The Positive Program
Kuczynski | UG alone gives you: * Syntactic structure * Infinite generativity * Well-formed expressions But: * No way to connect to world * No new concepts * No adaptation to novel situations | Rationality as Pattern Recognition
Kuczynski | X alone gives you: * Semantic grounding * Reference to world * Concept formation But: * No systematic structure * No infinite expressiveness * Limited to finite learned associations | Rationality as Pattern Recognition
Kuczynski | Rules as Patterns: Refuting Wittgenstein The Reconciliation: Rule-Application IS Pattern Instantiation KEY INSIGHT: When the mind applies "S → NP + VP", it's not: * Mechanically executing an algorithm * Consulting an internal rulebook * Following a procedure blindly | Rationality as Pattern Recognition
Kuczynski | Wittgenstein's Rule-Following Paradox THE REGRESS ARGUMENT: To follow rule R, you need to interpret R Interpreting R requires following interpretation-rule R' Following R' requires interpreting R' This requires rule R'' Infinite regress... | Rationality as Pattern Recognition
Kuczynski | Wittgenstein's conclusion: Rule-following can't be intellectualist (based on interpretation/understanding). It must be: "Just doing it"—blind conformity to training, no principled basis except agreement with community practice. | Rationality as Pattern Recognition
Kuczynski | The Refutation via Pattern Recognition THE KEY MOVE: Rule-following doesn't require interpretation when the rule describes a pattern you can directly recognize. | Rationality as Pattern Recognition
Kuczynski | Pattern recognition is BASIC—not mediated by further rules * Therefore: no regress | Rationality as Pattern Recognition
Kuczynski | Wittgenstein's puzzle: * Rule: "Add 2" * How do you know to continue: 1000, 1002, 1004... (not 1000, 1004, 1008...)? | Rationality as Pattern Recognition
Kuczynski | Pattern recognition answer: * You directly recognize the add-2 pattern * The pattern is: each number is 2 more than previous | Rationality as Pattern Recognition
Kuczynski | Why Pattern Recognition Blocks the Regress The regress requires: * Each step mediated by interpretation * Interpretation requires another rule * That rule requires interpretation * Etc. | Rationality as Pattern Recognition
Kuczynski | Pattern recognition breaks this: * Recognition is DIRECT (not mediated by interpretation) * No "rule for recognizing patterns" needed * Pattern recognition is GROUND FLOOR (can't regress further) | Rationality as Pattern Recognition
Kuczynski | Wittgenstein assumes: * Following rule R = interpreting R, then acting on interpretation * Interpretation = applying another rule (R') * Hence regress | Rationality as Pattern Recognition
Kuczynski | His answer: * Normativity = community agreement * "Correct" = conforms to what community does * No objective standard beyond social practice | Rationality as Pattern Recognition
Kuczynski | Pattern recognition answer: * Normativity comes from the pattern itself * Correct = actually instantiates the pattern * Incorrect = produces different pattern * Can be determined by checking instance against pattern | Rationality as Pattern Recognition
Kuczynski | The pattern is REAL—recognizing/instantiating it correctly matters objectively. | Rationality as Pattern Recognition
Kuczynski | Linguists describe those patterns as RULES. But the patterns are real —they're what the mind recognizes and instantiates. | Rationality as Pattern Recognition
Kuczynski | OLD VIEW: Rationality = Deduction * Following explicit logical rules * Step-by-step derivation * Algorithmic, recursive * Paradigm: Mathematical proof, formal logic | Rationality as Pattern Recognition
Kuczynski | NEW VIEW: Rationality = Pattern Recognition * Seeing structures, similarities, relationships * Holistic judgment * Non-algorithmic but systematic * Paradigm: Scientific discovery, expert diagnosis, skilled perception | Rationality as Pattern Recognition
Kuczynski | * Pattern recognition mediates BOTH The General Point Pattern recognition is PRIMARY. Formalization (Euclid, Chomsky, Aristotle, Frege) is SECONDARY. | Rationality as Pattern Recognition
Kuczynski | Kant and Hume on Induction, Causation, and the Methodology of the Human Sciences This paper is divided into three parts. | Introduction
Kuczynski | In the first, we discuss the cogency of Kant’s arguments concerning causation and induction. | Introduction
Kuczynski | Those arguments are spurious, we will find, because they all rest on the erroneous assumption that all non-empirical truths are trivial. | Introduction
Kuczynski | At the same time, so we will also find, each of those arguments corresponds to a cogent argument that does not involve that assumption. | Introduction
Kuczynski | In the second part, we discuss the viability of Hume’s analyses of causation and induction. | Introduction
Kuczynski | It is argued that those analyses are fallacious, and an attempt is made to identify the relevant fallacies. | Introduction
Kuczynski | In the third part, we draw some of the epistemological consequences of our response to Hume. | Introduction
Kuczynski | Kant held that some propositions are both synthetic and also a posteriori. (We will soon define these terms.) | Introduction
Kuczynski | This contention is the heart of the system of thought set forth in the Critique of Pure Reason. | Introduction
Kuczynski | I will now argue that this contention of Kant’s is false and that his argument for it is spurious. | Introduction
Kuczynski | But I will also argue that Kant’s false contention can readily be turned into a correct one and that his spurious argument can readily be turned into a cogent one. | Introduction
Kuczynski | First, I will argue that the premises of Kant’s argument turn out to entail the opposite of the thesis that there are synthetic a priori truths. | Introduction
Kuczynski | Those premises entail that there are virtually no a priori truths, let alone synthetic a priori truths. | Introduction
Kuczynski | Kant fails to distinguish the two quite distinct roles that mental images can have; and his views concerning the synthetic a priori are a reflection of that. | The Cogency of Kant’s Transcendental Arguments
Kuczynski | Kant’s astonishing claims about causality, personal identity, and rationality turn out to be correct and founded on cogent arguments. | The Cogency of Kant’s Transcendental Arguments
Kuczynski | Let us begin with some points about terminology. The word “concept” has two meanings – one logical, one psychological. | The Cogency of Kant’s Transcendental Arguments
Kuczynski | In this paper, I will typically use the word “concept” in its non-psychological sense; and when I describe a proposition as “conceptually necessary”, I mean that it holds entirely in virtue of facts about concepts in the non-psychological sense of the word. | The Cogency of Kant’s Transcendental Arguments
Kuczynski | For Kant, a priority is a property of our knowledge of propositions. | The Cogency of Kant’s Transcendental Arguments
Kuczynski | For Kant, a proposition is “a posteriori” if it is not a priori. | The Cogency of Kant’s Transcendental Arguments
Kuczynski | A sentence is “analytic” exactly if its semantics guarantees its truth. | The Cogency of Kant’s Transcendental Arguments
Kuczynski | Kant uses the term “analytic” to describe propositions – not sentences, and not our knowledge of anything. | The Cogency of Kant’s Transcendental Arguments
Kuczynski | A proposition is synthetic if its truth is not guaranteed by the structures of the concepts composing it. | The Cogency of Kant’s Transcendental Arguments
Kuczynski | For Kant, a+b is larger than a is a paradigm case of an “analytic” proposition, and John and Mary like to play tennis is a paradigm case of a proposition that Kant would describe as “synthetic.” | The Cogency of Kant’s Transcendental Arguments
Kuczynski | We may conclude that, for Kant, a proposition is “analytic” exactly if it is conceptually true – exactly if it holds entirely in virtue of the structures of the concepts composing it. | The Cogency of Kant’s Transcendental Arguments
Kuczynski | According to Kant, the propositions 7+5=12 and a straight line is the shortest distance between two points are synthetic a priori truths. | The Cogency of Kant’s Transcendental Arguments
Kuczynski | Kant is thus guilty not only of error, but of inconsistency with his own premises, in believing that 7+5=12 is synthetic. | The Cogency of Kant’s Transcendental Arguments
Kuczynski | Given that you don’t think =12 when you think 7+5, it seems to follow that your concept (i.e. your grasp) of 7+5 doesn’t contain or include your concept =12. | The Cogency of Kant’s Transcendental Arguments
Kuczynski | Arithmetical truths are not inductive generalizations that we could, in principle, find to be false. | The Cogency of Kant’s Transcendental Arguments
Kuczynski | How do you know that 7+5=12? Not through conceptual analysis. Not through the senses. You learn it through an act of the imagination. | The Cogency of Kant’s Transcendental Arguments
Kuczynski | By “intuition” Kant means an act of imaging: a kind of para-perception. | The Cogency of Kant’s Transcendental Arguments
Kuczynski | They tell us not that 7+5 really equals 12, but only that we are psychologically compelled to sense-perceive the world as being such that, in it, 7+5=12. | The Cogency of Kant’s Transcendental Arguments
Kuczynski | So intuition doesn’t really tell us anything about the external world. | The Cogency of Kant’s Transcendental Arguments
Kuczynski | The truth is that, on Kant’s account, even this knowledge turns to be a posteriori. | The Cogency of Kant’s Transcendental Arguments
Kuczynski | For Kant, you learn that you can’t represent parallel lines as intersecting by noting that you cannot imagine it; you observe your own mental imagery. | Kant’s Views on Synthetic A Priori Propositions
Kuczynski | When Kant says that “in the phenomenal world” parallels don’t meet, he is just saying that we cannot visually represent a world where parallels don’t meet. | Kant’s Views on Synthetic A Priori Propositions
Kuczynski | Diagrams serve as vehicles for a priori reasoning. | Kant’s Views on Synthetic A Priori Propositions
Kuczynski | This knowledge turns out to be quite a posteriori. | Kant’s Views on Synthetic A Priori Propositions
Kuczynski | One is guilty of incoherence if one thinks that (*) is false. | Kant’s Views on Synthetic A Priori Propositions
Kuczynski | Because we have certain cognitive machinery -- certain “forms of thought” -- we are compelled to see the world in a certain way. | Kant’s Views on Synthetic A Priori Propositions
Kuczynski | If you believe (d), you are guilty of logical incoherence: the proposition you believe entails a contradiction. | Kant’s Views on Synthetic A Priori Propositions
Kuczynski | Logical coherence is a relation among concepts. Psychological coherence is a relation among our grasp of concepts. | Kant’s Views on Synthetic A Priori Propositions
Kuczynski | What Kant refers to as ‘synthetic a priori’ truths are nothing other than what we are describing as hard analytic truths. | Kant’s Views on Synthetic A Priori Propositions
Kuczynski | Given only the fact that there are beings who have the ability to raise questions about anything, let alone about the necessary preconditions for experience, it follows that causal relations do not consist in mere sequences of events, but in internal relations of some kind. | Kant’s Views on Synthetic A Priori Propositions
Kuczynski | Kant himself proposed solutions. But those solutions falsely presuppose that the world known to us and studied by science is a creation of our own mental faculties. | Critique of Kant’s Psychological Necessity
Kuczynski | The justification for this inference would have to be empirical, not strictly logical. | Critique of Kant’s Psychological Necessity
Kuczynski | But there can be no such assurance unless there is an assurance that the future resembles the past. We are evidently caught in an unbreakable, vicious circle. | Critique of Kant’s Psychological Necessity
Kuczynski | There are no necessary, and therefore no causal, relations between distinct events, or at least none we could have any legitimate reason to believe in. | Critique of Kant’s Psychological Necessity
Kuczynski | But what we perceive does not give us any basis for perceiving that things altogether different from events are likely to accompany events. | Critique of Kant’s Psychological Necessity
Kuczynski | Given this, we might say that a causal connection consists in the operation of some force that demands the existence of the one event, given the existence of the other. But this is not a tenable proposal. | Critique of Kant’s Psychological Necessity
Kuczynski | If by “force” is meant some constituent of the spatio-temporal world, then there can be no legitimate reason to believe in its existence, as the following argument shows. | Critique of Kant’s Psychological Necessity
Kuczynski | To take a trivial example, this would be the case if we described A as the cause of B or if we described B as one of the successors of A. | Critique of Kant’s Psychological Necessity
Kuczynski | Focus on an arbitrary one of these mass-energy displacements. To give a complete description of this displacement, we must obviously describe what happens during some stretch of time, however short that stretch may be. | Critique of Kant’s Psychological Necessity
Kuczynski | This would have two consequences. First it would mean that any description of any given event would include a description of subsequent events. | Critique of Kant’s Psychological Necessity
Kuczynski | It might seem to meaningful to say that x is a square at instant t. After all, squareness is (it seems) a three-, not a four-dimensional property. | Critique of Kant’s Psychological Necessity
Kuczynski | But this involves a confusion of the having of four-dimensional properties with the gaining and losing of such properties. | Critique of Kant’s Psychological Necessity
Kuczynski | The belief that space and time are composed of points strikes me as a projection into the physical world of this fact about language. | Critique of Kant’s Psychological Necessity
Kuczynski | Hume’s analysis of the concept of “force” is beyond reproach. But that analysis is moot since, contrary to what Hume held, we don’t need forces to connect cause and effect. | Critique of Kant’s Psychological Necessity
Kuczynski | Finally, those descriptions never mention the fact that the data that motivates (and, so I will argue, justifies) our inductive beliefs is never, in the final analysis, propositional. | Critique of Kant’s Psychological Necessity
Kuczynski | If there is no possible causal connection between E and E*, then it makes little sense to say that they have any spatial or temporal position with respect to each other. | Critique of Kant’s Psychological Necessity
Kuczynski | It seems, then, that (O) is less like all and only rhenates are chordates than it is like all and only triangles are regions bounded by three coplanar lines such that any two of them, but not all three, intersect. | Critique of Kant’s Psychological Necessity
Kuczynski | Hume famously argued that there are no strictly logical grounds for the view that every event must have a cause – that there are no ex nihilo events. | Critique of Kant’s Psychological Necessity
Kuczynski | The spatio-temporal manifold would comprise ex nihilo (uncaused) events only if it comprised events that were not co-located with other events – only if it comprised events that it didn’t comprise. | Critique of Kant’s Psychological Necessity
Kuczynski | Hume’s conception of causality involves a falsely “digital” conception of spatiotemporal reality. | Hume’s Analysis of Induction and Causality
Kuczynski | The spatio-temporal world is thus, almost by definition, completely permutation-friendly. | Hume’s Analysis of Induction and Causality
Kuczynski | The permutation-friendliness definitive of spatio-temporal location, as Hume understands it, is identical with the absence of causal connections between distinct states of affairs. | Hume’s Analysis of Induction and Causality
Kuczynski | Spatio-temporal continuity is a form of persistence. Consequently, continuity is a causal phenomenon. | Hume’s Analysis of Induction and Causality
Kuczynski | Hume’s “demonstration” that there are no causal relations (or that such relations reduce to regularities) is thus an artifact of his assumption that space and time have a structure comparable to that of an array of digits. | Hume’s Analysis of Induction and Causality
Kuczynski | Strictly speaking, it is not our knowledge of (a)-(a4), or of any other propositions, that motivates and justifies our inductions: what does so is the sensory data that those propositions so schematically model. | Hume’s Analysis of Induction and Causality
Kuczynski | What is reported by such a proposition is not the content of the perception itself, but rather a judgment about the content of that perception. | Hume’s Analysis of Induction and Causality
Kuczynski | The logical basis for induction is precisely what is lost in the process of digitization involved in embodying one’s knowledge in (a)-(a4). | Hume’s Analysis of Induction and Causality
Kuczynski | When you see a white swan, what your perception gives you is a continuous state of affairs. | Hume’s Analysis of Induction and Causality
Kuczynski | There is thus a sense in which (a)-(a4) are not the data that motivate (or legitimate) belief in (b). | Hume’s Analysis of Induction and Causality
Kuczynski | What is given to you in such representations is never a discrete state but, at most, a relatively discrete state of affairs. | Hume’s Analysis of Induction and Causality
Kuczynski | Hume holds that counter-inductive beliefs like (A) involve unusual, but otherwise acceptable, permutations of objects and properties. | Hume’s Analysis of Induction and Causality
Kuczynski | Hume rightly said that (A) and (C) are compatible propositions. | Hume’s Analysis of Induction and Causality
Kuczynski | It is tempting to think that (A) is logically incompatible with the existence of known facts, like that described by: (C) no stone known to anyone has ever sprouted wings. | Hume’s Analysis of Induction and Causality
Kuczynski | Hume’s conception of induction involves a similar fallacy. | Hume’s Analysis of Induction and Causality
Kuczynski | our answers typically don’t go much beyond what those associations give us. | Revisiting the Analogue-Digital Distinction
Kuczynski | Given that association is an affect-driven process, it is to be expected that it will refer us to emotionally pregnant breakdowns in the uptake of perceptual information, and not to the smooth flow of information mediating between such cataclysms. | Revisiting the Analogue-Digital Distinction
Kuczynski | Hume’s views on induction are thus artifacts of his own methods. | Revisiting the Analogue-Digital Distinction
Kuczynski | Our experience gives us smooth curves. Our sentential representations of our experience (“past fires have been hot”) give us isolated points that are compatible with infinitely many different curves. | Revisiting the Analogue-Digital Distinction
Kuczynski | the viability of induction is bound up with the existence of causal mechanisms linking states of affairs. | Revisiting the Analogue-Digital Distinction
Kuczynski | Our acceptance of Hume’s analyses of causation and induction, despite their obvious discordance with everything we believe, is a special case of that acquiescence and is thus an expression of that same wish. | Revisiting the Analogue-Digital Distinction
Kuczynski | Correct inductions are analogue-logically valid inferences from the data embodied in our sense-perceptions. | Revisiting the Analogue-Digital Distinction
Kuczynski | Hume’s counter-intuitive analysis of causation presupposes that spatio-temporal relations are non-causal, and thus that space and time are empty vessels whose properties are wholly invariant with respect to those of the events populating them. | Revisiting the Analogue-Digital Distinction
Kuczynski | Once it is acknowledged that temporal co-locatedness must be understood in causal terms, we have no trouble finding a purely logical foundation for our inductive practices. | Revisiting the Analogue-Digital Distinction
Kuczynski | a belief that is very likely to be correct is not, in virtue of that fact, knowledge. | Logical Basis of Induction
Kuczynski | Our knowledge of the external world is not merely probable. Our beliefs concerning the external world analogue-logically valid inferences from the data of experience. | Logical Basis of Induction
Kuczynski | space and time are themselves causal notions. | Logical Basis of Induction
Kuczynski | For E to come before E* is for E is to be a cause of E*. For those events to be simultaneous is for neither to be a cause of the other. | Logical Basis of Induction
Kuczynski | if E1 has no causal relation to E2, then (unless they are simultaneous) they are ipso facto not parts of the same event. | Logical Basis of Induction
Kuczynski | an instant in time is the class of all events such that there is no causal connection between any two of them. | Logical Basis of Induction
Kuczynski | Conceptual, i.e. analytic connections, betoken an identity, or overlap, of content. | Logical Basis of Induction
Kuczynski | Whenever we talk about “the same place”, we must identify the relevant framework. | Logical Basis of Induction
Kuczynski | Evolution becomes an all purpose explanatory stop-gap, much as God used to be. | Logical Basis of Induction
Kuczynski | in the final analysis, Chomsky and Kant say that, give or take a few nuances, we are born knowing everything we will ever know. | Logical Basis of Induction
Kuczynski | Humean empiricism is inconsistent with this; and so, despite first appearances, is Chomskyan-Kantian nativism. | Consequences for Scientific Explanation
Kuczynski | Their nativism is not rationalism; it is species-empiricism, as opposed to individual-empiricism; and, notwithstanding the appearance of scientific rigor associated with the hallowed term “evolution”, their position is as incapable as Hume’s in allowing us to have any theoretical or inductive knowledge. | Consequences for Scientific Explanation
Kuczynski | Hume makes it impossible to acquire any knowledge that transcends the deliverances of one’s senses at a given moment. | Consequences for Scientific Explanation
Kuczynski | Since we can’t learn anything, we must be born knowing everything that we ever come to know. | Consequences for Scientific Explanation
Kuczynski | But there is another – in this context, much more important – kind of innate knowledge. | Consequences for Scientific Explanation
Kuczynski | The cognitive machinery through which we acquire empirical knowledge would seem to be embody knowledge of conceptual truths. | Consequences for Scientific Explanation
Kuczynski | Some kind of knowledge is needed to see relations of bearing among empirical data and, thus, to synthesize such data into theories (and even – I believe, following Kant and David Marr – into perceptions). | Consequences for Scientific Explanation
Kuczynski | The kind of knowledge that enables one to delineate the logical consequences of the information at one’s disposal is entirely analytic and non-empirical. | Consequences for Scientific Explanation
Kuczynski | It therefore isn’t necessary to agree with the implausible view of Kant and Chomsky that we innately know Newtonian mechanics, and need only a modicum of sense-experience to activate this latent knowledge. | Consequences for Scientific Explanation
Kuczynski | Fortunately, it isn’t necessary to posit so much innate empirical knowledge to explain our success at forming theories. | Consequences for Scientific Explanation
Kuczynski | There are obviously some cases where the cause of a belief is distinct from its justification. | Consequences for Scientific Explanation
Kuczynski | On Hume’s view, nothing causes anything. Instead of causation, there are only regularities. | Consequences for Scientific Explanation
Kuczynski | To explain an event is thus to show only that it is an instance of some regularity. | Consequences for Scientific Explanation
Kuczynski | If we accept Hume’s views on induction, then this is exactly what we are forced to say. | Consequences for Scientific Explanation
Kuczynski | Psychological explanation, it is said, consists in entering into the subjectivity of the person or persons studied. | Consequences for Scientific Explanation
Kuczynski | But the word “explanation” is not ambiguous – it is not like the word “bank”. | Consequences for Scientific Explanation
Kuczynski | In both cases, you are showing why it makes sense that some event occurred. | Consequences for Scientific Explanation
Kuczynski | Explanation always consists in showing a logical and principled relation between initial and terminal conditions. | Consequences for Scientific Explanation
Kuczynski | Exceptionless though they are, these regularities are obviously explanatorily irrelevant. | Consequences for Scientific Explanation
Kuczynski | As Clarence Lewis stressed in Mind and the World Order, for something to be copper (or water, or a table, or a bar of soap…) specifically is for it to have the right inductive and explanatory properties. | Consequences for Scientific Explanation
Kuczynski | One lesson of this discussion is that, contrary to what Hume alleges, explanation is not a purely statistical concept, since the terms in which the relevant statistics are articulated will inevitably be explanatorily committed. | Skepticism and the External World
Kuczynski | The physical world is characterized by many regularities (leaving aside the explanatorily sterile ones just discussed). But presumably these regularities express, and do not constitute, the existence of principled relationships. | Skepticism and the External World
Kuczynski | Our inhibitions to doing so vanish once we acknowledge the two main points of this paper, namely: propositions are merely models, and bad ones at that, of the analogue data given in sense-perception; and, second, space and time are not causally innocuous concepts. | Skepticism and the External World
Kuczynski | Revolutionary science consists in producing a new “paradigm”: a new framework within which research is to be conducted. A paradigm is not exactly a theory; it would be better described as a kind of meta-theory – a general outlook within which the construction of theories takes place. | Skepticism and the External World
Kuczynski | In Kuhn’s view, since facts are all internal to paradigms, empirical facts cannot warrant the acceptance or rejection of a given paradigm. Paradigms are to be accepted or rejected entirely on non-epistemic grounds. | Skepticism and the External World
Kuczynski | If Kuhn is right, then once a paradigm is accepted, all the important issues have been decided in advance. So once a paradigm is accepted, scientific research consists in delineating the consequences of what is, from the viewpoint of someone operating within that paradigm, some trans-empirical and thus a priori set of categories. | Skepticism and the External World
Kuczynski | The debate between Kuhn and the positivists parallels the debate regarding psychological explanation between Dilthey and Hempel. On the one hand, we have a sterile and implausible descriptivist conception of scientific explanation: theories are statistical generalizations; to explain an event is to subsume it under such a generalization. | Skepticism and the External World
Kuczynski | Inductive explanation consists in finding principled relationships among data. The relevant logical principles are embodied in analogue-content, and thus resist articulation within the more familiar digital-propositional logic on which we focus in philosophical discourse. | Skepticism and the External World
Kuczynski | Hume’s statistical conception of explanation is false; and so, therefore, is his solution to the mind-body problem. | Skepticism and the External World
Kuczynski | Whether Freud’s theories are to be accepted is a strictly empirical question that is to be resolved wholly on the basis of the accuracy of the specific contents of those theories. | Skepticism and the External World
Kuczynski | Once it is agreed that we came into existence by a process of evolution, it becomes hard to maintain that we were created by an almighty God. | Conception and Causation Overview
Kuczynski | There is at least one other reason why the theory of evolution upsets us. It makes it very hard to believe in an afterlife. | Conception and Causation Overview
Kuczynski | If four pairs of shoes all have the same logo on them, they were probably made by the same company. | Conception and Causation Overview
Kuczynski | We don’t believe that amoebas or lizards or baboons go to heaven. | Conception and Causation Overview
Kuczynski | Every human being, when confronted with two very similar things, instinctively looks for a common cause. | Conception and Causation Overview
Kuczynski | In fact, it is painfully obvious that we are related to such creatures. | Conception and Causation Overview
Kuczynski | Of course, people didn’t know about evolution at a conscious level. They repressed this knowledge; they knew it only unconsciously. | Conception and Causation Overview
Kuczynski | But when we look at the anatomical basis of love, this sine qua non of the good life - -this highest actualization of the human spirit – takes on quite a different countenance. | Conception and Causation Overview
Kuczynski | So given that we evolved from such creatures, or from creatures of their ilk, it seems very arbitrary to maintain that we go to heaven when we die. | Conception and Causation Overview

Kuczynski | though sex, at least in certain contexts, is believed, supposedly, to be the highest manifestation of the human condition, it is also, evidently believed to be the lowest. | Conception and Causation Overview

Kuczynski | So grasping a norm doesn’t consist in being in possession of some kind of internal icon corresponding to that norm. | The Nature of Emotions and Ethics

Kuczynski | To understand a sentence token is to know what meaning it bears; and what meaning it bears is, ultimately, determined by what is meant by it or, at any rate, by what is meant – by what thought is intended to be conveyed - by the primitive expressions occurring in it. | Critique of Kant's Views

Kuczynski | We’ve just encountered yet another problem with that view. It is clear that triangles have three sides and squares have four sides and Jim’s pool has three sides all have something in common, corresponding to the fact that side is a constituent of all of them. | Cognitive Realism and Language
Kuczynski | But the concept side is not in any clear sense a constituent of a set of worlds. | Cognitive Realism and Language
Kuczynski | The fact that a grasp of a single concept or property is used in grasping different propositions suggests that propositions are built up out of concepts or properties. | Cognitive Realism and Language
Kuczynski | So we are vindicated, I think, in holding that propositions – if they exist – just are structures consisting of properties (concepts). | Cognitive Realism and Language

Kuczynski | For belief in such entities is very much not in the spirit of antipropositionalism. | Cognitive Realism and Language
Kuczynski | To sum up, the anti-propositionalist is barred from saying that facts about platonic entities make (T) be true. | Cognitive Realism and Language
Kuczynski | A proposition is a set of properties, and a proposition is true if the relevant properties are instantiated. | Wittgenstein's Influence
Kuczynski | The ordered triple <Plato, the relation of punching, Socrates> isn’t true or false. | Wittgenstein's Influence
Kuczynski | A sentence is not just a heap of referring terms. A corresponding point holds about propositions. | Wittgenstein's Influence
Kuczynski | A proposition is a set of objects and properties that are unified in some way. | Wittgenstein's Influence
Kuczynski | The difference between a proposition and a non-propositional heap is somehow coded in these grammatical articulations. | Wittgenstein's Influence
Kuczynski | The proposition Socrates punched Plato has a unique decomposition into minimal parts. | Wittgenstein's Influence
Kuczynski | The expression “punches” corresponds to something with holes in it; these holes enable Smith and Jones to link up with that thing in the appropriate way. | Wittgenstein's Influence
Kuczynski | Frege’s “solution” is really just a way of labeling the problem, as Davidson [380] said. | Wittgenstein's Influence
Kuczynski | Let me start out by stating, in extremely rough outline, the position I will defend. | Wittgenstein's Influence
Kuczynski | I will argue that propositions are unordered sets of properties. | Wittgenstein's Influence
Kuczynski | These points help us deal with another important fact. | Wittgenstein's Influence
Kuczynski | The sentence “Smith punches Jones” encodes an arrangement of objects and properties. | Wittgenstein's Influence
Kuczynski | In that case, the proposition that Smith punches Jones consists of Smith, Jones, and the relation of punching being arranged in a certain way. | Wittgenstein's Influence
Kuczynski | Are grammatical articulations referring terms with the special property that the things to which they refer confer unity on what would otherwise an unordered heap? | Wittgenstein's Influence

Kuczynski | It is a matter of philosophical and scientific debate whether, given an event E happening at t, anything other than the entire state of the universe just prior to t can be the complete cause of E. | Analytic Philosophy as Logical Analysis
Kuczynski | In a word, “the cause” of an event is an INUS-condition for that event. | Analytic Philosophy as Logical Analysis
Kuczynski | Thus, Smith’s being drunk was an insufficient but non-redundant part of an unnecessary but sufficient condition for his crashing. | Analytic Philosophy as Logical Analysis
Kuczynski | J.L. Mackie (1917–1981) made it clear what is meant by “the cause of E,” as this expression is typically used. | Analytic Philosophy as Logical Analysis

Kuczynski | Frank Jackson (1943-) and Philip Pettit (1945–) have shown this to be false. | Analytic Philosophy as Logical Analysis

Kuczynski | There is one respect in which actual psychopaths resemble M that deserves special mention: like M, they have an extremely flexible relationship to the truth. | Brentano and Meinong: Non-Analytic Methods
Kuczynski | So the psychopath doesn't have to lie. For the psychopath, some beliefs are more useful than others; so, for him, there is operational truth and falsehood. | Brentano and Meinong: Non-Analytic Methods
Kuczynski | The highly conditional nature of their commitment to the truth forces them to make decisions on the basis of an unduly constricted body of knowledge, the results being that they are likely to commit horrible gaffes that alienate them from institutions within which one has to operate to succeed. | Brentano and Meinong: Non-Analytic Methods
Kuczynski | But psychopaths are like a house of cards. | Brentano and Meinong: Non-Analytic Methods

Kuczynski | The psychopath has minds, but they don’t have selves. | Brentano and Meinong: Non-Analytic Methods
